But you? What were your thoughts of me the next day, and the next day after that, whilst I waited in suspense which turned to torture for a word from you that would recall that hour? What were your thoughts? Where were you? I was waiting for you at the Lascelles as you had promised you would come over from Oxford the very next day. You did not come﻿—not for days﻿—weeks﻿—”
“Rosemary!”
“Not for days﻿—weeks﻿—” she insisted, “and I waited for a sign﻿—a letter﻿—”
“Rosemary, at the time you understood!”
“I only understood,” she retorted with cold irony, “that you blamed yourself for having engaged my young affections﻿—that you had your way to make in the world before you could think of asking a girl to share your poverty﻿—and so on﻿—and so on﻿—every time we met﻿—and in every letter you wrote﻿—whilst I﻿—
“Whilst you did not understand, Peter,” she went on more calmly. “Whilst you spoke of the future, of winning fame and fortune﻿—”
“For you, Rosemary!” he cried involuntarily, and buried his head in his hands. “I was only thinking of you﻿—”
“You were not thinking of me, Peter, or you would have known that there was no poverty or toil I would not gladly have shared with the man I loved.”
“Yes! poverty﻿—toil﻿—on an equal footing. Rosemary; but you were rich, famous: already you had the world at your feet﻿—”
“And you did not care for me enough, Peter,” she said with a note of fatality in her voice, “to accept wealth, comfort, help in your career from me﻿—
“Peter Blakeney the cricketer,” he declaimed with biting sarcasm; “don’t you know, he is the husband of Rosemary Fowkes now. What a glorious career for a man, eh, to be the husband of a world-famous wife?”

===Chinese===

“一个人出世时，尘世一切慈悲的精灵都来照临，把礼物撒满他的膝头，他还谈什么雄心？有雄心的是我们这些穷鬼——而且眼睁睁看着它落空。”
“你的雄心，你摆在爱情之上、摆在一切之上，”罗斯玛丽插进来，直直地望着他的眼睛。“你谈爱情，彼得，”她接着说，忽然激烈起来，尖利的话终于像从不堪重负的心底倾泻出来。“你懂什么是爱情？你说得对，那天夜里我确实躺在你的怀里，用我的整个生命爱着你，我的魂寻找你的魂，在那个难忘的吻里找到了它。天哪！我本来能多么爱你啊，彼得！可你呢？第二天，第三天，你心里把我怎么想？那时我在悬望中等你一句话，悬望熬成了煎熬，只求一句话来召回那个时辰。你想的是什么？你在哪儿？我在拉塞尔斯家等你，因为你许诺过第二天就从牛津过来。你没有来——多少天——多少个礼拜——”
“罗斯玛丽！”
“多少天——多少个礼拜——”她一口咬定，“我等一个信号——一封信——”
“罗斯玛丽，你当时是明白的！”
“我只明白，”她带着冷冷的讥诮回敬，“你懊悔招惹了我少女的感情——明白你要先在世上闯出路来，才谈得上娶一个姑娘同你共贫贱——如此等等——如此等等——每次我们见面——你每封信里都这么写——而我——”
“而你不明白，彼得，”她接着说，平静了些。“你一味谈将来，谈赢取名声和财富——”
“为了你，罗斯玛丽！”他不由自主地喊道，把头埋进双手里。“我心里只有你——”
“你心里并没有我，彼得，不然你就会知道，只要跟着我爱的男人，世上没有哪种贫穷、哪种操劳我不甘之如饴。”
“是啊！贫穷——操劳——在平等的地位上，罗斯玛丽。可你有钱，有名：世界已经拜倒在你脚下——”
“你就是爱我不够，彼得，”她说，声音里带着宿命的调子，“不肯从我这里接受财富、安逸、对你前程的扶助——”
“‘板球手彼得·布莱克尼’，”他挖苦地朗诵道，“如今他是什么人？罗斯玛丽·福克斯的丈夫。男人有这样光辉的前程，呃？给一位名满天下的妻子当丈夫。”

===Original===

“It would only have been for a time,” she protested.
“A time during which youth would have flown away on the wings of life, taking with it honour, manhood, dignity﻿—”
“And love?”
“Perhaps.”
There was silence between them after that. The last word had been spoken, the immutable word of Fate. Peter still sat with his head buried in his hands, his elbows resting on his knees﻿—a hunched up figure weighed down by the heavy hand of an inexorable past.
Rosemary looked down at the bent head, and there, in the shadow, where no one could see save the immortal recorder of sorrows and of tears, a look of great tenderness and of pity crept into her haunting eyes. It was only for a moment. With a great effort of will she shook herself free from the spell that for a while had held possession of her soul. With a deliberate gesture she drew back the curtain, so that her face and figure became all at once flooded with light, she looked down upon the kaleidoscopic picture below: the dusky orchestra had once more begun to belch forth hideous sounds, and hellish screams, the puppets on the dancing floor began one by one to resume their gyrations. Several among the crowd, looking up, saw and recognised Rosemary: she smiled and nodded to them, waved her fan in recognition. She was Rosemary Fowkes once more, the most talked-of woman in England, the fiancée of Jasper Tarkington, queen of her set, admired, adulated, the comet of the past two seasons.
“There’s that tiresome George Orange,” she said in her coldest, most matter-of-fact tone. “He is making desperate and ludicrous signs. I strongly suspect him of making straight for this box. Shall we try to give him the slip?”
Her quiet voice seemed to act like an anodyne on Peter’s jangled nerves. He straightened out his tall figure, quietly pulled the chairs away, to enable her to pass. She, too, rose and prepared to go. It seemed difficult not to say another word, or to look him once more straight in the eyes; and yet to speak words now, after what had just passed between them, seemed more difficult than anything. His hand was on the door handle. The other side of the door people were moving up and down, talking and laughing. Another second or two and she would pass out of his sight﻿—pass out of his life more effectually even than she had done when she gave her word to Jasper Tarkington. Another second.

===Chinese===

“那也只会是一时的事。”她抗议道。
“这一时里，青春早已插上生命的翅膀飞走，连同荣誉、丈夫气、尊严一起带走——”
“还有爱情呢？”
“也许吧。”
此后两人之间是一片沉默。最后的话已经说出了，命运那不可更改的话。彼得依旧坐着，头埋在手里，肘支在膝上——一个蜷缩的身影，被无可挽回的过去那只沉重的手压弯了。
罗斯玛丽低头望着那颗低垂的头。就在这阴影里，在这除却那记录悲伤与眼泪的不朽史官谁也看不见的地方，一缕巨大的温柔与怜惜悄悄爬进她那双摄人心魄的眼睛。这只是一瞬。凭着极大的意志力，她把自己从一度攫住她灵魂的符咒里挣脱出来。她从容地拉开帘子，让灯光一下子淹没了她的脸和身影；她俯视楼下那万花筒般的图景：昏暗的乐池又开始喷出可怕的声响，地狱般的尖啸，舞池里的木偶们一个个重新转起圈来。人群里有几个抬头看见了她、认出了她：她朝他们微笑、颔首，挥着扇子致意。她又成了罗斯玛丽·福克斯，全英格兰最炙手可热的女人，贾斯珀·塔金顿的未婚妻，她那个圈子的王后，人人仰慕、人人逢迎，过去两个社交季里最耀眼的彗星。
“那个讨厌的乔治·奥兰治来了，”她用最冷、最就事论事的声调说。“他在拼命打手势，打得可笑。我十分怀疑他就要直奔这个包厢来。咱们溜开避他好不好？”
她平静的声音像一剂止痛药，抚慰了彼得烦乱的神经。他直起颀长的身子，悄悄把椅子挪开，好让她过去。她也站起身准备走。似乎不说一句话、不再直直地看他一眼就走出去，未免太难；而经过了方才那一切之后再说话，又比什么都难。他的手已经搭在门把上。门外人影往来，笑语喧阗。再过一两秒，她就要走出他的视线——比她把终身许给贾斯珀·塔金顿那天更彻底地走出他的生命。再过一秒。

===Original===

But just then she raised her eyes, and they met his.
“Rosemary!” he said.
She shook her head and smiled gently, ironically perhaps, indulgently also as on a rebuked child.
“I had better go now, Peter,” she said quietly. “I feel sure George Orange is on his way to drag me to his wife’s box.”
Just for another second he did not move.
“It is no use, Rosemary,” he said, and in his turn smiled as on something very dear, very precious, wholly unattainable. “It is no use, my dear.”
“What is no use, Peter?” she murmured.
“Thinking that all is over.”
“In six months’ time, if I am alive,” she rejoined coolly, “I shall be Jasper Tarkington’s wife.”
“I know it, dear. Jasper is my friend, and I would not harbour one disloyal thought against him. But you being the wife of an enemy or of my best friend is beside the point. I cannot shut you out of my life, strive how I may. Never. While I am as I am, and you the exquisite creature you are, so long as we are both alive, you will remain a part of my life. Whenever I catch a glimpse of you, whenever I hear the sound of your voice, my soul will thrill and long for you. Not with one thought will I be disloyal to Jasper, for in my life you will be as an exquisite spirit, an idea greater or less than woman. Just you. If you are happy I shall know it. If you grieve, Heaven help the man or woman who caused your tears. I have been a fool; yet I regret nothing. Sorrow at your hands is sweeter than any happiness on earth.”
It was quite dark where they stood side by side in this moment of supreme farewell. Each felt the inevitableness of it all﻿—the fatality. Pride on either side had built a barrier between them: honour and loyalty would consolidate it in the future. Too late! Everything was too late!
Peter bent his knee to the ground and slowly raised the hem of her gown to his lips. But Rosemary did not move: for that one instant her limbs had become marble, and in her soul she prayed that her heart, too, might turn to stone.
Then Peter rose and opened the door, and she passed out into the world again.

===Chinese===

可就在这时她抬起眼来，正遇上他的目光。
“罗斯玛丽！”他说。
她摇摇头，温和地笑了，也许带着讥诮，也带着姑息，像对受了责备的孩子。
“我这会儿最好走了，彼得，”她平静地说。“我敢肯定乔治·奥兰治正要来拖我去他太太的包厢。”
又是一秒，他没有动。
“没有用的，罗斯玛丽，”他说，轮到他微笑了，像对着一件非常亲爱、非常珍贵、全然不可企及的东西微笑。“没有用的，亲爱的。”
“什么没有用，彼得？”她喃喃地问。
“以为一切都完了，这种想法。”
“半年之后，只要我还活着，”她冷冷地应道，“我就是贾斯珀·塔金顿的妻子了。”
“我知道，亲爱的。贾斯珀是我的朋友，我不会对他存一点不义的念头。可你做我仇敌的妻子也好，做我至交的妻子也好，都无关紧要。我怎么挣扎，也不能把你关在我的生命之外。永远不能。只要我还是我，只要你还是这样一件精妙的造物，只要我们俩都活着，你就仍是我生命的一部分。每当我瞥见你，每当我听见你的声音，我的灵魂就要颤栗，就要渴慕你。对贾斯珀我不会有一念之不义，因为在我的生命里，你将是一个精魂，一个比女人更大或更小的理念。只是你。你若幸福，我会知道。你若悲伤，天佑那惹你落泪的人。我一直是个傻瓜；可我无所悔恨。得自你手的忧伤，甜过世上一切欢乐。”
在这诀别的至高一刻，两人并肩站着的地方一片幽暗。两人都感到了这一切的无可避免——那宿命。双方的骄傲在他们中间筑起了一道屏障：荣誉与忠义日后还将把它加固。太迟了！一切都太迟了！
彼得屈膝跪地，缓缓把她裙裾的边缘举向唇边。罗斯玛丽一动不动：那一瞬间，她的四肢已成了大理石，她在灵魂里祈求，她的心也能化作顽石。
随后彼得站起身，推开门，她重新走回人间去了。

## V / 五
===Original===

Outside in the corridor Rosemary met Sir George Orange, who claimed her then and there and dragged her willy-nilly to his wife’s box. She never looked back once to see what Peter was doing. He had become merged in the crowd, and, anyway, this was the end.
She found herself presently being talked to, flattered, adulated by the distinguished Romanian who turned the full battery of his mellow eyes and his persuasive tongue upon her, bent on making a breach in the wall of her prejudices and her thinly veiled enmity.
She told no one, not even Jasper, the gist of her conversation with Naniescu. He had put a proposal before her﻿—a proposal which meant work for Rosemary Fowkes﻿—the Uno of the *International Review*. He had proposed that she should go to Transylvania, study for herself the conditions now prevailing in the territory occupied by Romania, and publish the result of her studies in the English and American Press. And this was just the sort of work that Rosemary longed for, now, more than at any other time of her life. Naniescu had played his cards well. He had known how to flatter, insidiously, delicately, this popular writer who had captured the public fancy, and whose influence with pen and personality was paramount with a vast section of review and newspaper readers in England. What he had proposed could in no way hurt the most delicate scruples of an oversensitive conscience, and the proposal came as a veritable Godsend to Rosemary at this moment when her whole soul was in a turmoil of remorse, longing, and rebellion. That her love for Peter Blakeney was not dead, she had known well enough all along, but she had little dreamed until this hour how completely it still possessed her, what power his glance, his touch, his nearness still had over her. She had thought of her love as a heap of smouldering ashes, and lo! it had proved itself to be a devastating fire that burned fiercely beneath.
And Peter?
Peter had set the future above the present; his pride above his love, and she, wounded to the quick, had allowed ambition and pride to throw her into Jasper Tarkington’s arms. It was all done now. Irrevocably done. But even at the moment when she most bitterly regretted the past, she was resolved to keep her word loyally to Jasper. Sitting beside him in the car that took her home from the Albert Hall ball, she allowed her hand to rest contentedly in his.

===Chinese===

门外的走廊里，罗斯玛丽遇上了乔治·奥兰治爵士，他当场便把她认下，不由分说拉到妻子的包厢里去了。她一次也没有回头去看彼得在做什么。他已经没入人群里了；再说，这也就是终结了。
不一会儿，她就发觉自己正被那位尊贵的罗马尼亚人攀谈着、恭维着、奉承着；他把那双温柔的眼睛和那条娓娓动听的舌头全部火力都对准了她，一心要在她偏见和那层薄纱似的敌意的高墙上轰开一道缺口。
她同南尼斯库谈话的要旨，她谁也没有告诉，连贾斯珀也没告诉。他向她提出了一项提议——一项意味着给罗斯玛丽·福克斯、《国际评论》（International Review）的“乌诺”派下差事的提议。他提议她到特兰西瓦尼亚去，亲自考察罗马尼亚占领区目前的状况，把考察结果发表在英美报刊上。而这正是罗斯玛丽渴望的那一类工作，此刻比她一生任何时候都更渴望。南尼斯库这一手玩得漂亮。他知道怎样阴柔而巧妙地奉承这位风靡一时的作家——她赢得了公众的喜爱，她的笔和她的个人魔力，对英国评论和报纸读者中的一大群人有着至高无上的影响。他所提议的事，无论如何伤不着最敏感的良心、最纤细的顾忌；而对此刻的罗斯玛丽——她的整个灵魂正泡在悔恨、渴望与反抗的翻腾里——这项提议无异于天赐。她对彼得·布莱克尼的爱并没有死，这一点她心里一直清楚；可直到此刻她才约略梦到，这爱依然多么彻底地占有着她，他的目光、他的触碰、他的贴近，依然对她有多大的威力。她一直把自己的爱当作一堆焖着的死灰，可你瞧！它偏偏证实自己是一场烈火，在底下凶猛地烧。
那么彼得呢？
彼得把将来摆在了现前之上，把骄傲摆在了爱情之上；而她，伤到了心坎里，便任凭野心和骄傲把她抛进了贾斯珀·塔金顿的怀抱。如今一切都定了。无可挽回地定了。可即使在她最痛悔过去的此刻，她也决心忠诚地履行对贾斯珀的诺言。坐在送她从阿尔伯特音乐厅舞会回家的汽车里，她让自己的手安安稳稳地留在他的手里。

===Original===

His arm was round her, and her cheek rested against his shoulder. She did not speak, for she was very tired, but she listened, unshrinking, to the tender words which he whispered in her ear. Dear, kind Jasper! He had thoughts only for her. From the moment when she finally promised that she would be his wife, he had loaded her with delicate attentions and exquisite gifts. Every word he spoke was soothing and restful, so different to Peter’s tempestuous outbursts, his unrestrained, passionate eloquence that would leave her limp and bruised, unable to understand his next mood, his sudden indifference to everything save his own future pursuits.

===Chinese===

他的手臂搭着她的肩，她的脸颊靠在他肩上。她不说话，因为她太累了；可他把那些温柔的话贴在她耳边低语时，她毫不畏缩地听着。亲爱又厚道的贾斯珀！他心里只有她。从她最终答应做他妻子的那一刻起，他就用体贴的关切和精美的礼物把她堆得满满的。他说的每一句话都抚慰、都安然，同彼得的暴风骤雨多么不同——那不由分说、火热燎人的雄辩，总把她弄得瘫软乌青，摸不透他下一刻的心思，摸不透他对眼前一切、除他自己前程之外的一切，怎么说冷就冷了。

## VI / 六
===Original===

It was only a couple of days later that Rosemary broached to Jasper Tarkington the subject that was uppermost in her mind. She had lunched with him at the Ritz, and they walked together across St. James’s Park to her flat in Ashley Gardens. It was one of those rare days of June which make of England one of the most desirable countries to be alive in. The air was soft, with just that delicious feeling of moisture in it that gives additional fragrance to the scent of the hawthorn: it vibrated with the multitudinous sounds of birdsong, a twitter and a singing and a whistling that thrilled the ear with their heavenly melodies.
Rosemary Fowkes was very nearly as tall as her fiancée, and Jasper Tarkington had a slight stoop which brought his eyes on a level with hers. Scoffers were wont to say that Tarkington’s stoop was nothing but affectation; it certainly was a characteristic of him as is a monocle with some men. His whole appearance was one of super-refinement: he essentially gave the impression of a man who had seen so much of the world that he had become surfeited with it, and thoroughly weary. The weary expression was never absent from his eyes, which were very dark and set rather close together, and though he was quite a young man﻿—still on the right side of thirty﻿—there were a good many lines round them﻿—as well as round his expressive mouth and firm chin. He had slender, beautifully shaped hands which, when he walked, he kept behind his back holding a malacca cane that was adorned with a green tassel. There is no doubt that there was a hint of affectation about Jasper Tarkington’s appearance and manner, although in conversation he spoke with true Anglo-Saxon directness. He was always dressed with scrupulous correctness, and affected the Edwardian rather than the ultramodern modes. On the whole an arresting personality, whose kindly expression attenuated the somewhat harsh Wellingtonian features, and the hard outline of the narrow hatchet face.
Rosemary Fowkes, walking beside him in her irreproachably cut tailor-made looked like a young Diana, radiant with youth and health. Her skin, her eyes, her hair, the jaunty little hat she wore, the trim shoes and neat silk stockings appeared strangely out of harmony with the stooping figure of this disillusioned man of the world, with that vague air of Buckingham Palace about his grey frock coat and silk hat.

===Chinese===

不过两天之后，罗斯玛丽便向贾斯珀·塔金顿提起了她心头最要紧的那件事。她跟他一起在里茨饭店吃过午饭，两人穿过圣詹姆斯公园，往她在阿什利花园的公寓走去。这是六月里难得的日子，这样的日子使英格兰成为世上最宜人居住的国家之一。空气温柔，带着恰到好处的一丝润湿，给山楂花的香气更添了几分芬芳；空气里颤动着千声百啭的鸟鸣，呢喃、歌唱、哨音，天籁般的旋律叫人耳朵都听得醉了。
罗斯玛丽·福克斯差不多同她的未婚夫一样高，而贾斯珀·塔金顿微微伛着背，恰好使他的眼睛同她的齐平。好挖苦的人惯常说，塔金顿的伛背无非是装出来的派头；它确实已成了他的一个标记，就像单片眼镜之于某些人。他的整个人相是一种过分的精致：他给人的印象，本质上是那种见过太多世面、以致生腻、并且彻底倦怠了的人。那种倦怠的神情从不在他眼睛里缺席——那双眼睛颜色很深，长得相当近；他年纪还轻——还不到三十——眼角却已有不少细纹，嘴角和坚毅的下巴四周也有。他有一双修长漂亮的手，走路时背在身后，握一根缀着绿穗的白藤手杖。毫无疑问，贾斯珀·塔金顿的外表和举止有一点做戏的味道，虽然交谈起来，他说话倒真正是盎格鲁-撒克逊式的直截了当。他的衣着永远一丝不苟，偏爱爱德华时代的款式而不趋时新。总的说来是个惹眼的人物，他慈和的神情冲淡了那副威灵顿式的、略见严苛的相貌，冲淡了那张窄长斧形脸上冷硬的轮廓。
罗斯玛丽·福克斯走在他身旁，一身剪裁无可挑剔的定制套装，像一位年轻的狄安娜，焕发着青春和健康的光彩。她的皮肤、她的眼睛、她的头发、她那顶俏皮的小帽、整洁的鞋子和精致的丝袜，同身边这个伛背的、心灰意懒的世间过来人显得那样格格不入——他那件灰色长礼服、那顶丝帽，隐隐透着一股白金汉宫的气派。

===Original===

It was whilst walking through the park that Rosemary spoke to her fiancé about Naniescu’s proposal. Jasper listened attentively and without interrupting her, until she herself paused, obviously waiting for him to speak. Then he said:
“And you have fallen in with General Naniescu’s views?”
“Yes!” she replied, after an instant’s hesitation. “The whole thing appeals to me very much, and I am flattered by the confidence which the Romanian Government apparently has in my judgement. And of course,” she added, “I am not bound in any way.”
“Have you made any definite promises to Naniescu?”
“Not quite definite. I wanted first of all to consult your wishes.”
“Oh, my dear!” Tarkington interjected, and for one instant a light of youth and folly illumined his tired eyes. “Did I not promise you when you made me so immeasurably happy that you should be absolutely free to follow your career in whatever manner you choose? I am far too proud of you to wish to hamper you in any way.”
“You have always been the dearest, kindest, most considerate creature on God’s earth,” Rosemary rejoined, and in her eyes there came a look so soft, so tender, so womanly, that the man on whom it fell hardly dared to meet it. “But you are not forgetting, are you, Jasper,” she went on earnestly, “that politically we don’t always see eye to eye, you and I?”
“So long as we see eye to eye in other things,” he said, “what does it matter? When I asked you, my dear, to be my wife, I knew that I would not be mating with a silly doll. I am not fatuous enough to imagine that you would change the trend of your beliefs in order to harmonise them with mine.”
Rosemary made no reply for the moment. Probably had they been alone she would have put out her hand and given his a grateful and understanding squeeze. As it was, the tears gathered in her eyes, for Jasper had spoken so naturally, and at the same time so nobly, that her heart was more than ever touched by those splendid qualities in him which his actions and his words were constantly revealing to her.

===Chinese===

正是在穿过公园的时候，罗斯玛丽对未婚夫讲起了纳尼埃斯库的提议。贾斯珀留神听着，不打断她，直到她自己停住，显然在等他开口。于是他说：
“那你已经赞同纳尼埃斯库将军的看法了？”
“是的！”她迟疑了一瞬，答道。“整件事都非常合我的心意，罗马尼亚政府显然信任我的判断，这也叫我受宠若惊。当然，”她补充道，“我什么义务也没有承担。”
“你给纳尼埃斯库什么明确的承诺了吗？”
“不算明确。我想先听听你的意思。”
“哦，亲爱的！”塔金顿插话，一刹那间，一线青春和痴气的光照亮了他倦怠的眼睛。“你使我那样无法估量地幸福时，我难道没有答应过你，你可以完全自由地以你选择的任何方式去走你的事业之路？我以你为荣还来不及，哪会想用任何方式束缚你。”
“你一向是天底下最亲爱、最仁厚、最体贴的人，”罗斯玛丽应道，眼睛里浮起一种那样温柔、那样多情、那样有女人气的神情，落在它上面的人几乎不敢迎接。“可你没有忘吧，贾斯珀，”她认真地说下去，“在政治上，你我不总是一致的，是不是？”
“只要我们在别的事情上一致，”他说，“这有什么要紧？我亲爱的，我求你做我妻子的时候，就知道我不是在跟一个傻娃娃成亲。我还没有自负到那种地步，以为你会改变自己信念的走向来迁就我的。”
罗斯玛丽一时没有回答。若是只有他们两人，她多半会伸出手去，把感激和心领神会握进他的手里。此刻，泪水涌上了她的眼睛，因为贾斯珀说得那样自然，又那样高贵，她的心比以往任何时候都更被他的言行不断展现给她的那些美好品质打动了。

===Original===

Perhaps she was nearer to being in love with Jasper Tarkington at this hour that she had been since first he asked her to be his wife; and when the glory of this June afternoon, the twittering of birds, the scent of syringa and lilac in the air brought back with nerve-racking insistence memories of Peter’s voice and Peter’s touch, it was by mentally comparing the character of the two men as she knew them that she succeeded in casting those memories away.
“You are wonderfully good to me, Jasper,” she sighed.
“One cannot,” he retorted simply, “be good to that which is most precious in life: one can only worship and be grateful. But now tell me something more about your plans. I feel a little bewildered, you know, at the suddenness of them.”
“I have not yet made any definite plans,” she replied, “and as I told you, I have made no definite promise to General Naniescu. As a matter of fact, I intend writing him a final acceptance or refusal tonight.”
“But you incline towards an acceptance?”
“Frankly, yes!”
“That would mean﻿—?” he queried.
“That I start for Budapest within the next few days.”
“What about your passport?”
“General Naniescu assured me that he would see to that.”
“But you would not stay long in Budapest?”
“No, only a couple of days. I shall go straight on to Transylvania. I have been there before, you know.”
“No, I did not know.”
“Peter’s mother was a great friend of mine. You know I was a motherless kid, and she took me under her wing on many, many occasions. At one time I travelled with her a good deal, and she took me several times with her when she went to Transylvania to stay with her relations. I know them all. They are dears.”
“And, of course, they are extraordinarily hospitable over there,” Tarkington admitted dryly.
“Hospitable to a fault! Mrs. Blakeney’s sister, who is Countess Imrey, was kindness itself to me when I was in Transylvania two years ago for the *International*. In any case, I should go to her first. The Imreys have a beautiful château not far from Kolozsvár.”
“I am afraid we must call it Cluj now,” Jasper interposed with a smile.
“Yes,” Rosemary retorted hotly. “Aren’t those little pinpricks damnable? Changing the name of a city that has been Hungarian for centuries, and that has been the centre of some of the most epoch-making movements in Hungarian history. It is mean and petty!

===Chinese===

也许在这一个钟头，她比贾斯珀·塔金顿初次求她做妻子以来的任何时候都更接近于爱他；当这六月午后的光艳、鸟雀的啁啾、空气里山梅花和丁香的香气，以折磨神经的执拗把彼得的声音、彼得的抚触带回回忆里来时，她正是靠在心里比较这两个她所熟识的男人的品格，才把那些回忆甩开的。
“你对我太好了，贾斯珀。”她叹道。
“对生命中最珍贵的东西，”他简单地回敬，“谈不上好：只能崇拜和感激。不过现在再给我讲讲你的计划。说实话，它们来得这么突然，我有点晕头转向。”
“我还没有什么确定的计划，”她答道，“就像我告诉你的，我也没给纳尼埃斯库将军什么明确的承诺。其实，我打算今晚就写信给他，最后答允或者回绝。”
“可你是倾向于答允的？”
“坦白说，是的！”
“那就意味着——？”他问。
“意味着几天之内我就动身去布达佩斯。”
“那你的护照呢？”
“纳尼埃斯库将军向我保证，这事他会打理。”
“可你不会在布达佩斯久留吧？”
“不，只待两三天。我就直接去特兰西瓦尼亚。我从前去过那儿，你知道的。”
“不，我还不知道。”
“彼得的母亲是我的一位挚友。你知道我从小没了娘，好多好多回都是她把我收在她翅膀底下。有一阵我常跟着她旅行，她去特兰西瓦尼亚住亲戚家时，也带我去过好几次。那边的人我都认得，个个都可爱。”
“当然，那边的人好客得出奇。”塔金顿干巴巴地承认。
“好客得过了头！布莱克尼太太的姐姐，就是伊姆雷伯爵夫人，两年前我为《国际评论》去特兰西瓦尼亚时，待我真是好到家了。无论如何，我会先去她那儿。伊姆雷家在离克洛兹瓦尔不远的地方有一座漂亮的城堡。”
“恐怕我们如今得管它叫克卢日了。”贾斯珀含笑插话。
“可不是嘛，”罗斯玛丽愤愤地回敬。“这些小针尖儿扎人还不够可恶吗？把一座匈牙利人住了几百年的城、一座在匈牙利历史上好几次开风气之先的城改了名字！又卑鄙又小气！

===Original===

You must admit, Jasper,” she insisted, “that it is mean and far more galling to a proud, if conquered, nation than other more tangible deeds of oppression. Why, even the Germans when they took Alsace-Lorraine from France did not rename their towns!”
Jasper Tarkington smiled at her vehemence.
“Naniescu, I perceive,” he said, “has set himself a difficult task.”
“He has,” she admitted with a merry laugh. “But I left him no illusions on the subject. He knows that at the present moment, and with all the knowledge which﻿—as I reminded him﻿—I gathered at first hand two years ago, I am just as severe a critic of his government as I was then. He, on the other hand, declares that if I will divest myself of every prejudice and go to Transylvania with an open mind, I shall understand that Romania is acting not only in her own, very obvious, interests, but also in the interests of European peace. Well,” Rosemary concluded gaily, “I am going to accept General Naniescu’s challenge, and I am going to Transylvania with an open mind. I am to have a perfectly free hand. Not a word in any article I choose to write is to be censored: he declares that he will show me the truth, and nothing but the truth, and that his government is only too ready to accord me every facility for investigation and for placing the case before the British public.”
She paused to draw breath after this long peroration. As she walked so freely along, the eyes of many a passerby were cast with undisguised admiration on the graceful girlish figure, the face aglow with youth and animation, the sparkling eyes, the lips which Nature had so obviously framed for a kiss. Jasper Tarkington said nothing for the moment; when she had finished speaking he sighed, involuntarily perhaps, and his tired eyes took on a still more wearied look. Was it that he felt he could not altogether follow this exquisite woman along the path of ambition which she trod with so youthful a step? Was he just a little too old, a little to blasé, to share all that enthusiasm, that pride, that burning desire to live every moment of the span of life, to fill every hour with deeds and spoken thoughts which would abide when youth had gone?
Who shall say?

===Chinese===

“你得承认，贾斯珀，”她坚持道，“这比其他更实在的压迫行径更卑鄙，更叫一个骄傲的——虽然是战败的——民族痛心。瞧，连德国人从法国手里夺走阿尔萨斯-洛林的时候，也没有给那些城镇改名！”
贾斯珀·塔金顿对着她的激烈微笑了。
“我看得出，”他说，“纳尼埃斯库给自己揽了件难办的差事。”
“可不，”她带着快活的笑承认。“不过这事我没让他抱幻想。他知道，此时此刻，凭着我——我提醒过他——两年前第一手搜集的全部了解，我对他的政府仍旧是同当年一样严厉的批评者。他呢，则宣称只要我抛开一切成见、敞开思想去特兰西瓦尼亚，我就会明白，罗马尼亚的所作所为不只为她自己那点一目了然的利益，也是为了欧洲的和平。好吧，”罗斯玛丽轻快地作结，“我打算接受纳尼埃斯库将军的挑战，敞开思想去特兰西瓦尼亚。我会得到完全的自由。我愿意写的任何文章，一个字也不得删检：他宣称要给我看真相，全部的真相，还说他的政府非常乐意给我一切调查的便利，好把案情摆到英国公众面前。”
这一番长篇陈词之后她停下来喘气。她这样轻盈地走着，路人莫不用毫不掩饰的艳羡打量这婀娜的少女身影：那张因青春与生气而容光焕发的脸，那双明亮的眼睛，那双老天爷分明照着亲吻的模样造就的嘴唇。贾斯珀·塔金顿一时没有作声；等她说完，他叹了口气——也许是不由自主的——倦怠的眼睛里添了更倦的神色。是不是他觉得，自己无法完全跟随这个精妙的女人走上她以如此青春的步子踏着的雄心之路？是不是他稍嫌太老了些、太心如止水了些，分享不了她的那份热忱、那份自豪、那种把生命里的每一刻都活到极致、用青春逝去后仍能长存的事迹和言论填满每个钟头的炽烈欲望？
谁说得清呢？

===Original===

Jasper Tarkington had never been communicative; his best friends knew little of his life, and though he, too, in his day had used his unquestioned mental gifts for political journalism, he had never been the ardent propagandist that this beautiful apostle of lost causes desired to be. His silence now acted as a slight damper on Rosemary’s enthusiasm.
“I am sorry, dear,” she said gently. “I always seem to forget that you and I are in opposite camps over this one thing.”
“We shan’t be that for long,” he retorted lightly, “if Naniescu’s hopes are fulfilled.”
Strangely enough, just as he spoke he saw General Naniescu and M. de Kervoisin, who were entering the park at Queen Anne’s Gate as they themselves were coming out of it. The three men raised their hats, and Rosemary gave Naniescu and his friend a pleasant nod.
“I don’t think,” Tarkington said after a moment of two, “that our friend Naniescu will be very fond of me after this.”
“Why? On the contrary, he should be grateful that you have not tried to oppose him in any way.”
“I am going to oppose him in one way, though,” Jasper resumed earnestly. “I don’t intend to interfere with his plans or yours, my dear, as I said before; but there is one thing I am going to ask you, Rosemary.”
“What is it, dear?” she asked impulsively. “I am so glad you are going to ask me for something. All the giving has been on your side up to now.”
“Not so fast, little one. You mayn’t be ready to do what I want.”
“Is that likely?” she retorted. Then added with gentle earnestness: “There is nothing in the world I wouldn’t do for you, Jasper.”
“Will you marry me,” he asked abruptly, “before you go away?”
She did not reply immediately, for in truth she was very much taken aback. Her engagement to Jasper Tarkington was very recent, and up to now he had not once spoken of a definite date for the marriage. She felt herself placed in an awkward position, for the fact that only a few seconds ago she had assured him that there was nothing she would not do for him. And now this request for an immediate marriage. She certainly was not prepared for it. Everything in her urged her to refuse. The memory of that hour in the box at the Albert Hall, her talk with Peter, her realisation that Peter still held her heart, still ruled over her thoughts; everything, in fact, except a sense of gratitude urged her to refuse.

===Chinese===

贾斯珀·塔金顿从来不爱吐露心事；他最好的朋友对他的一生也知之甚少。他当年虽然也把自己无可置疑的头脑用于政论新闻，却从不像这位美丽的不幸事业之传道者所渴望成为的那样，是个热烈的鼓动家。此刻他的沉默给罗斯玛丽的热忱稍稍泼了点冷水。
“对不起，亲爱的，”她温柔地说。“我总是忘了，在这一件事上，你我是对立的两营。”
“这话长不了，”他轻快地回敬，“要是纳尼埃斯库的希望能够实现的话。”
说来也怪，他话音刚落，就看见纳尼埃斯库将军和德·凯尔瓦赞先生正从安妮女王门走进公园，而他们两个恰从公园里走出去。三个男人举帽致意，罗斯玛丽朝纳尼埃斯库和他的朋友愉快地点了点头。
“我看，”过了一两分钟，塔金顿说，“这回以后，咱们那位朋友纳尼埃斯库不会再喜欢我了。”
“为什么？恰恰相反，你一点也没设法反对他，他该感激才是。”
“不过我倒要在一点上反对他，”贾斯珀认真地说下去。“亲爱的，就像我方才说的，我不打算干涉他的计划，也不打算干涉你的；可是有一件事要求你，罗斯玛丽。”
“什么事，亲爱的？”她冲动地问。“你肯向我要求点什么，我真高兴。到现在为止，给的一直是你。”
“先别急，小家伙。我要你做的事，你未必肯做呢。”
“那可能吗？”她回敬。随即又温柔而认真地加了一句：“为了你，世上没有什么我不肯做的，贾斯珀。”
“你肯不肯，”他突兀地问，“在我们出发之前嫁给我？”
她没有立刻回答，说实在的，她大大吃了一惊。她与贾斯珀·塔金顿的订婚还不久，直到此刻，他一次也没谈起过婚期的确切日子。她觉得自己处境很窘：几秒钟之前她才向他保证过，为了他世上没有她不肯做的事，而现在却来了这个立刻结婚的请求。她确实毫无准备。她内里的一切都在催她拒绝。对阿尔伯特音乐厅包厢里那一小时的记忆，她同彼得的谈话，她意识到彼得依然握着她的心、依然统治着她的思想——事实上，除了感激之心，一切都在催她拒绝。

===Original===

And yet she could not﻿—not after what she had said, not after all that Jasper Tarkington had done for her. While all these thoughts were whirling in her brain as she walked along, mechanically now, all the spring gone out of her step, something of the joy of living gone out of her spirits, she vaguely heard Jasper’s quiet, gentle voice.
“You mean so much to me, Rosemary,” he was saying, “that life here in England while you were God knows where, in tribulation, perhaps, perhaps in danger, needing me too, perhaps without knowing it, would be unendurable. I could not do it. I should follow you, anyway, and come as near to you as I dared, yet without the right to look after you as closely as I would wish. Well, my dear, you are far too womanly and kind to inflict such torture upon me. For it would be torture, and I would go under through it all. I don’t know if you quite understand, but﻿—”
There was an unusual vibration in his voice; it seemed as if, for once, passion would get the better of his habitual restraint. Tarkington always spoke slowly and directly, but for once words appeared to be failing him. However, just then they turned into Victoria Street, and the noise and bustle of traffic, his meticulous care of Rosemary while they crossed the road, brought him back to the prosiness of life. Nor did he speak again till they had reached the quietude of Ashley Gardens.
“Will you come up?” Rosemary asked, pausing at the entrance of one of the blocks of flats.
He shook his head.
“I think I would like you to think it all over quietly,” he said. “I want you to remember that when I am asking you to hurry on our marriage, I only do it because I want to have the right to look after you. I won’t interfere with you in any way whatever. I give you my word that as my wife you will be every bit as free as you are now﻿—more so, really, because in that part of Europe a married woman can claim an independence which convention absolutely denies to a girl. In Budapest you will meet people of your own nationality, and of your own set. I could not bear the thought that your loveliness would leave you a ready prey to gossip or malice. There now,” he added, with a self-deprecatory smile, “I have said more than I meant to. My first excuse is that you are more than life to me, and as you are so precious, I foresee dangers where perhaps none exist.

===Chinese===

然而她不能拒绝——说了那些话之后不能，贾斯珀·塔金顿为她做了一切之后更不能。这些念头在她脑子里打着旋，她继续往前走，此刻已是机械地迈步，脚步里的春天没有了，生之欢愉也去了一分，贾斯珀那安静温和的声音她只恍惚听见。
“你对我是这样要紧，罗斯玛丽，”他正说着，“你到天知道什么地方去，受苦，也许有危险，也许需要我而自己不觉得——那时我留在英国的生活，会叫人受不了。我做不到。我反正会跟着你去，尽量靠近你，可又没有权利如我希望的那样贴身照顾你。唉，亲爱的，你这样有女人气、这样仁厚，断不肯叫我受这样的刑。那真是酷刑，我会整个儿垮掉的。我不知道你是否完全明白，不过——”
他的嗓音里有一种不寻常的颤动；似乎这一次，激情就要压倒他惯常的克制。塔金顿说话素来缓慢而直接，可这一次他似乎找不到话了。好在这时他们转进了维多利亚街，街上的喧嚷和车马、过马路时他对罗斯玛丽无微不至的照拂，把他带回了生活的平淡。直到走进阿什利花园的清静，他才又开口。
“上来坐坐吗？”罗斯玛丽在一片公寓楼门口停下来问。
他摇摇头。
“我想让你安安静静把这一切想清楚，”他说。“我要你记住，我催我们的婚事，只因为我想取得照顾你的权利。我绝不会以任何方式干涉你。我向你保证，做了我的妻子，你同现在一样自由——真的，还会更自由些，因为在欧洲那一带，一个已婚女子能享有习俗绝对不肯给一个姑娘的独立。在布达佩斯你会遇到你自己的同胞、你自己圈子的人。一想到你的美貌会叫你成为闲话和恶意的现成猎物，我就受不了。好了，”他带着自嘲的微笑加了一句，“我说得比我本意的多了。我的第一个借口是，你对我比生命还重；你既然这样珍贵，我便在没有危险的地方也预见危险。

===Original===

My second is that I am pleading for my own happiness﻿—I was almost going to say for my life. You are not like other women, Rosemary; you are above the petty conventions of trousseaux and crowded weddings. As soon as I have your answer I will get the special licence and we’ll be married in your parish church without fuss and ceremony. So think it over, my dear, and let me have your answer as early tomorrow morning as you can. Remember that I shall scarcely live until I have your answer.”
She made no reply; only put out her hand, which he took in his. There was no glove on it, and for a moment it seemed that in spite of passersby, in spite of the conventional atmosphere of this part of London, he would raise that little hand to his lips. His eyes rested on her with a look of passionate desire; so intense was his gaze that suddenly she felt almost afraid. Rosemary had never seen Jasper’s eyes look quite like that. As a rule they were so gentle, sometimes mildly ironical, at others only weary. But now it almost seemed as if, in order to bend her will to his, he was striving to exert some kind of power that was outside himself, as if he had called to his aid forces that would prove more invincible than those that were within him. The spell﻿—it seemed like a spell﻿—only lasted a couple of seconds; the next instant his look had turned to one of infinite tenderness. He patted her hand and reiterated gently:
“Think it over, my dear, when you are alone.”
Instantly she felt the tears gathering in her eyes. His gentleness, his tender care of her, appealed to all that was truly womanly in Rosemary Fowkes. Self-reliant, brilliantly clever, independent in thought and actions as she was, she responded all the more readily to a man’s desire for the right to protect as well as to cherish. Her independence had found its birth in loneliness. Fatherless, motherless in very early life, she had soon enough shaken herself free from any trammels that well-meaning relations desired to put over her actions. Her genius had consolidated her independence, but it had never stifled those vague longings for submission and self-abnegation which are the sublime satisfaction of a true woman’s soul.
After Jasper Tarkington left her, and when she was alone in her flat, Rosemary Fowkes turned to the one thing that had never failed her in the great moments of her life. She turned to prayer.

===Chinese===

“第二个借口是，我在为自己的幸福恳求——几乎要说是为我的性命。你不像别的女人，罗斯玛丽；嫁妆啦、闹哄哄的婚礼啦，这些琐碎的规矩都在你之下。一得到你的答复，我就去办特别许可证，我们在你教区的教堂里成婚，不声张，不铺张。所以想一想吧，亲爱的，明天一早，越早越好，把答复给我。记住，等你的答复时，我简直活不下去。”
她没有回答；只伸出手来，他把它握在自己手里。那手上没有戴手套，有一瞬间，他仿佛要不理会路人、不理会伦敦这一带拘谨的空气，把那只小手举到唇边。他望着她，目光里是炽热的渴望；那凝视强烈得很，她忽然几乎觉得害怕了。罗斯玛丽从没见过贾斯珀的眼睛这个样子。平日里它们是那样温和，有时带着淡淡的讥诮，有时只是倦怠。可此刻，他几乎像是要把她的意志拗向自己的意志，正竭力施展出某种身外的力量，仿佛他召来了比他自身所有的更强悍、更不可抗拒的援军。这道符咒——它像一道符咒——只持续了两三秒；转瞬间，他的神情已化作无限的温柔。他拍拍她的手，轻轻地又说了一遍：
“想一想吧，亲爱的，等你独自一个人的时候。”
她顿时觉得泪水涌上眼睛。他的温柔、他对她的体贴呵护，唤起了罗斯玛丽·福克斯身上一切真正的女性。她自立、聪明绝顶、思想行动都独立，却正因如此，更乐于响应一个男人想要保护兼珍惜的权利。她的独立诞生于孤独。极早就没了爹娘，她不久便挣脱了好心的亲戚们想加在她行动上的一切羁绊。她的天分巩固了她的独立，却从来没有窒息那种对依顺与自我舍弃的朦胧渴望——那是一个真正的女人的灵魂至高的满足。
贾斯珀·塔金顿走后，罗斯玛丽·福克斯独自待在公寓里，转向她一生重大关头从未辜负过她的那件事。她转向祈祷。

===Original===

On her knees, and with her heart filled with longing and a sorrow that she dared not face, she prayed for help and for guidance. She had no one to turn to but Him who said with infinite understanding and love: “Come unto me all ye that travail and are heavy laden and I will refresh you.”
In the midst of worldly joys, satisfied ambition, hopes for the future and pride in the past, Rosemary Fowkes would tonight have felt desperately lonely and lost in bewilderment before a divided duty﻿—duty to self, duty to Jasper﻿—but for the comfort of prayer, the thought of all that lay beyond this world of ours, a world that is so sordid and petty even at its best.

===Chinese===

她跪下来，心里盛满渴望和一种她不敢面对的悲伤，祈求帮助，祈求指引。除了那位以无限的理解和慈爱说过“凡劳苦担重担的人，可以到我这里来，我就使你们得安息”的主，她无人可投。
若不是祈祷的安慰，若不是想到我们这个世界之外还有的一切——这个世界纵在最好的时候也这样污浊、琐碎——那么置身尘世的欢愉、满足了的雄心、对将来的希望、对过去的自豪之中的罗斯玛丽·福克斯，今夜面对一分为二的职责——对己之责，对贾斯珀之责——定会感到孤苦无依、惶然若失。

## VII / 七
===Original===

The next two or three weeks were like a dream for Rosemary Fowkes. She left herself no time to think. The future beckoned to her with enticing arms, holding prospects of activities, of work that would fill the mind to the exclusion of memory. That evening when she rose from her knees, she rose with a resolve, and never for one moment after that did she allow herself an instant of regret. She wrote a line to Jasper to tell him that she would do as he wished; she was prepared to marry him as soon as his own arrangements were completed.
She also wrote to General Naniescu, agreeing to his proposal. She reserved to herself complete freedom of action to send any articles or reports she chose to English or foreign Press; all that she desired from him was a confirmatory letter, promising that nothing she ever wrote would pass through the censor’s hands. This he at once sent her. Nothing could be more fair, more straightforward. Rosemary’s chivalrous mind responded wholeheartedly to Naniescu’s generosity, and the feeling that it would probably be in her power to do real good, not only to individuals but to peoples, acted as a soothing balm upon her bruised heart.
On the other hand, nothing could have exceeded Jasper’s kindness and consideration during the days immediately preceding her marriage. It almost seemed as if his super-sensitive soul had received a faint inkling of what was going on in Rosemary’s mind. Nothing appeared too onerous, no sacrifice too great where Rosemary’s comfort and desires were at stake, and at times﻿—such are the contradictions of a woman’s nature﻿—she felt almost impatient with him for his magnanimity, almost obsessed by the unselfishness of his love.
She only saw Peter Blakeney once before she and Jasper left for Budapest, and that was on the day of her wedding. By one of those involuntary blunders so peculiar to dim-sighted lovers, Jasper Tarkington had asked Peter to be his best man. What it was that had induced Peter to accept, Rosemary could not conjecture. His impulses had always been strange and unaccountable, and this one was more unaccountable than most. Perhaps he merely wished to pander to his own mad desire to see her once again, perhaps it was just a semi-barbaric instinct in him that pushed him to self-torture.

===Chinese===

接下来的两三个星期，对罗斯玛丽·福克斯来说恍如一场梦。她不给自己留一点思考的工夫。未来正张开诱人的双臂向她招手，捧出一派事业的前景，一桩桩足以填满心头、把记忆挤出去的工作。那天晚上她从膝上站起来时，是带着决断站起来的；打那以后，她一刻也不容自己有半瞬的悔意。她给贾斯珀写了一封短信，告诉他她愿意照他的意思办；只等他自己的安排停当，她随时可以同他结婚。
她还写信给南尼斯库将军，接受了他的提议。她给自己保留了完全的行动自由：想给英国或外国报刊投什么文章、什么报道，全由她自己；她向他要求的只有一封确认函，保证她写的任何东西都不经检查官之手。这封信他立刻就寄来了。再没有比这更公道、更爽直的了。罗斯玛丽那侠义的心肠全心全意地响应了南尼斯库的慷慨，而那种也许真能为个人、甚至为民族做点实事的感觉，像一剂安抚的香膏，敷在她受了伤的心上。
另一方面，在她婚期前的那些日子里，贾斯珀的体贴和关怀也无可复加。他那过分敏感的灵魂，仿佛已隐隐约约摸到了罗斯玛丽心里正在进行的事。凡关系到罗斯玛丽的舒适和心愿，没有什么差事是太重的，没有一样牺牲是太大的；有时——女人的天性本就这般矛盾——她几乎因他的宽宏而不耐烦，几乎被他那份无私的爱缠得发慌。
她同贾斯珀动身去布达佩斯之前，只见过彼得·布莱克尼一面，那正是在她结婚的那天。贾斯珀·塔金顿犯了近视的情人特有的那种不由自主的糊涂，竟请彼得做他的伴郎。是什么促使彼得答应下来，罗斯玛丽猜不透。他的冲动向来古怪、说不清，这一次比哪一次都更说不清。也许他不过想满足那再见她一面的疯狂欲望，也许只是他身上那股半野蛮的本能，推着他去自我折磨。

===Original===

Rosemary by now had sufficient hold over herself to meet him calmly; not one line in her beautiful face, not one look in her haunting eyes, betrayed what she felt, after the wedding ceremony, when she accepted Peter’s warmly expressed good wishes for her happiness. Even her sensitive ear could not detect the faintest note of irony or bitterness in his voice. After that he said a few words about the projected journey to Hungary, about which Jasper had spoken to him. She would be seeing his relatives there﻿—the Imreys, the Heves. Elza Imrey was his mother’s sister and such a dear, and Philip used to be a jolly boy; but Rosemary knew them all. She knew she would be made very welcome. Peter ended by speaking with great earnestness about his little cousin Anna Heves; her father, who had been Mrs. Blakeney’s only brother, was dead, and Peter had an idea that Anna was not altogether happy.
“She has left home for some reason I can’t quite fathom,” he said, “and lives now at Kolozsvár﻿—I mean Cluj. She writes to me sometimes, and when I know the exact day when you will be in Cluj, I will write and tell her to go and see you. I suppose you will put up at the Pannonia?”
Rosemary nodded and Peter went on talking about little Anna, as he called her. “I know you will be kind to her,” he said. “You remember her as a child, of course; in a way she is still a child, and so pretty and enthusiastic. Give her a kiss from me when you see her.”
Which Rosemary, of course, promised to do. Then she gave him her hand, without saying anything, for she could not trust herself to speak much, and he kissed it just above the wrist, but more like a knight doing homage to his lady than a lover who gazed, perhaps for the last time, on the woman he worshiped.
It was after the marriage ceremony that the dreamland in which Rosemary had moved these past days became more intangible, more of a spirit-world than before. The brief days in a dreary hotel at Folkestone would have been unendurable but for her state of mind, which almost amounted to semiconsciousness. Then came the weary journey to Budapest, the sleepless night in the train, the awful meals in the crowded, stuffy restaurant-car, the ceaseless *rub-a-dub-dub*, *rub-a-dub-dub* of the wheels that bore her away farther﻿—ever farther from that bygone world which had become the might-have-been.

===Chinese===

到了这时，罗斯玛丽已能把自己约束得足以平静地见他；婚礼之后，她接受彼得那番热烈道出的幸福祝愿时，美丽的脸上一丝纹路、那双萦怀难忘的眼睛里一丝神色，都没有泄露她心中的感受。连她那双敏感的耳朵，也听不出他嗓音里有半点讥诮或苦涩的影子。随后他谈了几句匈牙利之行——贾斯珀同他说过这事。她会在那边见到他的亲戚——伊姆雷家、赫维斯家。埃尔莎·伊姆雷是他母亲的妹妹，是位可人；菲利普从前是个快活孩子；可这些罗斯玛丽都认得。她知道自己会受到殷勤的接待。末了，彼得极恳切地谈起了他的小表妹安娜·赫维斯；她的父亲——布莱克尼太太唯一的兄弟——已经去世，彼得总觉得安娜过得并不怎么快活。
“她因为某种我摸不透的原因离家了，”他说，“现在住在克洛兹瓦尔（Kolozsvár）——我是说克卢日（Cluj）。她有时给我写信。等你确定了到克卢日的日子，我就写信叫她去看你。你们会住潘农尼亚旅馆吧？”
罗斯玛丽点头，彼得又继续谈小安娜——他这样称呼她。“我知道你会待她好的，”他说，“你当然记得她小时候的样子；某种意义上说她现在还是个孩子，又漂亮又热心。见到她时替我吻她一下。”
这个罗斯玛丽自然答应了。然后她伸出手去，什么也没说，因为不敢多开口；他在她腕上轻轻吻了那只手，不像一个也许正最后一次凝望心上人的恋人，倒像一位向贵妇致敬的骑士。
婚礼之后，罗斯玛丽这几天游历其中的梦境，变得更加抓摸不透了，比先前更像一个精灵世界。在福克斯通一家乏味的旅馆里度过的短短几日，若不是她那近乎半昏迷的心境，本是捱不下来的。接着是去布达佩斯的困乏旅程：火车上不眠的一夜，拥挤闷热的餐车里难以下咽的饭食，车轮没完没了的*哐嗒哐嗒*、*哐嗒哐嗒*，载着她越来越远——离那个已成“本可如是”的旧世界越来越远。

===Original===

And through it all, like a ray of light, so persistent that it ceased to impress, was Jasper’s constant, unwearying care of her. He never seemed too tired to minister to her wants, to arrange cushions for her, a footstool, to open or close the window, the thousand and one little attentions, in fact, which most travellers are too self-engrossed to render.
And as Rosemary sat in her corner seat during those two wearisome days gazing out of the window with eyes that failed to take in the beauties of successive landscapes, her mind gradually became at peace with her heart. Her youth, her buoyancy of spirits, reasserted themselves, made her envisage life in all its brightest aspects, as it presented itself before her with cornucopia filled to the brim with all that made it worth the living. Work and a noble mate! What more could heart of woman desire? And Rosemary closed her eyes, and in a quickly fleeting dream sighed for the one thing that would have made her life a paradise, and﻿—still dreaming﻿—she felt hot tears of regret trickle slowly down her cheeks.
She woke to feel Jasper’s arms around her and his lips kissing away her tears.

===Chinese===

而在这一切之中，像一道光线一样贯穿始终、持久得反而不为人觉察的，是贾斯珀对她无微不至、不懈不怠的照料。他从不显得疲惫得顾不上伺候她的需要：替她垫靠垫、摆脚凳、开窗关窗，以及千百件大多数旅人因为只顾自己而懒得去做的小殷勤。
那劳顿的两天里，罗斯玛丽坐在角落的座位上望着窗外，一任眼前次第闪过的美景从眼中溜走；渐渐地，她的头脑同她的心达成了和解。她的青春、她轻快的天性重新抬头，教她从最明亮的一面看待人生——人生正捧着一只堆满值得活着的一切的丰饶角站在她面前。事业，和高贵的伴侣！女人的心还能奢求什么？于是罗斯玛丽合上眼，在一个倏忽即逝的梦里，为那唯一能把她的生命变成天堂的东西叹息了一声——仍旧在梦里——她感到悔恨的热泪顺着脸颊慢慢地淌。
她醒过来时，只觉贾斯珀的手臂搂着她，他的唇正把她的眼泪一一吻去。

## VIII / 八
===Original===

Budapest had been baking all day under a merciless sun in late July. But at this hour the coolness of a clear moonlit evening sent everyone out of doors. The Corso was crowded.
Rosemary Tarkington, on the terrace of the café, sat sipping delicious coffee and lazily watching the throng. Now and then she would look straight out before her, and her eyes would lose all sense of fatigue as she gazed on the incomparable panorama before her: the ornate palace of the Hapsburgs, and the cathedral of St. Matthias, and on the left, towering above all, high upon the rock, the great grim fortress that for over a century had held the Turks at bay and saved Europe from the hordes of Islam. One by one tiny lights began to wink and to blink in the houses that rose tier upon tier on the slopes across the river, whilst down below gaily illuminated boats flitted to and fro upon the turbulent waters of the Danube, carrying a burden of merrymakers home from the shady island of Ste. Marguerite close by. The whole scene before Rosemary’s eyes was one of unrivalled picturesqueness and animation. No town in Europe presents quite so enthralling a spectacle, and one whose charm is still further enhanced by the strains of those half-sad, half-voluptuous Hungarian melodies which come to the ear from out the shadows, or from the passing river boats, gentle as a caress, soothing to nerves and senses by their sweet melancholy rhythm, or exhilarating when they break into their peculiarly harmonious syncopated cadences.
Rosemary had specially elected to put up at the Hungaria rather than in one of the more modern, recently built hotels. For her the Hungaria was full of associations, of joyous times spent there when she was still a schoolgirl in the days before the war. She had travelled in Hungary and Transylvania under ideal conditions with Mrs. Blakeney, Peter’s mother, seeing the best this romantic country had to offer, welcomed always with that large-hearted hospitality peculiar to these kindly people.

===Chinese===

七月末的布达佩斯，一整天被无情的太阳烤着。可到了这个时辰，清朗月夜的凉意把人人都请出了门。滨河大道（Corso）上人头攒动。
罗斯玛丽·塔金顿坐在咖啡馆的露台上，啜着美味的咖啡，懒懒地望着人群。她时不时正眼望向前面，凝望眼前那幅无与伦比的全景时，眼睛里的倦意便一扫而空：哈布斯堡王朝装饰华丽的王宫，圣马加什大教堂，还有左边高踞岩上、俯临一切的森严大堡垒——一个多世纪以来，它把土耳其人挡在外面，使欧洲免遭伊斯兰游群的蹂躏。对岸层层叠叠依山而起的屋宇里，小小的灯火一盏接一盏地眨起眼来；底下，灯火通明的游船在多瑙河湍急的水面上来回穿行，载着一船船寻欢的人，从近旁绿荫覆顶的圣玛格丽特岛回家去。罗斯玛丽眼前的整幅景象，秀丽与热闹兼绝。欧洲没有哪座城能呈现出这样迷人的奇观，而它的魅力又添上一层乐音——那些半忧伤、半旖旎的匈牙利旋律，从暗处、从驶过的河船上传进耳中，温柔如爱抚，那甜美悒郁的节奏抚慰着神经与感官，一旦转入它那特有的、和悦的切分音调，又令人振奋。
罗斯玛丽特意拣了匈牙利饭店（Hungaria）下榻，没有住那些更时新、新近才盖起来的旅馆。在她，匈牙利饭店满是旧联想——战前她还是女学生的年月，在那里度过的一段段快乐时光。她曾在理想的条件下随布莱克尼太太——彼得的母亲——游历匈牙利和特兰西瓦尼亚，把这浪漫国度最好的东西看了个够，处处受到这些厚道人特有的那种宽厚热忱的款待。

===Original===

But memory recalled more strenuous times, too, those in the early days of her journalistic career, when her heart was filled with pity for the sufferings of a proud and ill-starred country, whose fairest lands had been flung like rags by thoughtless politicians as a sop to those who had been her associates in the war until the hour when self-interest prompted them to throw in their lot with the other side.
“You must be very tired, Lady Tarkington,” a pleasant voice said close to her elbow.
“Not tired,” Rosemary replied, “but rather dazed. The journey over from England is slower and much more fatiguing than it used to be.”
Captain and Mrs. Payson were sitting beside her at the table. Recently attached to the British Military Mission in Hungary, Captain Payson and his young wife lived at the Hungaria. It had been a great pleasure for them to see Rosemary again, whom they had known for several years, and after supper they had all foregathered on the terrace over their coffee. Some few minutes before this Jasper had elected to take a turn on the Corso, to stretch his legs and to smoke a cigar, but Rosemary felt too lazy to move, and she like to talk to the Paysons, who were genial and intellectual, and with whom she had a great deal in common in the way of associations and friends.
“The place has not altered much,” Rosemary went on after a while. “The people here are always gay and cheerful﻿—in spite of﻿—of everything.”
“Yes,” little Mrs. Payson assented lightly. “Give them their music, their delicious wines and perfect cooking, and nine out of ten Hungarians won’t care if they are ruled by King or Emperor, by foreign tyrant or Bolshevist ruffian.”
“I always think Ruth is wrong when she says that,” Captain Payson put in earnestly. “The Hungarians are sportsmen, as we are, and they are taking their punishment like sportsmen. They are not going to let the world see how much they suffer. In that way they are very different from the Germans.”
“They behaved with unparalleled folly,” Rosemary remarked.
“Yes,” the captain retorted, “and with commendable loyalty. The Hungarians are a nation of gentlemen, just as the British. They, like ourselves, are worshippers of tradition. They are royalists in their hearts, almost to a man.

===Chinese===

可记忆也唤回更艰难的岁月——她记者生涯早期的那些日子，那时她心里满是对一个骄傲而不幸的国家的怜悯：它最丰美的国土，被轻率的政客们像破布一样抛出去，赏给那些直到利害逼着他们倒向另一边之前一直与它同袍作战的伙伴们。
“您一定很累了，塔金顿勋爵夫人。”一个悦耳的声音在她肘边响起。
“不累，”罗斯玛丽答，“只是有点发懵。从英国过来的这趟路，比从前更慢，也累人得多。”
佩森上尉和佩森太太正同她坐在一桌。上尉新近派驻英国驻匈牙利军事代表团，同年轻的妻子就住在匈牙利饭店。重见相识多年的罗斯玛丽，他们高兴极了；晚饭后大家便聚到露台上喝咖啡。这之前几分钟，贾斯珀自去滨河大道上遛一圈，伸伸腿，抽支雪茄；罗斯玛丽懒得动，也乐意同佩森夫妇聊聊——两位和气又有见识，论交游和朋友，同她共通的地方很多。
“这地方没怎么变，”过了一会儿罗斯玛丽接着说，“这里的人总是这样快快活活——不管——不管怎样。”
“是啊，”小小的佩森太太轻快地应和，“只要有音乐，有美酒和一手好菜，十个匈牙利人倒有九个不在乎统治他们的是国王还是皇帝，是外国暴君还是布尔什维克流氓。”
“鲁丝说这话我就总认为不对，”佩森上尉恳切地插进来，“匈牙利人是运动家，同我们一样，正拿运动家的气度承受他们的惩罚。他们不肯让世界看见自己受多大的苦。这一点上他们同德国人大不一样。”
“他们当年的行为愚蠢透顶，”罗斯玛丽说。
“是啊，”上尉回道，“可也忠诚得可敬。匈牙利是一个绅士的民族，正如英国人。他们同我们一样崇奉传统。他们心里保皇，几乎无一例外。

===Original===

Just thing what their feelings must be whenever they look across the river and gaze on that gorgeous palace over there, whence their anointed King has been driven by petty foreign politicians who scarcely knew where Hungary was situated on the map.”
Before Rosemary could pursue the subject she caught sight of her husband forging his way towards her between the crowded tables of the terrace.
“Naniescu is down below,” Jasper said as soon as he had reached his wife’s side. “I told him you were up here, and he said he wished to pay his respects. He is talking to some friends for the moment, but he will be here directly.”
“Then Ruth and I had better run,” Captain Payson said lightly. “He and I are always on the verge of a quarrel when we meet.”
He and his wife rose and took their leave; there was much talking and laughing and promises to meet on the morrow. When they had gone Rosemary said to her husband: “I would rather not have seen General Naniescu tonight. I am very tired, and honestly I don’t feel at my best.”
“I am so sorry,” Jasper replied at once, full of contrition. “I did my utmost to put him off. I knew, of course, that you must be very tired. But he leaves Budapest early tomorrow morning. He is going to Cluj﻿—”
“Cluj?” she asked, puzzled, then laughed lightly. “Oh, ah!” she went on. “I always forget that dear old Kolozsvár is Cluj now.”
“Naniescu was anxious to see that our passports were quite in order, and as this is important﻿—”
“You did quite right, dear,” Rosemary rejoined gently, “as you always do. I don’t suppose the general will keep us long﻿—though he is a terrible talker,” she added with a sigh.
A moment or two later the handsome Romanian came up to Rosemary’s table.
“Ah, dear lady,” he said, and with habitual elaborate gesture he took her hand and raised it to his lips. “What a joy it is to see that you have fulfilled your promise and that you are here at last.”
He sat down at the table but declined Jasper’s offer of a liqueur or cup of coffee.
“I am only here for a moment,” he said, “overwhelmed with work and with engagements. But I thought it would save you trouble if I just looked at your passports and saw that they were entirely in order.”
“That is more than kind,” Rosemary rejoined, whilst Jasper went immediately to fetch the passports. For a moment or two Rosemary remained silent and absorbed.

===Chinese===

想想看，他们每次望过河去，眺望对岸那座辉煌的王宫，心里是什么滋味——他们受膏的国王，就被一班在地图上几乎找不着匈牙利的小小外国政客从那里赶了出来。”
罗斯玛丽还没来得及接着谈下去，就看见丈夫在露台挤挤挨挨的桌间朝她走来。
“南尼斯库在下面，”贾斯珀一到妻子身边便说，“我告诉他你在这儿，他说想过来致敬。他这会儿正同几个朋友说话，马上就上来。”
“那我和鲁丝还是溜之大吉吧，”佩森上尉轻松地说，“我们俩一见面就总要吵到打架边缘。”
他和妻子起身告辞；说笑一阵，约定明天再会。他们走后，罗斯玛丽对丈夫说：“今晚我倒不想见南尼斯库将军。我太累了，说实话，状态也不佳。”
“真对不起，”贾斯珀立刻答道，满心歉疚，“我竭力推辞来着。我当然知道你一定很累。可他明天一早就要离开布达佩斯。他要去克卢日——”
“克卢日？”她问，一时没转过弯，随即轻轻笑了。“哦，啊！”她接着说，“我老是忘记，亲爱的老克洛兹瓦尔如今叫克卢日了。”
“南尼斯库急着查看我们的护照是否完全妥帖，既然这事要紧——”
“你做得完全对，亲爱的，”罗斯玛丽温婉地应道，“你一向做得对。我想将军不会耽搁我们多久——不过他可是个要命的健谈家，”她叹口气补上一句。
一两分钟后，那位英俊的罗马尼亚人来到了罗斯玛丽的桌前。
“啊，亲爱的夫人，”他说着，用他惯常的那套繁复手势托起她的手，举到唇边，“您兑现了诺言、终于到了这里，这多叫人欢喜。”
他在桌旁坐下，却谢绝了贾斯珀敬的利口酒和咖啡。
“我只待一小会儿，”他说，“公务和应酬把我忙得团团转。不过我想，干脆由我来看一看你们的护照、确认万事妥帖，可以替你们省点事。”
“那真是再好不过，”罗斯玛丽答道；贾斯珀随即去取护照。有一两分钟，罗斯玛丽默默出神。

===Original===

An indefinable something had caused her to shrink when she felt General Naniescu’s full lips upon her hand﻿—something hostile and portentous. The next moment this feeling had gone, and she was ready to chide herself for it. Naniescu was earnest, persuasive, elaborately polite in manner and florid of speech just as he had been in London, when first he put his proposal before her, and certainly there was not a hint of anything sinister about him.
“I am looking forward to my visit to Transylvania,” Rosemary said quite gaily.
“You will find every official there ready to welcome you, dear lady,” Naniescu assured her. “You need only express a wish, to find it met in every possible way. And if you should do me the honour of requiring my personal services, needless to say that I should fly immediately to obey your commands.”
Rosemary shrugged her pretty shoulders.
“I don’t anticipate any such call upon your valuable time,” she said coolly.
“Ah, one never knows. You, dear lady, are going amongst a strange people,” he added with a sigh. “People whose supposed grievances have made bitter.”
“I have old friends in Transylvania, and will feel as safe with them as I should in my flat in London.”
“You will stay the whole time with the Imreys?” the general asked.
“Who told you I was going to stay with them?” she retorted quickly.
“You yourself, dear lady,” he replied, unperturbed, “or did I merely make a shrewd guess? Anyway, on that unforgettable evening at the Albert Hall, when first I had the honour of an introduction to you, I saw you dancing with Mr. Blakeney. The Countess Imrey is his mother’s sister﻿—you told me that you had friends in Transylvania﻿—the inference surely was obvious. I trust I have not offended you,” Naniescu went on in his most mellifluous tone, “by the suggestion.”
“No, no,” Rosemary replied, already vexed with herself for having unwittingly provoked the Romanian into one of those elaborate speeches which irritated her and gave her a vague feeling that malicious irony lurked behind so much blandness. “Mrs. Blakeney was a dear friend of mine; she and I travelled a great deal together, and I stayed more than once with the Imreys, not only at Kis-Imre, but in their beautiful house at Kolozsvár.”

===Chinese===

南尼斯库肥厚的嘴唇落在她手上时，一种说不清的东西使她一缩——某种怀有敌意、带着凶兆的东西。可下一刻这感觉就没了，她倒想为此责备自己。南尼斯库同在伦敦初次向她提出提议时一样：恳切、会说动人、礼数繁缛、辞藻浮艳；他身上当然没有半点阴鸷的影子。
“我盼着这次特兰西瓦尼亚之行呢，”罗斯玛丽说得相当欢快。
“那边的每一位官员都准备欢迎您，亲爱的夫人，”南尼斯库向她保证，“您只需表示一个愿望，就会看到它被千方百计地满足。倘若您肯赏脸用得着我个人效力，那更不必说，我一定飞奔前来听候您的差遣。”
罗斯玛丽耸了耸漂亮的肩膀。
“我料想不会那样劳驾您的宝贵时间，”她冷冷地说。
“啊，这可说不准。您，亲爱的夫人，要去一群生人中间，”他叹口气补了一句，“一群据说满腹冤屈、因而变得刻毒的人。”
“我在特兰西瓦尼亚有老朋友，同他们在一起，会像在伦敦自己的公寓里一样安全。”
“您要全程都住在伊姆雷家？”将军问。
“谁告诉您我要住他们家？”她很快顶了一句。
“您自己呀，亲爱的夫人，”他不动声色地答，“要不就是我猜了个精光？总之，在阿尔伯特音乐厅那个难忘的晚上，我刚有幸被人引见给您时，看见您同布莱克尼先生跳舞。伊姆雷伯爵夫人是他母亲的妹妹——您对我说过您在特兰西瓦尼亚有朋友——这推论还不是明摆着的。但愿我没有因为这点推测而冒犯您，”南尼斯库用他最甜润的嗓音接着说。
“没有，没有，”罗斯玛丽答，已经在生自己的气——怪自己无意中又引得这罗马尼亚人搬出一篇繁缛的演说；这种演说叫她恼，又给她一种隐隐的感觉，仿佛那么多甜言底下藏着恶意的讥诮。“布莱克尼太太是我的一位要好的朋友；我们一同旅行过许多回，我不止一次住过伊姆雷家——不止在小伊姆雷（Kis-Imre），也在他们克洛兹瓦尔那座美丽的宅子里。”

===Original===

“Ah, then,” the general rejoined, “if you know the house at Cluj, you would﻿—in the scarce probable likelihood of your wishing to command my services﻿—know where to find me?”
“What do you mean?”
“I am living in the Imreys’ house now.”
“But﻿—how can that be?” Rosemary retorted, somewhat puzzled, for she knew that in this part of Europe the idea of letting their house to strangers would never occur to proud, wealthy people like the Imreys, as it does so readily to those of their caste in England. But when General Naniescu, with an indifferent shrug, replied dryly: “Oh, the house was a great deal too big for the occupation of a small family. On public grounds we cannot allow the many to suffer for the whims of a few,” Rosemary frowned, no longer puzzled. She felt rather than saw that the Romanian’s dark, mellow eyes rested on her for an instant with a look of quiet mockery. But it was a mere flash. The next moment he was as suave as before, and said with that perfect deference which he had always affected when speaking to her about her work:
“That question, dear lady, will be one which I earnestly hope you will approach with an open mind, and on which your brilliant intellect will, I trust, shed the light of truth.”
Jasper’s return with the passports brought on a fresh train of thought. Naniescu pronounced them to be in perfect order. He added a special note and signature to the visa which had been obtained from the Romanian Consul in London. Rosemary was feeling very tired and longed to go to bed, but Naniescu stayed on, talking desultorily to Jasper about politics and social conditions, all matters which Rosemary did not feel sufficiently alert to discuss. Her thoughts wandered away and she scarcely heard what the two men were saying; she was, in fact, just meditating on a polite-form of abrupt leave-taking when something that Naniescu said arrested her attention.
“My Government,” the Romanian was saying, obviously in reply to a remark from Jasper, “is quite alive to the evil wrought by those pernicious articles which appear from time to time in English and American newspapers﻿ ﻿…”
“Then why doesn’t your censor stop them?” Jasper queried bluntly.
“He would, my dear Lord Tarkington,” Naniescu rejoined blandly, “he would. But those devils are so astute. How they manage to smuggle their articles through the post I for one cannot for the life of me make out.”

===Chinese===

“啊，那么，”将军应道，“既然您认得克卢日那座宅子，万一——仅仅万分之一的可能——您想差遣我，您就知道上哪儿找我喽？”
“这是什么意思？”
“我如今就住在伊姆雷家的宅子里。”
“可是——这怎么可能？”罗斯玛丽反问，颇为不解，因为她知道，在欧洲这一带，像伊姆雷家这样高贵殷实的人家，绝不会想到把宅子租给外人——不像他们同一阶层的英国人，动辄如此。可南尼斯库将军淡淡耸一耸肩，干巴巴地答道：“哦，那座宅子给一户小小的人家住，实在太大。从公益讲，我们不能让多数人为少数人的癖好受苦。”罗斯玛丽皱起了眉头——不再是困惑了。她感觉得到、几乎看得到，罗马尼亚人那双温柔的深色眼睛在她身上停了一瞬，带着静静的嘲弄。可那不过一闪。下一刻他又同先前一样温文，用他每逢谈到她的工作时惯有的那种毕恭毕敬的口吻说：
“这个问题，亲爱的夫人，正是我恳切希望您以开明的胸怀去对待的；但愿您那睿智的头脑，能为它照亮真理之光。”
贾斯珀拿着护照回来，引出了新的话头。南尼斯库宣称护照完全妥帖，又在伦敦罗马尼亚领事签发的那页签证上添了一行特别的批注和签名。罗斯玛丽困极了，只想去睡，南尼斯库却坐着不走，同贾斯珀有一搭没一搭地谈政治、谈社会状况——都是些罗斯玛丽自觉不够清醒、无从讨论的事。她的思绪飘开了，两个男人说什么几乎没进耳朵；她其实正暗自斟酌一种不失礼数的突然告退，南尼斯库的一句话忽然撞住了她的注意力。
“我国政府，”罗马尼亚人正说着，显然是在回答贾斯珀的一句议论，“对英美报刊上时不时出现的那类恶毒文章所造成的祸害，是十分清楚的……”
“那你们的检查官怎么不拦住它们？”贾斯珀直截了当地问。
“他会拦的，塔金顿勋爵，”南尼斯库婉转地应道，“他会拦的。可那些魔鬼精得很。他们到底怎么把文章从邮路里偷运出去的，我打死也想不明白。”

===Original===

“Ah,” Rosemary put in with a smile, as quietly ironical as Naniescu’s had been a while ago, “you still carry on a strict censorship, then? You do not believe in liberty of speech or of the Press.”
“We do, dear lady, indeed we do. But unfortunately the English and American Press are so easily captured by sentimentality. Put a case before them of supposed wrong, however preposterous and palpably false, and they will revel in it, print it with capital headlines, and so capture the imagination of their sentimental, unthinking readers that these will no longer listen to the voice of reason or of truth. We are too proud﻿—or perhaps not clever enough﻿—to combat such barefaced propaganda; a strict censorship may be a crude weapon, but it is the only one at our command. What would you? A man who is attacked defends himself as best as he can.”
“But in this case your weapon is failing you?” Jasper queried in his quiet, incisive way. “Whoever sends those articles to England and America is apparently too clever for you.”
“For the moment﻿—yes,” Naniescu admitted. “But,” he went on more lightly and at last rose to take his leave, “I fear my irresponsible prattle is keeping Lady Tarkington away from the rest she so much needs. Dear lady, pray accept my humble homage, and my earnest wish that your stay in our poor country will afford you all the delight that you anticipate.”
He raised Rosemary’s hand to his lips with the same show of gallantry that marked his every action in her presence. Just before he finally released it he looked up with deep earnestness into her eyes:
“Let me once more assure you, dear lady, that as far as you are concerned every word you write will be transmitted in its entirety and with all possible speed to its destination. All that you need do is to send your articles and letters in a sealed packet undercover to me. I give you my word of honour that you will be satisfied.”

===Chinese===

“啊，”罗斯玛丽含笑插进来，那笑意同方才南尼斯库的一般无二，安静里带着讥诮，“这么说，你们还在搞严格的新闻检查？你们不相信言论自由、出版自由。”
“我们相信，亲爱的夫人，千真万确相信。可惜英美报刊太容易上滥情的当。只要摆一个所谓冤屈的案子给他们——不管多么荒唐、多么一戳即破——他们就津津有味，用大字标题登出来，把他们那些多情而不动脑子的读者的想象统统俘获过去，叫这些人再也听不进理性或真理的声音。我们要么太骄傲——要么不够聪明——斗不过这种厚脸皮的宣传；严格的检查也许是一件粗家伙，可它是我们手边仅有的一件。有什么法子？人挨了打，总得尽力自卫。”
“可这一回，您的家伙不顶用了？”贾斯珀用他那种安静、锋利的口吻问，“把这些文章寄往英国和美国的那位，显然比你们高明。”
“眼下——是的，”南尼斯库承认。“不过，”他放轻了口吻，终于起身告辞，“我怕是我这不着边际的闲扯，耽误了塔金顿勋爵夫人急需的休息。亲爱的夫人，请接受我卑微的敬意，并请相信我诚恳的祝愿：但愿您在我们这个可怜的国家小住，能给您带来您所期盼的全部愉快。”
他举起罗斯玛丽的手吻了吻，还是他在她面前一举一动都摆出的那副骑士气派。就在终于放手之前，他抬起头，深深恳切地望着她的眼睛：
“请容我再一次向您保证，亲爱的夫人：只关您的事，您写下的每一个字，都将一字不易、尽快地送达它的目的地。您只需把文章和信件封好，加封套寄到我手里。我以名誉向您担保，您会满意的。”

## IX / 九
===Original===

Until the moment of her arrival in Cluj, Rosemary had felt nothing but exhilaration whenever she thought of her work and of the good which she proposed to do, thanks to the facilities so magnanimously accorded her by Naniescu. Just for one moment at Budapest, when she first met the handsome Romanian, she had been conscious of a slight feeling of mistrust, an instinctive dislike of the man’s fluent speech and affected gestures. But on reflection she had persuaded herself that this sudden aversion was bound to arise at first contact with those elaborate manners which pass for gallantry in most of the Latin and Slav countries of Europe. The contrast between Naniescu’s exaggerated politeness and Jasper’s unobtrusive consideration had naturally reacted on her sensibilities to the detriment of the Romanian.
Anyway, the sensation soon wore off. She had a very happy time in Budapest. The Paysons were charming; she met several friends, both English and Hungarian, who made her very welcome, and Jasper was, as usual, thoughtfulness itself. The journey across Hungary filled her with that gentle melancholy which those limitless expanses of earth and sky engender in the mind of imaginative people. It was close on harvesting time, and to right and left of the permanent way the great fields of corn stretched out like a sea of ruddy gold to the purple line of the horizon far away. Rosemary loved to gaze on these measureless stretches of country, whereon for mile upon mile nothing showed above the line of waving corn save, at rare intervals, the thatched roof of a tiny homestead peeping from behind a clump of grey-green willow, or an isolated well, with one gaunt arm stretched skywards, around which a herd of young horses had halted for the midday rest. Her eyes followed with loving intensity the winding ribbon of the dust-laden road, bordered by tall, slender poplars or twisted acacia trees, and at intervals the great patches of vivid green amidst the gold, where row upon row of watermelons turned their huge, shimmering carcases to the warmth of the sun.
A faint perfume of heliotrope and mignonette hung in the air, and just for one moment Rosemary’s dreamy gaze caught a glimpse of an exquisite mirage on the far distant horizon﻿—a vision of towers and minarets and of a cool, shady stream painted with fairy brush upon the moisture-laden atmosphere.

===Chinese===

直到抵达克卢日的那一刻为止，罗斯玛丽每逢想起自己的工作、想起多亏纳尼埃斯库慨然给予的种种便利而将要做的善举，心里便只有振奋。只是在布达佩斯，同那位英俊的罗马尼亚人初次见面时，有一瞬间她意识到一丝不信任，一种对这个人口若悬河的谈吐和做作的手势的本能反感。可转念一想，她劝服了自己：初接触欧洲大部分拉丁和斯拉夫国家里被当作殷勤的那一套繁縜礼数，这种突兀的反感是必然要冒头的。纳尼埃斯库夸张的客套同贾斯珀不动声色的体贴两相对照，自然在她敏感的心上起了作用，吃亏的是罗马尼亚人。
无论如何，这种感觉不久便淡去了。她在布达佩斯过得很愉快。佩森夫妇殷勤极了；她还遇到几位英国和匈牙利的朋友，都待她非常热络；贾斯珀呢，一如既往，体贴入微。横贯匈牙利的旅途让她心里充满一种温柔的怅惘——那种无边的天与地，最容易在富于想象的人心里生出这种情绪。快到收割的时节了，铁路两侧，大片的玉米田像一片赤金色的海，一直铺到远处紫色的地平线。罗斯玛丽爱凝望这一望无际的原野，绵延多少英里，除了偶尔从一丛灰绿色柳树后面探出来的小小农舍的草顶，或是一口孤零零的水井——伸出一条瘦长的胳膊直指天空，井边停着一群小马歇陶——在翻滚的麦浪之上，什么也看不见。她的目光深情而专注地追着那条蜿蜒的、尘土弥漫的大路——路旁立着修长的白杨或虬曲的洋槐——还有金色原野间一片片鲜明的绿，那里一行行西瓜把硕大油亮的身子朝着太阳的暖意翻转。
空气里悬浮着一缕天芥菜和木樨草的淡淡香气；有一瞬间，罗斯玛丽蒙眬的目光在极远的地平线上瞥见一幅精妙的海市蜃楼——一片塔楼与尖顶、一道清凉荫翳的溪流，用仙人的画笔绘在饱含水汽的天空上。

===Original===

It was a phantom picture that vanished almost as soon as it appeared, but upon the watcher’s super-sensitive mind it left in its swift transit an impression as of a magic land, a paradise the gates of which had for one brief second been opened by celestial hands, so that she might glimpse the garden of Eden beyond﻿—the world of happiness and of love which for her must ever remain elusive and unattainable.
---
The arrival at Cluj was dreary and disappointing. From Budapest she herself had telegraphed to the hotel she knew so well, and had sent a letter at the same time asking the proprietor to have a hot supper ready for herself and Lord Tarkington. The hotel appeared unfamiliar when she stepped out of the little cab which had brought them from the station. The smiling hall-porter who used to greet Mrs. Blakeney with respectful familiarity on arrival was no longer there; an out-at-elbows, ill-dressed, unwashed porter took charge of their luggage. The proprietor, he said, was not in the house, and he himself was in charge of the place. He bluntly explained in broken German that under the new management no meals except early morning coffee were served in the hotel, the restaurant being now under separate ownership. The lady and gentleman could get something to eat there, no doubt.
It was all very cheerless, and to Rosemary very strange. The gay little town of Kolozsvár, usually so full of animation at this late evening hour, seemed already asleep. The streets were ill-lighted; there was an air of desolation and melancholy about this place. The hotel itself had become stuffy, dirty and ill-lighted. The furniture looked dilapidated, the bed-linen was coarse and the rooms none too clean. Rosemary spent a wretched night; but she was a hardened traveller and had before now put up with worse inconveniences than these. There was always the comforting thought that it was the only night that she would spend in Cluj. The next day Count Imrey’s carriage and horses (he was not allowed to have a motorcar) would be taking her and Jasper to Kis-Imre, where a big welcome and every conceivable luxury awaited them both.
All that she was waiting for now was to see Anna Heves; little Anna, as Peter called her, the pretty, enthusiastic child to whom Rosemary had promised to give a kiss for Peter’s sake.

===Chinese===

这是一幅幻影，几乎一出现便消散了；可它倏忽掠过时，在这位神经过于敏锐的观者心上留下了一种印象，仿佛一片有魔力的土地，一座天堂，天上的手把它的门向她开了一秒钟，好让她瞥见门外的伊甸园——那个幸福与爱的世界，对她来说却必须永远可望而不可即。
---
抵达克卢日是阴郁而令人失望的。还在布达佩斯，她便给她熟得不能再熟的那家旅馆拍了电报，同时发了封信，请老板为她自己和塔金顿勋爵备好一顿热夜宵。可当她从接站的小马车上下来时，旅馆竟显得陌生了。从前那位总带着恭敬而亲热的笑容迎候布莱克尼太太的门房不见了；一个露着肘弯、衣衫褴褛、蓬头垢面的搬运工接管了他们的行李。他说老板不在，如今这地方归他管。他用蹩脚的德语生硬地解释说，换了新东家，旅馆里除了一清早的咖啡，什么饭也不供应了，餐馆如今归了别人。太太和先生要吃东西，去那儿准能吃上。
这一切都凄凄惨惨，对罗斯玛丽来说又非常陌生。克洛兹瓦尔这座快活的小城，往常这个夜深时分正是热闹的时候，此刻却像已经睡熟。街道灯光昏暗；这地方罩着一层荒凉和忧郁。旅馆本身也变得憋闷、肮脏、昏暗。家具显得破旧，床单粗劣，房间也谈不上干净。罗斯玛丽过了一夜苦楚；可她是个走惯了远路的人，比这更糟的不便也都将就过。总还有个宽慰的念头：这是她在克卢日要过的唯一一夜。明天，伊姆雷伯爵的马车和马匹（他不许有汽车）就会载着她同贾斯珀去基什伊姆雷，那儿有一场盛大的欢迎和想得出来的一切享受等着他们俩。
她如今唯一等候的，就是见到安娜·赫韦什；小安娜——彼得这么叫她——那个漂亮、热烈的孩子，罗斯玛丽答应过要替彼得给她一个吻。

===Original===

And in the morning, just as Rosemary had finished putting up her hair and slipped into a dressing-gown preparatory to going in to breakfast with Jasper, there was a knock at the door and Anna came in. Sweet, enthusiastic Anna, who gazed at her shyly with Peter’s eyes and then smiled with Peter’s smile. She would have been pretty, too, but for the unhealthy pallor of her cheeks and the dark rings that circled her eyes﻿—Peter’s eyes!
“I am so ashamed, Miss Fowkes,” Anna murmured shyly; but at once Rosemary broke in, stretching out her arms:
“Aren’t you going to kiss me, Anna?”
With a pathetic little cry the girl ran into Rosemary’s arms, and, her head buried on her friend’s shoulder, she burst into tears. Rosemary let her cry for a moment of two; her own eyes were anything but dry, for with a quick glance she had taken in the girl’s changed appearance, also the shabby clothing, the worn boots, the unmistakable air of grinding poverty and, worse still, of insufficient food. Poor little Anna! If Peter saw her now!
After a few moments the girl raised her head and dabbed away her tears. Rosemary led her to the sofa, made her sit down beside her, and took both her thin little hands in hers.
“To begin with you must not call me Miss Fowkes, Anna,” she said. “I was always Rosemary, wasn’t I?”
Anna nodded, and a wan little smile struggled round her lips.
“And, you know, I am married now,” Rosemary went on. “Hadn’t you heard?”
Anna shook her head. She could not yet trust herself to speak.
“Of course,” Rosemary said gaily, “how stupid of me. Jasper and I were married very quietly in London, and we are not people of such importance that your Hungarian papers would chronicle the fact. My husband is Lord Tarkington, the best and kindest of men. I’ll tell him presently that you are here. He would love to see you.”
“No, no, Rosemary dear!” Anna broke in quickly, “don’t tell Lord Tarkington that I am here. I﻿—I never see strangers now. You see, I have no decent clothes, and﻿—”
“Jasper would look at your sweet little face, Anna, and never notice your clothes. And you are not going to call my husband a stranger, are you?”
Then, as Anna was silent, and with head bent appeared to be staring into nothingness, Rosemary continued lightly, even though her heart felt heavy at sight of the havoc wrought in this young thing by miseries at which she could still only guess.

===Chinese===

早晨，罗斯玛丽刚盘好头发、披上晨衣准备过去同贾斯珀用早餐，门上响起敲击声，安娜进来了。甜美而热烈的安娜，用彼得的眼睛羞怯地望着她，又用彼得的微笑笑了。若不是两颊病态的苍白和围着眼圈的黑影——彼得的眼睛！——她本来也会是漂亮的。
“我真是不好意思，福克斯小姐，”安娜羞怯地低语；罗斯玛丽立刻打断她，张开了双臂：
“你不打算吻我吗，安娜？”
姑娘一声令人心酸的小小喊叫，扑进罗斯玛丽的怀里，把头埋在朋友的肩上，放声哭了。罗斯玛丽由她哭了一两分钟；她自己的眼睛也绝算不得干，因为她一眼便看尽了姑娘容颜的改变，还有那寒酸的衣服、磨旧的靴子、那种错不了的赤贫气息，更糟的是吃不饱的迹象。可怜的小安娜！彼得要是现在看见她！
过了一会儿，姑娘抬起头，把眼泪拭去。罗斯玛丽把她领到沙发上，让她挨着自己坐下，把两只瘦削的小手都握在自己手里。
“第一，你别再叫我福克斯小姐，安娜，”她说。“我一直都是罗斯玛丽，不是吗？”
安娜点点头，一丝苍白的小小的笑容在她唇边挣扎着浮起。
“还有，你知道，我现在结婚了，”罗斯玛丽说下去。“你没听说吗？”
安娜摇摇头。她还不放心开口。
“当然，”罗斯玛丽快活地说，“我真傻。贾斯珀和我在伦敦悄悄结的婚，我们又不是什么要人，你们的匈牙利报纸才不会登这条消息呢。我丈夫是塔金顿勋爵，最好最厚道的人。我这就去告诉他你来了。他一定乐意见你。”
“不，不，亲爱的罗斯玛丽！”安娜急忙插嘴，“千万别告诉塔金顿勋爵我在这儿。我——我如今谁也不见了。你知道，我没有一件像样的衣服，而且——”
“贾斯珀会看你这张甜美的小脸，安娜，绝不会看你的衣服。再说，你总不会把我丈夫叫外人吧，会不会？”
见安娜不作声，低着头像是凝望虚空，罗斯玛丽便继续轻快地说下去——尽管眼见这年轻造物被她此时还只能猜测的种种苦难精蹋成这样，她心头沉重。

===Original===

“By the way, little ’un,” she said, “I don’t yet know what you are doing in Kolozsvár﻿—or Cluj﻿—tiresome name, I never can remember it! Your cousin, Peter Blakeney, told me I should find you here, and that he had written to tell you I should be at the Pannonia today; but that is all I know. Where is your mother?”
“She is still in Ujlak, of course,” the girl replied more calmly, “looking after the place as best she can. But, of course, it is very hard and very, very difficult. They have taken away so much of the land, some of the best pasture, over twelve hundred acres; mother has only about two hundred left. There is not enough for the horses’ feed. Mother had to have ten brood mares destroyed this spring. It was no use trying to keep them, and she could not bring herself to sell them. Imagine mother having her mares killed! It would have broken her heart, only she has had so much to endure lately she﻿—”
Once more the girl broke down; a lump in her throat choked the bitter words. Rosemary frowned.
“But, then, why are you not at home with your mother, Anna?” she asked.
“I earn a little money here, and Marie is at home. She is younger than I, you remember, and she was always mother’s favourite.”
“How do you earn money, Anna? At what?”
Anna hesitated for a moment. She looked up and saw Rosemary’s eyes fixed questioningly upon her, and those eyes were so full of kindness that the girl’s reticence, even her bitterness, melted under the warmth of that gaze.
“I help in the shop of Balog, the grocer,” she replied simply.
“Balog, the grocer? You?”
The cry of surprise, almost of horror, had come involuntarily to Rosemary’s lips. She thought of Mrs. Blakeney, the exquisite grande dame who, after her marriage to Peter’s father, the eminent scientist, had won her position in English society by her charm, her tact and that air of high breeding which is becoming so obsolete these days. She thought of Peter himself, who had inherited so much of his mother’s charm and all her high-souled notions of noblesse oblige, of what was due to birth and descent. Did Peter know what little Anna was suffering under this new regime brought about by a treaty of peace that was to bring the millennium to all the peoples of Europe? With a sudden impulse Rosemary put her arms once more round the shrinking little figure.
“Anna,” she said earnestly, “I think you are absolutely splendid!

===Chinese===

“对了，小家伙，”她说，“我还不知道你在克洛兹瓦尔——还是克卢日？讨厌的名字，我怎么也记不住！——做什么呢。你表哥彼得·布莱克尼告诉我，说你在这儿，还说他写过信告诉你我今天到潘诺尼亚旅馆；可我知道的就这些了。你母亲呢？”
“她当然还在乌伊洛克，”姑娘平静了些，答道，“尽力照看着那份家业。可是，当然，太难了，非常非常难。他们夺走了那么多土地，有几块是最好的牧场，一千二百多英亩；妈妈只剩下大约两百英亩了。连马的饲料都不够。今年春天，妈妈不得不叫人弄死了十匹母马。养是养不起的，可她又下不了狠心卖掉。想想吧，妈妈叫人弄死了她的母马！这本来会叫她心碎的，只是她近来要受的事太多了，她——”
姑娘又说不下去了；喉咙里的一个硬块呛住了那些辛酸的话。罗斯玛丽皱起了眉。
“那么，安娜，你为什么不在家陪着母亲呢？”
“我在这儿挣一点钱，玛丽在家里。她比我小，你记得的，她一直是妈妈的宠儿。”
“你怎么挣钱呢，安娜？做什么？”
安娜迟疑了片刻。她抬头正遇上罗斯玛丽询问的目光，那双眼睛盛满了善意，姑娘的缄默、连她的怨懟，都在那目光的温暖下融化了。
“我在杂货商巴洛格的铺子里帮忙。”她简单地答。
“巴洛格的杂货铺？你？”
这声惊讶、近乎骇然的叫喊，是不由自主从罗斯玛丽唇边溜出来的。她想起布莱克尼太太，那位精致的贵妇人——同彼得的父亲、那位著名科学家结婚之后，凭着她的风致、她的分寸和那种如今日渐稀见的名门气派，在英国社交界赢得了地位。她想起彼得本人，他承了母亲那么多的风致，又承了她全部贵族义务的高贵信条——出身高贵的人应尽的本分。彼得可知道，小安娜在这套由一纸据说要给欧洲各民族带来千禧年的和约造成的新制度下受着什么？罗斯玛丽一阵冲动，重新搂住那瑟缩的小小身影。
“安娜，”她恳切地说，“我觉得你实在了不起！”

===Original===

I admire your pluck more than I can say. But surely, surely you could find more congenial work than selling groceries!”
She paused a moment, her active brain at once turning to projects that had little Anna’s welfare for their aim. Little Anna could not go on selling groceries in an obscure Romanian town. It was unthinkable! Surely Peter did not know. And how could Rosemary face him with the news that she had found little Anna selling groceries at Cluj?
Something must be done, and quickly, to alter such an awful state of things. While she remained silent, thinking, and Anna, equally silent, fidgeted with long, thin fingers the tassel of her friend’s dressing-gown, Rosemary became conscious that Jasper was watching her from the doorway of the next room. How long he had been standing there she did not know. She looked at him over Anna’s bent head, and, as usual, she read in his expressive face a divination of her thoughts. It almost seemed as if, with a slight nod of his head, he was actually approving of what she had not yet put into words. Then he stepped back into the other room and quietly closed the door.
“Listen, little one,” Rosemary said eagerly. “I am here at the invitation of the Romanian Government; that is to say, General Naniescu, who, I understand, is military governor of Transylvania, has asked me to come over here and study the conditions, both social and political. I shall be writing several articles for English and American papers, and I simply must have a secretary for my ordinary correspondence, and﻿—”
Anna shook her head.
“I don’t know how to type,” she said rather curtly, “and I can’t do shorthand.”
“Neither of which is necessary,” Rosemary retorted.
Anna looked her straight in the eyes. “You don’t imagine,” she said quietly, “that if your articles revealed even a particle of the truth they would ever be allowed to pass the censor, and if they concealed the truth you would not expect my father’s daughter to associate herself with them.”
“That’s a brave patriotic speech, Anna,” Rosemary rejoined with a triumphant little laugh, “but you need not be the least afraid. My articles will contain the truth, and the censor will have not power over them. I give you my word.”
But Anna was unconvinced.
“Rosemary dear,” she said earnestly, “don’t think me ungrateful or obstinate.

===Chinese===

“你的勇气叫我佩服得说不出话来。可是当然、当然，你总找得着比卖杂货更相宜的活儿吧！”
她停了片刻，那颗活跃的脑子立刻转向一个个以小安娜的福祉为目标的主意。小安娜不能在一座不起眼的罗马尼亚小镇上继续卖杂货。这不可想象！彼得一定不知道。罗斯玛丽又怎么有脸去见他，告诉他自己在克卢日撞见小安娜在卖杂货？
得想办法，赶快，来改变这可怕的境况。她沉默地思量着，安娜也同样沉默，用细长的手指捻弄朋友晨衣的穂子，这时罗斯玛丽觉察到贾斯珀正在隔壁房间的门口望着她。他在那儿站了多久，她不知道。她越过安娜低垂的头看他，同往常一样，从他那张富于表情的脸上读出了对她心事的洞察。他微微点了一下头，几乎像是当真赞许她尚未出口的话。随后他退回另一间屋，轻轻带上了门。
“听着，小家伙，”罗斯玛丽急切地说。“我是应罗马尼亚政府的邀请来的；这就是说，据我所知是特兰西瓦尼亚军政长官的纳尼埃斯库将军，请我过来考察这里的社会和政治状况。我要为英国和美国的报纸写好几篇文章，我简直少不了个秘书，替我料理寻常通信，还有——”
安娜摇头。
“我不会打字，”她相当生硬地说，“速记也不会。”
“这两样都用不着，”罗斯玛丽回敬。
安娜直直地望着她的眼睛。“你总不至于以为，”她平静地说，“你的文章里若露出哪怕一星半点真相，还能过得了检查官那一关；而文章若是隐瞒真相，你也不会指望我父亲的女儿同它们沾边吧。”
“好一篇勇敢的爱国演说，安娜，”罗斯玛丽带着得胜的小笑应道，“可你一点也不必担心。我的文章会包含真相，检查官对它们没有半点权力。我向你担保。”
可安娜不信。
“亲爱的罗斯玛丽，”她恳切地说，“别以为我不领情、别以为我固执。”

===Original===

Just imagine what it would mean to me to give up this awful grinding routine that wearies me at times to such an extent that I go into the cathedral and beg and pray to God that I might soon die and escape from it all. But you know, dear, when one’s country is as unfortunate as ours has become, one must do one’s utmost to help and serve her, mustn’t one?”
“Why, of course,” Rosemary assented, puzzled by the girl’s strange earnestness, the glow of ardent patriotism that all at once emanated from that drooping, slender figure; “but I don’t quite see how you are serving your country by selling groceries in Balog’s shop.”
“No! no! not by that,” Anna went on eagerly. “Oh, I know that I can trust you, Rosemary, and you can’t imagine what a relief it is to me to have someone to talk to. I have not spoken like this to a soul for nearly two years. And sometimes I feel as if I must choke. But one dare not talk to anyone these days, for government spies are everywhere. You never know who will betray you; the concierge of your house, the woman who washes the stairs, or the beggar to whom you give alms. Oh! I could tell you things﻿—However all of us who are suffering unspeakably under our new tyrants are determined that the outside world shall hear the truth, but there is such a strict censorship that one dare not send anything through the post except what is absolutely banal and meaningless.”
The girl paused a moment, her eyes wandered searchingly around the room, rested for an instant first on one door, then on another, as if in fear that those spies whom she so dreaded were lurking behind them, then, satisfied that she was alone with her English friend, whom she knew she could trust, she said abruptly:
“You remember my cousin, Philip Imrey?”
“Of course.”
“He always had a great talent for writing. When he was quite a boy he used to write poetry and little stories. He is only nineteen now; next year he will have to do his military service in the Romanian army, and that is a perfect hell for every Hungarian! Just think, Rosemary, if an Englishman had to serve in the German army! Isn’t it unthinkable? But still, that cannot be helped! We are the vanquished race, and we have to pay the price. But we are determined that the nations of the West shall know the truth! So Philip and I, between us, thought of a plan.

===Chinese===

“你想一想，丢开这套可怕的、磨人的苦役，对我意味着什么——这苦役有时把我磨得那么狠，我走进大教堂，只求上帝让我早点死，好从这一切里逃脱。可是你知道，亲爱的，一个人的祖国变得像我们这样不幸的时候，人就得竭尽全力去帮助她、侍奉她，是不是？”
“那当然，”罗斯玛丽附和，却被姑娘异样的恳切、那从低垂纤瘦的身子里忽然焕发出的炽热爱国之光弄糊涂了；“可我不大明白，你在巴洛格的铺子里卖杂货，怎么就算是报效祖国。”
“不！不！不是靠那个，”安娜急切地说下去。“哦，我知道我可以信得过你，罗斯玛丽，你想不到能找个人说说话，对我是多大的解脱。快两年了，我没这样对一个人开过口。有时候我简直觉得要憋死了。可这些日子谁也不敢跟谁说话，政府的密探到处都是。你永远不知道谁会出卖你：楼下的看门人、擦楼梯的女人、你施舍过的叫花子。哦！我能告诉你的事情——反正，我们这些在新主子底下受着说不出的苦的人，打定了主意要让外面的世界听到真相，可检查森严得很，除了绝对平淡无味的废话，什么也不敢邮寄。”
姑娘停了停，眼睛探询地在屋里逮巡，先在一扇门上、又在另一扇门上停留一瞬，仿佛唯恐她如此惧怕的那些密探正躲在门后；随后，确信屋里只有她信得过的英国朋友，她突兀地说：
“你记得我表哥菲利普·伊姆雷吧？”
“当然。”
“他一向很有写作的天分。还是个孩子的时候，他就写诗、写小故事。如今他才十九岁；明年他就得去罗马尼亚军队里服兵役了，那对每个匈牙利人都是十足的地狱！你想想，罗斯玛丽，要是一个英国人非得去德国军队里当兵！不可想象吧？可这也没法子！我们是战败的种族，只好付这个代价。但我们打定了主意，要让西方各国知道真相！于是菲利普跟我俩人，想出了一个计划。”

===Original===

We thought of it for two years, and it took some time to organise. At last I obtained what I wanted, mother’s consent that I should come to Cluj to earn my living, and a post in Balog’s grocery shop. Balog sends Transylvanian goods regularly to Budapest: mustard, cheese, vegetable seeds; I have to pack them. Now do you understand?”
Rosemary nodded. “Yes, I think I do! Philip writes those articles which appeared in the *Evening Post* and caused such an outburst of sympathy for the Hungarians of Transylvania throughout Great Britain. And you﻿—?” she added, and her eyes full of tenderness and compassion rested with undisguised admiration on the shrinking little figure of Anna Heves.
“He rides over from Kis-Imre,” the girl continued simply, “and brings me the articles which he has written, and I consign them inside the grocery parcels to the firm at Budapest, who, of course, are in entire sympathy with us, and post them on to England. Oh! it is splendid, Rosemary dear,” the girl continued with glowing eyes, “to be able to do all this. Now you see, don’t you? that I could not possibly give it all up.”
“Yes, Anna, I do see that. But you are running terrible risks, little ’un.”
“I know I am, and so does Philip; but you don’t know how happy it makes us. The days when an article of his goes to Budapest is a fête day for us both.
