Jolly chap with a ripping wife﻿—done splendid work in the Air Force during the war.

===Chinese===

她的迟疑只延了一两秒；她指望彼得没有察觉。反正，他问“去布达佩斯了？”时，她这回毫不犹豫地答：“对，布达佩斯。”她还相当欢快地加了一句：“他周末就回来。彼得，你想象不到他不在时我多想他！兴许我就是为这个瘦了，手也发热。”
“兴许吧，”彼得简洁地应。
随后不知怎么，话头冷了；彼得刚站到她身边时她感到的那阵幸福，也从她身上溜走了。她突然觉得冷，虽然晚间这么热，方才她简直喘不上气来。身后不远处，菲利普的嗓音听来欢快而家常，毛罗斯·伊姆雷喉音很重的笑、埃尔莎幸福的小小咯咯声，穿过香甜的晚风荡过来。可怜的罗斯玛丽打了个寒噤。
“我们往前走走，”她问，“还是等等他们？”
“往前走吧，”彼得答；随后又笨拙地、孩子气地补一句：“那还用说！”
两人并肩往前走。罗斯玛丽一时不知说什么，便抛出一句关于板球赛的问话。这一下打开了彼得的话匣子。他一下子抛掉了那种突兀、拘谨的神气，滔滔不绝起来。板球赛准是一场大大的成功。罗斯玛丽不觉得这主意棒吗？什么国际联盟，还是管那玩意儿叫什么来着——且去它的呢！照彼得的意见，要叫人凑到一块儿、彼此了解，没有什么比一场痛痛快快的板球赛或足球赛更好的。叫人互相了解，这是彼得的信条。当然，这边这些外国佬都得学着做像样的运动家。输了不许绷脸。这一点彼得会盯着的。总之，那位老什么什么将军够意思极了。他帮了彼得大忙，把罗马尼亚队拉起来了，还发给全队去霍德梅泽的免费车票，比赛就在那儿打。霍德梅泽在匈牙利境内，老什么什么——就是南尼斯库——说，他宁可让罗马尼亚队上匈牙利去，也不愿匈牙利队上这边来。彼得倒无所谓在哪儿打。这准是件顶呱呱的盛事。他要当罗马尼亚队的队长，佩森当匈牙利队的队长。罗斯玛丽认得佩森吗？快活的家伙，太太也棒极了——战争里在空军干得漂亮。

===Original===

He had something to do with the Military Commission on disarmaments. He was at Budapest now, and Jasper would probably see him while he was there. Payson was coming over to Hódmezö by aeroplane. Wouldn’t that create a sensation. There was a splendid landing ground quite close to Hódmezö fortunately. Payson’s wife was coming with him. She was so keen on flying. Ripping couple, they were! Didn’t Rosemary think so? Oh! and Peter had had telegrams of good wishes from no end of people, and a jolly letter from dear old Plum Warner. Did Rosemary know Plum Warner? There was a cricketer if you like! No one like him, in Peter’s opinion. The science of the man! Well, the dagoes should learn that cricket is the finest game in the world! Didn’t Rosemary agree with him?
Rosemary gave monosyllabic replies whenever Peter gave her the chance of putting in a word. She could not help smiling at his enthusiasm, of course. It was so young, so English, so thoroughly, thoroughly fine! But somehow she could not recapture that lovely feeling of security, that sheer joy in having Peter near her, and she kept asking herself whether it was really Peter who had changed﻿—who had become younger, or she who had grown old. In this youthful athlete with his self-assurance and his slang, she vainly sought the wayward, sometimes moody, always captivating Peter, whose tempestuous lovemaking had once swept her off her feet.
At one moment she tried to lead the conversation into a more serious channel: “How do you think Anna is looking?” she asked abruptly.
“A bit peaky.” Peter replied lightly, “poor little mole! When you go back to England,” he went on more gravely, “you ought to take her with you. It would do her all the good in the world. Take her out of herself, I mean.”
“She wouldn’t come,” Rosemary replied earnestly.
“Don’t you think so?”
“Why, Peter,” she retorted, feeling exasperated with him for this air of indifference even where Anna was concerned, “you know Anna would not come. For one thing,” Rosemary added impulsively, “I don’t suppose she would be allowed to.”
“You mean her mother wouldn’t let her?”
“No,” she replied laconically. “I didn’t mean that.”
“Well, then?” he retorted.

===Chinese===

他在裁军军事委员会里有个差事。眼下在布达佩斯，贾斯珀在那儿多半会见到他。佩森要坐飞机来霍德梅泽。那还了得，准轰动。幸好霍德梅泽近旁有一块顶好的降落场。佩森太太也一起来。她迷飞行迷得不行。多好的一对！罗斯玛丽不觉得吗？哦！还有，彼得收到了数不清的贺电，亲爱的老普拉姆·沃纳还来了封痛快信。罗斯玛丽认得普拉姆·沃纳吗？那才叫板球家！照彼得的看法，无人能比。那人的学问！哼，得叫这些外国佬明白，板球是天底下最好的运动！罗斯玛丽不同意吗？
彼得一给她插话的机会，罗斯玛丽便一个字一个字地应着。当然，他的热劲叫她忍不住微笑。多么年轻，多么英国，多么彻头彻尾的正派！可不知怎么，她再也找不回那种安稳的甜滋味、那种彼得在身边的纯粹的欢喜了；她不住地问自己：到底是彼得真的变了——变得年轻了，还是她自己老了。在这个满口俚语、自信满满的年轻运动员身上，她徒劳地寻找那个任性、时而抑郁、却永远迷人的彼得——他那暴风骤雨的求爱，曾一度把她整个卷走。
有一刻她想把谈话引上更正经的轨道：“你看安娜气色怎么样？”她突然问。
“有点憔悴，”彼得随口答，“可怜的小鼹鼠！你们回英国的时候，”他更正经地说下去，“该把她带去。对她只有天大的好处。我是说，好把她从她自己心里带出来。”
“她不会去的，”罗斯玛丽诚恳地答。
“你不这样想？”
“嗳，彼得，”她回道，为他这副连安娜的事都漫不经心的样子恼了，“你知道安娜不会去的。再说了，”罗斯玛丽冲动地加一句，“我想人家也不会放她去。”
“你是说她母亲不肯？”
“不，”她简洁地答，“我不是那意思。”
“那是什么？”他顶回来。

===Original===

Then, as Rosemary, shocked, angry, remained silent, holding her lips tightly pressed together, almost as if she were afraid that words would slip out against her will, Peter shrugged his broad shoulders and rejoined flippantly:
“Oh, I suppose you mean old What’s-his-name﻿—Naniescu﻿—and all that rubbish. I don’t think he would worry much. He has been a brick, letting Anna and Philip out like that. I expect he would just as soon see them both out of the country as not. Jolly good thing it would be for both of them! They would learn some sense, the monkeys!”
He paused and looked round at Rosemary. Then, as she seemed to persist in her silence, he insisted:
“Don’t you agree with me?”
“Perhaps,” she replied, with a weary sigh.
“Anyway, you’ll think it over, won’t you?” Peter went on. “I am sure you could fix it up with old Naniescu. He admires you tremendously, you know.”
It was all wrong, all wrong. Peter used to be so fond of little Anna. “Give her a kiss for me,” were almost the last words he had spoken to Rosemary on the day of her wedding. His own affairs evidently pushed every other consideration into the remotest corner of his brain; and cricket matches were apparently of more importance than the danger which threatened Anna and Philip. Nor had Rosemary any longer the desire to break her promise to Jasper. She no longer wished to speak to Peter about Anna and Philip, or about the horrible alternative which Naniescu had put before her. Peter﻿—this Peter﻿—would not understand. Jasper had not understood either﻿—but he had misunderstood in a different way. Rosemary realised how right he had been to extract that promise from her. Was not Jasper always right? And was it intuition that had prompted him, after all, rather than an attack of jealousy of which Rosemary, in her heart, had been so ready to accuse him?
Suddenly she felt a longing to get away from Peter, from this Peter whom she neither knew nor trusted. “I’ll go in now, I think,” she said abruptly; “the dew is rising, and my shoes are very thin.”
And she started to walk more quickly. Slowly the shades of evening had been drawing in. Rosemary had not noticed before how dark it was getting.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽又惊又恼，不作声，双唇紧紧抿住，几乎像是怕话会违拗着溜出来；彼得耸耸宽阔的肩膀，轻浮地应道：
“哦，我猜你是说老什么什么——南尼斯库——那一套啰嗦。我看他才不操心呢。放安娜和菲利普出来，他够意思了。依我看，巴不得他们俩出国才好呢。对两个人都是天大的好事！也好学点见识，这两个猴崽子！”
他顿住，回头看看罗斯玛丽。见她似乎执意沉默，他又追问：
“你不同意我的话？”
“也许吧，”她叹了口气，倦倦地答。
“反正你会想想的，对不对？”彼得接着说，“我敢说你能同老南尼斯库办妥的。他佩服你佩服得不得了，你知道。”
全都不对了，全都不对了。彼得从前多疼小安娜。“替我吻她一下”——那几乎是他婚礼那天对罗斯玛丽说的最后几句话。他自己的事，显然把别的念头脑后挤到最偏僻的角落去了；板球赛显然比威胁着安娜和菲利普的危险更要紧。罗斯玛丽也不想毁对贾斯珀的承诺了。她不再想同彼得谈安娜和菲利普，谈南尼斯库摆在她面前那个可怕的抉择。彼得——现在这个彼得——不会懂的。贾斯珀也没懂——不过他是以另一种方式不懂。罗斯玛丽这才明白，他逼她许下那个诺言是多么正确。贾斯珀不是永远正确吗？而促使他那样做的，到底是不是直觉，而不是她心里那么乐于指控他的妒忌病发作呢？
突然她渴望离开彼得，离开这个她既不认识、也不能信任的彼得。“我想进去了，”她突然说；“露水起来了，我的鞋很薄。”
她加快了脚步。暮色缓缓围拢过来。罗斯玛丽先前没留意天已经这么黑了。

===Original===

The line of shrubbery behind the perennial border was like a solid wall; and on the other side of the path the stretch of lawn, with its great clumps of pampas grass and specimen trees, became merged in the gathering shadows. Beyond the lawn glimmered the lights of the château, and the veranda in front of the drawing-room was like a great patch of golden light, broken by the long, straight lines of its supporting columns. There was no moon, only an infinity of stars; and in the flower border the riot of colour had faded into the gloom, leaving just the white flowers﻿—the nicotiana, the Madonna lilies, a few violas﻿—to break the even mantle spread by the night.
From the direction of the château there came a loud call of “Hallo!” to which Peter gave a lusty response. A voice shouted: “We are going in!”
“Right-o!” Peter responded. “We’ll come in too!”
Then suddenly he gave a bound, and in an instant had leaped the border and disappeared in the shrubbery beyond. Rosemary, taken completely by surprise, had come to a halt. From the shrubbery there came a loud cry of terror, then a swearword from Peter, and finally a string of ejaculations, all in Hungarian, and of distressful appeals for mercy in the name of all the saints in the calendar. The next moment Peter’s white flannels glimmered through the foliage, and a second or two later he reappeared lower down, coming up the path and half dragging, half pushing in front of him a huddled-up mass, scantily clothed in ragged shirt and trousers, and crowned with a broad-brimmed hat, from beneath which came a succession of dismal howls.
“What is it?” Rosemary cried.
“That’s what I want to know,” was Peter’s reply. “I caught sight of this blighter sneaking in the shrubbery, and got him by the ear, which he does not seem to like, eh, my friend?”
He gave the ear which he held between his fingers another tweak, and in response drew a howl from his victim, fit to wake the seven sleepers.
“Mercy, gracious lord! Mercy on a poor man! I was not doing anything wrong; I swear by holy Joseph I was not doing anything wrong!”
The creature, whoever he was, succeeded in wriggling himself free of Peter’s unpleasant hold. At once he turned to flee, but Peter caught him by the shoulder, and proceeded this time to administer something more severe in the way of punishment.
“Leave the man alone, Peter,” Rosemary cried indignantly.

===Chinese===

宿根花境后面那排灌木丛像一堵实墙；小径另一侧的草坪——带着大丛大丛的蒲苇和孤植的树——渐渐没进聚拢来的暗影里。草坪那边，庄园的灯火微微闪烁；客厅前的游廊像一大片金色的光，被廊柱长而直的影子割破。没有月亮，只有一天星斗；花境里那片喧艳已褪入幽暗，只剩白色的花——烟草花、圣母百合、几株堇菜——打破夜撒下的均匀的幕。
庄园那头传来一声响亮的“喂！”，彼得中气十足地应了一声。有个声音喊：“我们进去啦！”
“好嘞！”彼得应道，“我们也进来！”
突然他一个纵身，一眨眼跳过花境，没进那头的灌木丛。罗斯玛丽猝不及防，站住了。灌木丛里传出一声恐怖的大叫，接着是彼得的一句咒骂，最后是一串匈牙利语的惊叹，外加一连串奉历书上全体圣徒之名讨饶的凄惨哀告。下一刻，彼得的白色法兰绒在树叶间一闪，一两秒后他从下头重新露面，顺着小径走上来，身前半拖半推着一团蜷缩的东西——破衬衫破裤子勉强遮着身子，头顶一顶宽边帽，帽子底下一声接一声发出凄惨的嚎叫。
“怎么回事？”罗斯玛丽喊。
“我也正想知道，”彼得答，“我瞧见这家伙在灌木丛里鬼鬼祟祟，就揪住了他耳朵。他好像不乐意，嗯，我的朋友？”
他把捏在指间的耳朵又拧了一把，换来得他受害者一声能把七个沉睡者吵醒的嚎叫。
“饶命，仁慈的老爷！饶一个可怜人！我没干坏事；我凭圣约瑟起誓，我没干坏事！”
那家伙——不管他是谁——挣脱了彼得那难受的钳制。他转身就逃，可彼得一把揪住他肩膀，这一回动手给的惩罚更重了些。
“放开他，彼得，”罗斯玛丽愤然喊。

===Original===

“You have no right to ill-use him like that!”
“Oh, haven’t I? We’ll soon see about that!” Peter retorted roughly. “Now then, my friend,” he went on, speaking in Hungarian to the bundle of rags that had collapsed at his feet, “listen to me. You have tasted the weight of my boot on your spine, so you know pretty well what you can expect if you don’t tell me at once what you are doing at this hour of the night in the gracious count’s garden?”
The man, however, seemed unable to speak for the moment; loud hiccups shook his tall, spare frame. He held his two hands against the base of his desperate contortions in a vain attempt to get his right shoulder out of Peter’s grip.
“Peter,” Rosemary cried again, “let the poor wretch go. You must! Or I shall hate you.”
But Peter only retorted harshly: “If you weren’t here, Rosemary, I’d thrash the vermin to within an inch of his life. Now then,” he commanded, “stop that howling. What were you doing in their shrubbery?”
“I only wanted to speak with the gracious countess,” the man contrived to murmur at last, through the hiccups that still seemed to choke the words in his throat. “I have a message for her!”
“That’s why I caught you with this in your belt, eh?” Peter queried sternly, and drew something out of his pocket, which Rosemary could not see; he showed it to the man who promptly made a fresh appeal to the saints.
“The roads are not safe for poor gipsies, gracious lord. And I had the message﻿—”
“Who gave you a message for the gracious countess?” Rosemary asked him gently.
“I﻿—I don’t know, gracious lady. A fine gentleman on a horse called to me when I was gathering wood over by the forest of Normafa. He gave me a letter. ‘Take it,’ he said, ‘to the gracious countess over at Kis-Imre, but do not give it into any hands but hers, and only give it to her when she is alone.’ ”
“Where is the letter?”
“It is here, gracious lady,” the man replied and fumbling with the belt that held his ragged trousers round his waist, he drew from underneath it a oiled and crumpled rag that effectively looked like a letter in a sealed envelope. Peter would have snatched it out of his hand, but Rosemary interposed.
“Peter,” she said gravely, and stretched a protecting arm over the gipsy’s hand, “the man was told not to give it in any hand but Elza’s!”
“The man is a liar,” Peter riposted harshly.

===Chinese===

“你没有权利这样虐待他！”
“哦，我没有吗？咱们马上见分晓！”彼得粗声回道。“好了，我的朋友，”他接着用匈牙利语对瘫在他脚下的那堆破衣烂裳说，“听我说。你尝过我靴子的分量了，所以你心里很有数：不马上招出这个时辰你到仁慈伯爵的园子里来干什么，你还能指望什么。”
那人一时似乎说不出话；响亮的打嗝摇着他瘦长的骨架。他两只手死死抵住那拼命扭动的地方，白费力气想把右肩从彼得手里挣出来。
“彼得，”罗斯玛丽又喊，“放了这可怜虫。你必须放！不然我要恨你了。”
彼得只粗声回道：“要不是你在这儿，罗斯玛丽，我非把这混虫抽个半死不可。好了，”他命令道，“别嚎了。你在他们家灌木丛里干什么来着？”
“我不过想同仁慈的伯爵夫人说句话，”那人终于从还哽在喉咙里的话中挤出一句。“我给她带了口信！”
“所以我才在你腰带上搜出这个来的，嗯？”彼得厉声说，从口袋里掏出一样罗斯玛丽看不见的东西给他看；那人当即又向众圣徒告起饶来。
“路上不太平，可怜的吉普赛人不安全，仁慈的老爷。可我得了这口信——”
“谁托你给仁慈的伯爵夫人带口信？”罗斯玛丽温和地问他。
“我——我不知道，仁慈的夫人。一位骑马的好模样的先生叫住我，那时我在诺尔马法森林边上拾柴。他给了我一封信。‘送给，’他说，‘小伊姆雷那边的仁慈伯爵夫人，可不许交到旁人手里，只能交她本人，而且要等她一个人的时候才交。’”
“信在哪儿？”
“在这儿，仁慈的夫人，”那人答道，摸索着系住破裤子的腰带，从底下抽出一片油渍斑斑、皱皱巴巴、确实像一封装在封套里的信的东西。彼得想从他手里夺，罗斯玛丽拦住了。
“彼得，”她正色说，伸手护住吉普赛人的手，“人家吩咐过，除了埃尔莎本人的手，不许交到任何手里！”
“这家伙是个骗子，”彼得狠狠回敬。

===Original===

Just then Philip’s voice reached them from across the lawn.
“What are you two doing over there?”
“Philip, is your mother with you?” Rosemary shouted in response.
“Yes! We are just going in.”
“Ask her to wait a moment then.”
“What has happened?” Elza called.
“Nothing, darling,” Rosemary replied. “Send the others in and wait for me, will you?” Then she turned to the gipsy, and said kindly: “Walk beside me, and don’t try to run away; the gracious lord will not hurt you if you walk quietly beside me.”
And so the three of them walked across the lawn toward the château, Rosemary in front, and beside her the gipsy, whose long thin hands almost swept the grass as he walked with bent knees and arched back, throwing from time to time anxious glances behind him. But Peter was lagging behind.
When they were close to the château, they saw Elza coming down the veranda steps. Rosemary ordered the gipsy to wait, and ran to meet Elza; in a few words she told her what had occurred. Elza then came across the gravel path, and said to the gipsy: “I am the Countess Imrey. You may give me the letter!”
The man’s back became more curved than ever; he nearly touched the ground with his forehead. In the darkness Rosemary seemed to see his long, thin body curling itself up almost into a ball.
“I was told,” he murmured meekly, “to give the letter into the hands of the gracious countess only when she was alone.”
Instinctively Rosemary turned to look for Peter. To her surprise she saw him just above her, going up the veranda steps. He had his hands in the pockets of his trousers, and he was whistling a tune.
The gipsy whom he had so maltreated a little while ago no longer seemed to interest him. Rosemary called to him rather impatiently:
“Peter!”
He paused and looked down at her. “Hallo!” he said coolly.
“Do you think it is all right for Elza to talk with this man alone?”
Peter shrugged his shoulders. “Why not?” he said, with a laugh.
Then he called out to Elza:
“I say, Aunt Elza, if the wretch should try to kiss you, sing out, won’t you?”
Elza laughed good-humouredly.
“Of course I am not afraid,” she said. “And I do want to know about this mysterious letter.”
Rosemary would have liked to argue the point. She could not understand how it was that Peter took the matter so lightly all of a sudden.

===Chinese===

恰在此时，菲利普的声音从草坪那头传到他们耳中。
“你们俩在那边干什么呢？”
“菲利普，你母亲跟你们在一起吗？”罗斯玛丽扬声应道。
“在！我们正进去呢。”
“那就请她等一等。”
“出什么事了？”埃尔莎喊。
“没什么，宝贝，”罗斯玛丽答，“叫他们先进去，等等我，行吗？”然后她转向吉普赛人，和气地说：“走在我旁边，别想跑；你安安静静走在我旁边，仁慈的老爷就不会伤你。”
于是三人穿过草坪朝庄园走去，罗斯玛丽在前，吉普赛人挨着她——他弯着膝、弓着背，细长的手几乎扫着草，不时回头抛去不安的一瞥。彼得却落在后头。
离庄园近了，他们看见埃尔莎正走下游廊的台阶。罗斯玛丽吩咐吉普赛人等着，跑过去迎埃尔莎；三言两语把事情讲了。埃尔莎于是走过碎石路来，对吉普赛人说：“我就是伊姆雷伯爵夫人。你可以把信给我了！”
那人的背躬得比先前更弯，额头几乎触地。昏暗里罗斯玛丽仿佛看见他那瘦长的身子几乎蜷成了一个球。
“人家吩咐我，”他卑顺地喃喃道，“信只能等仁慈的伯爵夫人一个人的时候，交到她本人手里。”
罗斯玛丽本能地转身找彼得。令她吃惊的是，她看见他就在她上方，正走上游廊的台阶。他双手插在裤袋里，嘴里吹着曲子。
方才被他那样虐待过的吉普赛人，似乎已引不起他的兴趣。罗斯玛丽颇不耐烦地喊他：
“彼得！”
他停下，低头看她。“喂！”他冷冷地说。
“埃尔莎同这个人单独说话，你看不要紧吗？”
彼得耸耸肩。“有什么要紧？”他笑着说。
随后他朝埃尔莎喊：
“我说，埃尔莎姨，那坏蛋要是想亲你，你就喊一声，好不好？”
埃尔莎好脾气地笑了。
“我当然不怕，”她说，“这封神秘的信，我倒真想知道是怎么回事。”
罗斯玛丽本想争一争。她不明白，彼得怎么突然把这事看得这样轻。

===Original===

However, as Elza was playfully pushing her out of the way, whilst Peter calmly continued to stroll up the stairs, she only said with a final note of earnestness: “I shall be quite close, Elza. You have only to call, you know.”
“I know, I know,” Elza rejoined, still laughing. “You don’t suppose that I am frightened of a gipsy, do you?”
She waited a moment or two until Rosemary was out of sight, then she turned back to the man, and said:
“I am alone now. You may give me the letter.”

===Chinese===

可是埃尔莎正笑着把她往旁边推，彼得也径自缓步上台阶去了，她只好带着最后的诚恳说：“我就在近旁，埃尔莎。你只消叫一声，你知道的。”
“知道，知道，”埃尔莎应道，还在笑，“你总不至于以为我怕一个吉普赛人吧？”
她等了一两分钟，等罗斯玛丽走得看不见了，才转回身对那人说：
“现在只剩我一个人了。你可以把信给我了。”

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

Rosemary went slowly up the veranda steps. She did not feel that it would be loyal to pry into Elza’s secrets, but at the same time she wanted to remain well within call. From where she was she could see Peter’s broad shoulders blocking the French window which gave on the drawing-room. From somewhere in the house, both above and below stairs, came the sound of laughter and song.
A moment or two later she heard Elza’s footsteps behind her on the gravel walk, and presently Elza was there, going up the veranda steps beside Rosemary. She did not say a word, and Rosemary asked no questions. She could see that Elza was preoccupied. She also noticed that the letter﻿—or whatever it was﻿—was not in Elza’s hands.
Peter stood aside to allow the two ladies to step into the drawing-room. He asked no questions either, and Elza did not volunteer any information. It seemed as if the incident of the mysterious gipsy had never been. Later on Peter sat down at the piano and played a csàrdàs, for Philip and Anna to dance. They were beautiful dancers, both of them, and it was a pleasure to watch them swaying and bending to the syncopated cadences of the beautiful Hungarian music. Peter, too, had evidently that music in the blood. Rosemary had no idea he could play it so well. He seemed just as excited as the dancers, and accelerated the movements of the csàrdàs until little Anna called for mercy, and even Philip seemed ready to give in. For the time being Rosemary forgot her troubles in the joy of seeing those two enjoying themselves, and the delight of listening to Peter. What a pity, she thought, as she had often done, that he should waste all the poetry, the talent that was in him, and only devote his mind to cricket. She drew close up to the piano, to watch his slender fingers flying over the keys, and as she did so, her glance at one moment wandered to the small whatnot in the corner by the piano. There, in the midst of a miscellaneous collection of cigarette boxes, ashtrays, matchboxes, lay a small automatic.
Peter caught her eye, which at the moment expressed a mute inquiry. He shrugged his shoulders and smiled. He had a cigarette in a long holder in the corner of his mouth, but he contrived to murmur:
“Yes, the blighter; wasn’t I right to thrash him?”
Rosemary looked across at Elza.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽缓缓走上游廊的台阶。她觉得刺探埃尔莎的秘密有失忠厚，可同时又想留在听得见召唤的地方。从她站的地方，能看见彼得宽阔的肩膀堵在通往客厅的落地窗前。宅子里不知哪里，楼上楼下都传来笑声和歌声。
过了一两分钟，她听见身后砾石小径上埃尔莎的脚步声，转眼埃尔莎已在身边，同她一道走上台阶。她一言不发，罗斯玛丽也不问。她看得出埃尔莎心事重重。她还注意到，那封信——或不管是什么——已不在埃尔莎手里。
彼得侧身让两位女士走进客厅。他也不问，埃尔莎也不主动说什么。那神秘吉普赛人的插曲，仿佛从未发生过。随后彼得在钢琴前坐下，弹起一支恰尔达什，让菲利普和安娜跳舞。他们俩都是好看的舞者，看他们随那美丽的匈牙利音乐的切分调子摇曳、俯仰，是一种享受。彼得显然也天生流淌着这音乐。罗斯玛丽不知道他能弹得这么好。他同跳舞的人一样兴奋，把恰尔达什的节拍越催越快，直催得小安娜喊饶命，连菲利普也像要告饶了。一时间，罗斯玛丽看着这两个人的欢畅、听着彼得弹奏的快乐，忘掉了自己的烦恼。多可惜呀，她想——她常这么想——他把内里的诗意和才华全都荒废了，心思全放在板球上。她走近钢琴，看他修长的手指在琴键上飞舞；就在这时，她的目光有一瞬移到钢琴旁角落里的小摆设架上。那儿，在烟盒、烟灰缸、火柴盒混杂的一堆中间，躺着一支小小的自动手枪。
彼得接住她的眼色，那眼色此刻是无言的询问。他耸耸肩，笑了。他嘴角叼着长烟嘴上的香烟，却还含糊地咕哝出一句：
“是的，那混蛋；我揍他揍得对不对？”
罗斯玛丽望向埃尔莎。

===Original===

She sat quite placidly, as she always did, close to her husband’s chair, watching her Philip﻿—her soul in her eyes. She was smiling, and now and then she turned to say a word or two to Maurus; but to Rosemary she still looked preoccupied, and once she caught Elza’s large kind eyes fixed upon her with a curious, scrutinizing gaze.
An hour later when Rosemary was in her room and beginning to undress, there was a knock at her door, and Elza came in, with that kindly smile of hers still on her face, but with a troubled look in her eyes.
“May I come in for a moment, darling?” she asked.
Rosemary made her comfortable on the sofa, and sat down beside her. Elza took hold of both her hands and fondled them, stroking them up and down, and she began talking about Philip and Anna, and the dancing and the plans for future parties, and picnics and so on. Rosemary let her prattle on; it was her turn to scrutinise Elza’s face closely. That something was troubling this dear, kind creature was obvious. She was, as it were, gathering her moral forces before she broached something unpleasant that she had come to say. It was no use brusquing the matter, and Rosemary entered into Elza’s plans, discussed the coming dinner-parties, the proposed lists of guests, talked about Anna’s future, and made some remarks about Peter.
This brought the main subject on the tapis.
“Where did you and Peter first see that gipsy?” Elza asked presently.
“He was hiding in the shrubbery,” Rosemary replied, “behind the flower border. I didn’t see him. Peter saw him and pounced upon him, and dragged him out on to the path.”
“Funny he did not just go to the service door and ask for me, wasn’t it?”
“That’s what Peter thought. I am afraid he treated the poor wretch rather roughly.”
“I am sorry he did that,” Elza mused, and thoughtfully stroked Rosemary’s slender fingers between her own. “The man really had a message for me.”
“I know,” Rosemary rejoined; “a letter.”
“No, it wasn’t a letter,” Elza said, and looked Rosemary now straight between the eyes. “You know these gipsies are queer people. They have curious gifts of divination and prophecy. This man﻿—”
She seemed to hesitate, her glance wavered, and once more she started mechanically stroking Rosemary’s hands.
“But the man had a letter for you, Elza dear,” Rosemary insisted. “I saw it in his hand.”

===Chinese===

她照常安静地坐在丈夫的椅子旁，望着她的菲利普——眼睛里盛着她的魂。她微笑着，不时转头同毛罗什说一两句；可在罗斯玛丽眼里，她仍是心事重重；有一次她撞见埃尔莎那双善良的大眼睛正用一种古怪的、审视的目光盯着她。
一个钟头后，罗斯玛丽在自己房里开始更衣，门上响起敲击声，埃尔莎进来了，脸上还挂着那和蔼的笑，眼睛里却有一抹愁容。
“可以进来坐一会儿吗，宝贝？”她问。
罗斯玛丽让她在沙发上坐舒服，自己在她身旁坐下。埃尔莎握住她的双手，来回抚弄着，先谈起菲利普和安娜，谈跳舞，谈今后聚会、野餐种种打算。罗斯玛丽任她絮絮地说；这回轮到她细细端详埃尔莎的脸了。有什么事在烦扰这亲爱的好心人，一望便知。她是在攥起道德的力气，好开口一件她来要说的不愉快的事。急不得，罗斯玛丽便也凑着埃尔莎的计划谈，商量着即将举行的晚宴、拟请的客人名单，谈安娜的将来，又对彼得议论了几句。
这就把正题引上了台面。
“你和彼得最初是在哪儿看见那个吉普赛人的？”埃尔莎此刻问。
“他藏在灌木丛里，”罗斯玛丽答，“花境后头。我没看见他。彼得看见了他，扑上去，把他拖到小路上。”
“奇怪的是他没有径直到杂役门去求见我，是不是？”
“彼得也这么想。只怕他对那可怜虫相当粗暴。”
“那样我就难过了，”埃尔莎沉思着说，若有所思地用手捻抚罗斯玛丽纤细的手指。“那人真是带口信给我的。”
“我知道，”罗斯玛丽应道；“一封信。”
“不，不是信，”埃尔莎说，此刻直直地望进罗斯玛丽的眼睛。“你知道这些吉普赛人是古怪的人。他们有一种占卜和预言的奇异天赋。这个人——”
她似乎迟疑了，目光游移了一下，随即又不自觉地抚摸起罗斯玛丽的手来。
“可那人手里分明拿着一封给您的信，亲爱的埃尔莎，”罗斯玛丽坚持。“我亲眼看见的。”

===Original===

“Oh, that was only a blind; and so was his story about the gentleman on a horse. He told me that he had come all the way from Ujlak to speak with me. Ujlak is where I was born, and my dear brother and Peter’s mother. My sister-in-law lives there still. Anna was born there, and little Marie. It was my father’s home and my grandfather’s before him, and our ancestors’ for many generations. Well, this gipsy came from there.”
“In order to speak with you?”
“So he said.”
“Well, and what did he have to tell you?” Rosemary asked.
“That he had had a vision. My father had appeared before him in a dream, and told him that he must start at once and seek me. He was to tell me that he whom I love best in all the world is in immediate danger of death.”
Rosemary never moved; she was looking straight at Elza. Only when Elza paused, seeming to wait for some word from her, Rosemary said:
“That wretched creature told you that?”
Elza nodded. She went on simply:
“I see by your face, dear, that he told the truth, not only in that, but in what he said was to follow.”
“What was that?”
“He said that the stranger now within our gates knows of this danger, and would confirm what he said. Well, my darling, I only need look at your sweet face to see that that miserable wretch spoke the truth. He was inspired by a dream to come and speak with me. But I would not question him further. Those gipsies often lie, and they will tell you any tale in order to get a few coppers. But I saw your look when I told you what he said, and it is from you that I want the truth. What is the danger that threatens Philip?”
“Elza, darling﻿—” Rosemary murmured.
“I am his mother, you know,” Elza interposed, with her gentle, quiet smile. “I must know. He is all the world to me. And as soon as you knew that something threatened him, you should have told me, my darling.”
Then, as Rosemary was still fighting with herself, alternately praying to God for guidance, and striving to swallow the tears that were choking her, Elza went on quite quietly:
“It is difficult for you, of course,” she said, and patted Rosemary’s cheek like an indulgent mother, “but it would have been better to tell me at first. I have had a very, very happy week since the children came home, but looking back on it now, I don’t think that I was ever quite free from a vague sort of doubt.

===Chinese===

“哦，那不过是幌子；他讲的那个骑马的绅士的故事也是。他告诉我，他是专程从乌伊洛克赶来同我说话的。乌伊洛克是我的出生地，也是我亲爱的哥哥和彼得母亲的出生地。我嫂子如今还住在那儿。安娜生在那儿，小玛丽也是。那是我父亲的家，再往前是我祖父的，我们祖祖辈辈住了多少代。喏，这个吉普赛人就是从那儿来的。”
“专程来同您说话？”
“他是这么说的。”
“那他要告诉您什么呢？”罗斯玛丽问。
“说他得了一个异象。我父亲在梦里显现在他面前，说他必须立刻动身来寻我。他要告诉我：我在世上最爱的那个人，正处在立即丧命的危险里。”
罗斯玛丽一动不动；她直直地望着埃尔莎。直到埃尔莎停下来，像在等她一句话时，罗斯玛丽才说：
“那可怜虫这样告诉您？”
埃尔莎点头，简单地接着说：
“亲爱的人，我从你脸上看得出，他说的是实话——不但那句是，他接下来说的也是。”
“他说了什么？”
“他说，如今就在我们门里的这位客人，知道这危险，会证实他的话。喏，我的宝贝，我只要看看你这张甜美的脸，就知道那可怜虫说的是真话了。是一个梦指引他来同我说话的。可我没有再追问下去。这些吉普赛人常常撒谎，为几个铜子什么故事都编得出来。可我把他的话告诉你时，我看见了你的神色；我要从你这里听真话。威胁着菲利普的危险是什么？”
“埃尔莎，亲爱的——”罗斯玛丽喃喃道。
“我是他的母亲，你知道，”埃尔莎带着她温柔安静的微笑插进来。“我必须知道。他就是我的整个世界。你既然知道有什么威胁着他，早就该告诉我了，我的宝贝。”
见罗斯玛丽还在天人交战，一会儿求上帝指引，一会儿强咽着呛住喉咙的泪水，埃尔莎便十分平静地说下去：
“这对你自然很难，”她说，像一位慈爱的母亲那样拍拍罗斯玛丽的脸颊，“可当初就告诉我更好些。孩子们回家后，我过了一个非常非常幸福的星期；可如今回头一想，我从来不曾真正摆脱过一种隐隐的疑虑。我一直有点心神不定，每逢菲利普吻我，我都忍不住要哭。”

===Original===

I was always a little uneasy, and whenever Philip kissed me, I could not help crying.”
Elza had spoken in a curious, dreamy manner, her round blue eyes fixed somewhere on vacant space. But now she seemed to pull herself together, she looked once more at Rosemary, gave her an encouraging smile, and said in a perfectly quiet, matter-of-fact tone:
“Well, now tell me all about it. Philip’s release and Anna’s is only a temporary one. Is that it?”
Rosemary nodded. She could not trust herself to speak. Elza gave a little gasp, but her voice was still quite steady as she went on questioning Rosemary:
“What is the charge against them?”
“Philip wrote certain newspaper articles,” Rosemary replied, and her voice sounded mechanical, like that of an automaton, “which have appeared in the English and American press. Anna used to send those through in the parcels she packed up in Balog’s shop.”
“I knew about those articles,” Elza rejoined simply. “Everybody in Transylvania knew about them, but I did not guess that Philip had anything to do with them, or Anna. Then,” she went on with a little catch in her throat, “it means a charge of treason against the State?”
“Yes!”
“Military tribunal?”
“Yes.”
“And﻿—if they are found guilty﻿—a sentence of death?”
“No! No! No!” And Rosemary was on her knees with her arms round Elza’s shoulders, her tear-stained face turned up to her, protesting vigorously, strenuously, that which she knew was false. But Elza’s big, round eyes were tearless; she looked a little wildly perhaps, but quite kindly into the beautiful face that expressed such a world of love and sympathy. Then, gently but firmly, she disengaged herself from Rosemary’s arms.
“Well now, my dear,” she asked, very quietly, “all this being so, why did Naniescu let those children come home at all? Why should he postpone their trial, their﻿—their punishment?”
Rosemary’s head fell upon her breast.
“I don’t know,” she murmured.
But Elza put her podgy finger under Rosemary’s chin, and forced her to look up.
“Don’t lie to me, darling,” she pleaded softly; “tell me the truth.”
“I have told you the truth, Elza,” Rosemary protested through her tears.
“Then I must believe you, if you say so. And yet it is all very mysterious. Why should Naniescu wait? Why should he play with those poor children, like a cat does with a mouse? You know, Rosemary darling, what they gipsy said in the end?”

===Chinese===

埃尔莎说话时带着一种古怪的、梦一样的神情，圆圆的蓝眼睛定在半空的某处。可现在她似乎振作起来，又看了罗斯玛丽一眼，给她一个鼓励的微笑，用完全平静的、就事论事的语调说：
“那么，现在把一切都告诉我吧。菲利普和安娜的释放只是暂时的。是不是？”
罗斯玛丽点头。她不敢开口。埃尔莎轻轻抽了口气，可接着发问时嗓音仍然十分平稳：
“对他们的指控是什么？”
“菲利普写过一些报纸文章，”罗斯玛丽答，嗓音听起来机械得像自动人偶，“登在了英美报刊上。安娜常把它们夹在她在巴洛格铺子里打包的包裹里寄出去。”
“那些文章我知道，”埃尔莎简单地应道。“特兰西瓦尼亚人人都知道，只是我没猜到同菲利普有关，也没猜到安娜。那么，”她接着说，喉咙里轻轻一噎，“这就是对国家的叛国指控了？”
“是的！”
“军事法庭？”
“是的。”
“要是判了罪——就是死刑？”
“不！不！不！”罗斯玛丽跪在地上，双臂搂住埃尔莎的肩，泪湿的脸仰向她，激烈地、拼命地否认她明知是假的事。可埃尔莎那双圆圆的大眼睛里没有泪；她也许显得有点狂乱，却十分和善地望着这张流露着无限爱与同情的美丽的脸。随后，她温柔而坚定地从罗斯玛丽的手臂里挣脱出来。
“那么，亲爱的，”她十分安静地问，“既然如此，纳尼埃斯库为什么还放孩子们回家？他为什么推迟审判，推迟他们的——他们的刑罚？”
罗斯玛丽的头垂到胸前。
“我不知道。”她喃喃道。
可埃尔莎把胖胖的手指抵在罗斯玛丽的下巴下，逼她抬起头来。
“别对我撒谎，宝贝，”她轻声恳求；“告诉我真话。”
“我告诉你的就是真话，埃尔莎，”罗斯玛丽含泪抗议。
“你既然这么说，我就只好信你。可这一切还是很蹊跷。纳尼埃斯库为什么要等？为什么他要拿那些可怜的孩子耍着玩，像猫耍老鼠？亲爱的罗斯玛丽，你知道那吉普赛人最后说的话吗？”

===Original===

Rosemary shook her head.
“He said that the stranger within the gates had the power to save my son from death. Have you that power, Rosemary?”
“No! No!” Rosemary protested wildly. “If it were in my power, don’t you think that I would do anything in the world to save Philip and Anna?”
Elza nodded.
“Yes, dear,” she said gently. “Of course I do think it; but when the gipsy said that, I could not help feeling hopeful, for he was right in everything else he said﻿—”
Then suddenly she took Rosemary’s face between her two hands, and she gazed into her eyes with a look of almost fierce intensity in her own, as if she would wrest a secret from the depths of the younger woman’s soul.
“Swear to me, Rosemary,” she said, and her gentle voice sounded raucous and harsh, “swear to me that there is nothing in the world that you can do to save Philip!”
And Rosemary, returning her gaze, replied steadily:
“I swear to you that it is not in my power to save Philip and Anna. If it were, I would do it.”
Even then Elza did not cry. She just sat there quite, quite still, her big, round eyes quite dry, her mouth without a quiver, but sitting there so still, so still with her beautiful golden hair all round her face, the soft streaks of grey all about her temples, her fine features rigid, her podgy white hands resting on her knees; she looked such a tragic figure of despair that Rosemary could hardly suppress the cry of anguish that rose insistently to her throat.
“And so we can do nothing,” Elza said, with a note of quiet finality in her voice.
“Don’t say that, dear,” Rosemary protested. “Jasper, as a matter of fact, has gone to Bucharest to try and see the King personally. The Romanian Government owes some gratitude to my husband, as you know. I am quite sure that he will bring strong pressure to bear upon the authorities, and get a full pardon for Philip and Anna on the score of their youth.”
But Elza slowly shook her head.
“You don’t believe yourself, darling,” she said, “in what you say. The children have committed the unpardonable crime of being born Hungarians, and of resenting foreign tyranny in their native land. The King himself would be kind, I am sure, but Bucharest is a long way off, and the bureaucrats over here do not know the meaning of the word ‘mercy.’ ”
“But we know the meaning of the word ‘hope,’ Elza dear,” Rosemary said steadily, and struggled to her feet.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽摇头。
“他说，门里的这位客人有力量救我儿子免于死。你有这个力量吗，罗斯玛丽？”
“没有！没有！”罗斯玛丽狂乱地抗议。“倘若在我权力之内，你想我不会不惜一切救菲利普和安娜吗？”
埃尔莎点头。
“是的，亲爱的，”她温和地说。“我当然这么想；可吉普赛人这样说的时候，我忍不住生出希望来，因为他别的每件事都说中了——”
忽然她把罗斯玛丽的脸捧在自己两手之间，用一种几乎凶猛的凝重的目光直望着她的眼睛，仿佛要从这年轻女人的灵魂深处夺出一个秘密。
“对我起誓，罗斯玛丽，”她说，温柔的嗓音变得沙哑刺耳，“对我起誓，世上没有任何你能做的事可以救菲利普！”
罗斯玛丽迎着她的目光，稳稳地答：
“我向你起誓：救菲利普和安娜不在我的权力之内。倘若在，我一定救。”
即便这样，埃尔莎也没有哭。她只是坐在那里，十分、十分安静，圆圆的大眼睛十分干燥，嘴唇没有一丝颤动，只是那样坐着，那样静，那样静，美丽的金发环着她的脸，鬓边几绺灰发，清秀的面容僵着，胖胖的白手搁在膝上；她那副绝望的悲怆形象，叫罗斯玛丽几乎压不住那执意涌上喉咙的哀号。
“这样我们就无事可做了。”埃尔莎说，声音里有一种平静的定局。
“别这么说，亲爱的，”罗斯玛丽抗议。“贾斯珀其实已经去布加勒斯特，想设法亲自覘见国王。你们知道，罗马尼亚政府欠我丈夫几分情。我十分肯定，他会对当局施加强大的压力，看在两人年轻的份上为菲利普和安娜求到全面特赦。”
可埃尔莎缓缓摇头。
“你自己也不信你说的，宝贝，”她说。“这两个孩子犯的是不可赦的罪：生为匈牙利人，并且在自己的祖国怨恨外国的暴政。国王本人想必是仁厚的，这我信；可布加勒斯特远得很，这边的官僚不懂得‘怜悯’二字怎么讲。”
“可我们懂得‘希望’二字怎么讲，亲爱的埃尔莎，”罗斯玛丽坚定地说，挣扎着站起来。

===Original===

“We are not going to give up hope. You talk about your gipsies having the gift of prophecy. Well, it is my turn to prophesy now. Philip and Anna are in God’s hands, and you and I are going to pray so hard and so ceaselessly that God will help us, I am sure. I know,” she added firmly.
Elza gave a short, quick sigh.
“Oh, yes,” she said, “you are lucky, you English! Your religion means a great deal to you. But we, over here, are so different. We go to convent schools when we are too young to understand. Then we are all fire and enthusiasm, but we do not understand. After that we marry and live in those remote villages where the poor curé is only an illiterate peasant with whom we have nothing in common, whose habits are often such that we could not possibly make our confession to him. And so we soon forget what we learned in our childhood, and we come to trusting in ourselves rather than in God.”
She rose and, with the same motherly gentleness which she always showed to Rosemary, she folded the girl in her loving arms.
“Good night, my dear,” she said placidly. “I ought not to have kept you up so late. Good night, dear. Pray to your God for us all. The God of the English is more merciful, I think, than ours.”
“Elza,” Rosemary insisted, “promise me that you will not give up hope. Jasper comes back tomorrow. He may bring the best of news. Promise me that in any case you will not give up hope.”
The ghost of a smile appeared on Elza’s face.
“I will promise,” she said, “not altogether to give up faith.”
Rosemary kissed her tenderly. After that she escorted her as far as her room, and at the door she kissed her once more, and then she said, with solemn earnestness:
“Elza darling, will you believe me if I say that if I could give my life for those two children I would do it? If it were in my power to save them, I would. But it is not in my power to save them, to do anything, but to leave them in God’s hands.”
Elza returned her kiss with gentleness and affection.
“Dear, kind Rosemary,” she murmured; “go to bed, dear, you must be so tired.”
Then she quietly slipped into her room and closed the door. And Rosemary was left to face the night alone.

===Chinese===

“我们不会放弃希望。你说你们的吉普赛人有预言的天赋。好，现在轮到我来预言。菲利普和安娜在上帝手里，你我要祷告得那样恳切、那样不停，上帝必会帮助我们，我确信。我知道。”她坚定地加了一句。
埃尔莎短短地、急急地叹了口气。
“哦，是的，”她说，“你们英国人有福气！宗教对你们意味深长。可我们这边的人多么不同。我们太年幼不懂事的年纪被送进修道院学校。那时我们满腔火热和热忱，可我们不懂。后来我们出嫁，住进那些偏远的村子，那里的穷神父不过是个目不识丁的庄稼汉，同我们毫无共通之处，他的行止又常常叫我们没法向他办告解。于是我们很快忘掉童年学到的一切，渐渐只信自己，不信上帝了。”
她站起来，带着她一贯对罗斯玛丽显示的那种母性的温柔，把这姑娘搂进她慈爱的怀抱。
“晚安，亲爱的，”她平静地说。“我不该留你熬夜到这么晚。晚安，宝贝。为我们都向你的上帝祈祷吧。英国人的上帝，我想，比我们的更慈悲。”
“埃尔莎，”罗斯玛丽坚持，“答应我，不放弃希望。贾斯珀明天回来。他也许带回最好的消息。答应我，无论如何不放弃希望。”
埃尔莎脸上掠过一丝笑影。
“我答应你，”她说，“不完全放弃信心。”
罗斯玛丽温柔地吻她。随后她送她到房门口，在门口又吻了她一次，然后带着郑重的恳切说：
“亲爱的埃尔莎，倘若我说，只要能拿我的命换那两个孩子，我都肯换，你信我吗？救他们若在我的权力之内，我一定救。可救他们、做任何事，都不在我的权力之内，只能把他们交在上帝手里。”
埃尔莎温柔而深情地回吻她。
“亲爱的、好心肠的罗斯玛丽，”她喃喃道；“去睡吧，宝贝，你一定累极了。”
随后她悄悄进了自己的房，关上门。剩下罗斯玛丽独自面对这一夜。

## XIX / 十九
===Original===

What puzzled Rosemary was the gipsy.
What was the mystery of that vagabond found lurking in the park at nightfall with a revolver in his belt? What connection had he with the eyes that had watched Rosemary the night that she was talking with little Anna? And how had he come in possession of the inner history of Philip’s and Anna’s temporary release?
There was a mystery here. Somewhere. A disquieting, a terrifying mystery, not altogether to be accounted for by the spy system or other secret organisation of the Romanian Government.
All night Rosemary struggled with the puzzle. All night she wrestled with herself for the right to break her promise to Jasper and to lay all the facts of this case before Peter. She wanted to do this before Jasper’s return, and, anyway, he must release her﻿—he must﻿—from that promise which placed her in a false and disloyal position towards Peter. When Rosemary fell asleep the dawn was breaking, and she had almost made up her mind to tell Peter everything.
But the next morning when she went downstairs she found the whole house in a turmoil. Servants rushing to and fro, Elza in close conversation with the chef, Maurus shouting contradictory orders across the galleried hall. Peter was in the drawing-room playing a jazz tune this time, and Philip and Anna were foxtrotting, infusing even into this ugly so-called dance some of their own native grace.
As soon as Rosemary appeared she was greeted with regular war-whoops of delight. In a moment she was drawn into the whirlpool of excitement. Philip and Anna dragged her to the sofa, and they and Maurus and Elza all talked to her at once, while Peter, with the inevitable cigarette in the corner of his mouth, continued to pound away at the jazz tune.
From the deafening hubbub of conversation Rosemary gathered, in the first instance, that the gipsy band from Bonczhida were coming over the next day, and the gipsies of Bonczhida were the finest in Transylvania. Then that the Keletys were driving over from Hajdu, and the Fejérs from Henger; that perhaps Aunt Charlotte would come too and bring Marie; that the Keletys were bringing the Poltys, and the Fejérs having the Kékesy boys staying with them would of course bring them along. They reckoned that there would be ten or a dozen couples to dance, and with the mammas and papas they would be thirty to supper.

===Chinese===

叫罗斯玛丽想不透的，是那个吉普赛人。
黄昏时分躲在园林里、腰带上别着一把左轮手枪的流浪汉，他身上有什么哑谜？同罗斯玛丽和小安娜说话那夜盯着她的那双眼睛，他有什么牵连？他又是怎么知道菲利普和安娜暂时获释的内情的？
这里头有一个哑谜。在某处。一个叫人不安、叫人恐惧的哑谜，光凭罗马尼亚政府的间谍系统或其他秘密组织，还完全解释不通。
罗斯玛丽整夜同这个谜题搏斗。整夜她同自己搏斗，争取挣脱打破对贾斯珀的承诺、把本案全部事实摆到彼得面前的权利。她要赶在贾斯珀回来之前办到；再说，他也必须解除她——必须——解除那个使她对彼得处于虚伪而不义地位的承诺。罗斯玛丽睡着时天已破晓，她几乎已经打定主意，把一切都告诉彼得。
可第二天早晨她下楼时，发现整座宅子闹成了一团。仆人跑来跑去，埃尔莎同厨子密谈，毛罗什隔着有廊大厅喊出自相矛盾的命令。彼得在客厅里，这回弹的是一支爵士曲调，菲利普和安娜跳着狐步，连这支难看的所谓舞蹈也被他们注入了几分天生的优雅。
罗斯玛丽一露面，迎接她的是一声声欢喜的呐喊。转眼她便被卷进了兴奋的漩涡。菲利普和安娜把她拖到沙发上，他们俩加上毛罗什和埃尔莎一齐对她说话，彼得嘴里照例叼着烟，只管把那支爵士曲子敲得山响。
从震耳欲聋的七嘴八舌里，罗斯玛丽首先听明白：邦奇希达的吉普赛乐队明天要来，而邦奇希达的吉普赛人是特兰西瓦尼亚最棒的。接着是：凯莱蒂一家从海杜赶车过来，费耶尔一家从亨格尔来；说不定夏洛特姑妈也会来，还带着玛丽；凯莱蒂家会带上波尔蒂一家，费耶尔家正有凯凯西家的几兄弟小住，自然也会一并带来。他们盘算着，跳舞的能有十来对，加上爸爸妈妈们，吃夜宵的要有三十口。

===Original===

They expected most of the guests to arrive in time for luncheon, and in the afternoon they could have some tennis; then in the evening they would have a ball to which the officers from the garrison at Cluj had already been invited, and they had accepted by telephone. Among them were those who were going to play cricket with the Hungarians at Hódmezö under Peter’s direction.
At this marvellous statement Peter came to a pause in the music with a crashing chord, took the cigarette out of his mouth, and throwing up his hands, exclaimed:
“Going to play cricket with the Hungarians under Peter’s direction! Oh, blessed People! Ye ghosts of Fitzgerald, Pycroft, and of Lillywhite, do ye hear them and writhe up there in Heaven?”
Then he struck up the “March of the Men of Harlech.”
“If anyone says anything more about cricket,” he said solemnly, “I shall force them to play with warped bats and golf-balls on a ploughed field.”
Not a trace of anxiety or even preoccupation on any of those dear, beaming faces. Elza was as excited as any of them, worried to death because the carp they had got out of the lake for this evening’s supper were not really fat.
“They’re no bigger than a good-sized goldfish,” she said to Rosemary with a note of real tragedy in her voice, and her blue eyes at once looked anxious and troubled, as if the matter of the carp was the only thing that could worry her.
Rosemary made a great effort not to be a wet blanket in the midst of all this gaiety. In this she succeeded admirably. All she had to do was to smile and to nod her head, and now and then to cry out, “How splendid!” The others did all the talking, and when conversation subsided for a moment Peter came down with a fresh, crashing jazz tune.
Rosemary would have thought the whole scene a phantasmagoria﻿—illusive images that would presently be dispelled﻿—only that she had known these people ever since she was a child. She had studied their curious psychology, half barbaric, with all the primitive disregard of danger and the passion for pleasure, even at the point of death. She gave ungrudging admiration to Elza﻿—Elza who had sat in her room last night, rigid, dry-eyed, a living statue of despair. What went on behind that smooth, white brow of hers? What projects? What hopes? And little Anna? Anna knew. Anna guessed. She had spoken of her fears to Rosemary.

===Chinese===

客人们大多预计赶得上午饭；下午可以打打网球；晚上开一场舞会，克卢日驻军的军官们早已受到邀请，并且已在电话里应允了。他们中间，就有将来要在彼得指导下、同匈牙利人在霍德梅泽打板球的那些人。
听到这句妙语，彼得在琴上轰然一和弦收住乐声，从嘴里拿掉香烟，双手一扬，喊道：
“在彼得指导下同匈牙利人打板球！哦，蒙福的子民！菲茨杰拉德、皮克罗夫特、利利怀特的在天之灵啊，你们听见了没有？可曾在天上辗转？”
说罢他奏起《哈勒赫男儿进行曲》。
“谁再提板球，”他郑重宣布，“我就罚他们用翘曲的球拍和高尔夫球，到犁过的田里去打。”
那些亲爱的、红光满面的脸上，没有一丝焦虑，连心事也没有。埃尔莎同他们哪一个一样兴奋，为今晚夜宵从湖里捞上来的鲤鱼不够肥急得要死。
“它们不比条大点的金鱼大多少，”她对罗斯玛丽说，嗓音里带着真正的悲剧调子，蓝眼睛顿时显得又急又愁，仿佛鲤鱼是天下唯一能烦扰她的事。
罗斯玛丽竭尽全力，不做这场欢乐里的败兴人。这一点她做得漂亮极了。她只消微笑、点头，不时喊一声“好极了！”。说话全由旁人包了，谈话间歇时，彼得便砸下一支新的、轰响的爵士曲。
罗斯玛丽本会把这整个场面当作一场幻灯戏——转眼就会消散的幻象——只可惜这些人她从小就认识。她研究过他们那种古怪的心理：一半带着蛮性，全然原始地不把危险当回事，对享乐怀着一腔热情，哪怕站在死的边缘。她不由衷地钦佩埃尔莎——昨晚还坐在房里、僵直、无泪、一尊活的绝望雕像的埃尔莎。她那光洁的白额头后面在进行着什么？什么计划？什么希望？还有小安娜呢？安娜知道。安娜猜到了。她同罗斯玛丽说起过她的恐惧。

===Original===

Spoken of eyes that watched her, of eyes that were willing her to do something foolish that would compromise her irretrievably this time. Elza and Anna! What an example of self-possession, of self-control! Rosemary was almost ready to persuade herself that something had happened to reassure them both﻿—that, in fact, they knew the danger to be past.
Only that Elza avoided her glance, and that the dear soul, usually so placid, so stable, was just a thought more restless than usual, and her gentle voice would from time to time become shrill.
At last, genuinely tired and bewildered by so much noise, Rosemary jumped up and, laughing, declared that she must escape out of the bear-garden for a moment and get a breath of fresh air in the park. In order to reach the glass door that gave on the veranda, Rosemary had to go past the piano. Quite close. Peter looked up when she was near him, and she said to him as she went past: “They are very gay, aren’t they?”
“Elza has a perfectly mad plan in her head,” Peter replied, and struck a few loud chords so that no one save Rosemary should hear what he said. “For God’s sake, if you have any influence over her, get her to give it up.”
Then he shouted merrily: “I’ve had enough of those horrible American tunes. Who wants a csàrdàs?”
But he did not play a csàrdàs. For a moment or two his fingers wandered aimlessly over the keys, whilst his eyes followed Rosemary as she stepped through the glass door on to the sunbathed veranda. And as Rosemary felt the sun, the clear, luscious air, the scent of flowers and of distant pines, envelop her as in a warm mantle, there came wafted to her ears the soft strains of that exquisite Hungarian love-song: “There Is but One Beautiful Girl in All the World.” The piano now seemed to sing under Peter’s delicate touch and Rosemary paused and stood quite, quite still, letting the music sink into her, yielding to its voluptuous cadence, and allowing her thoughts, her desires, her longings, to soar upwards to that infinity to which music alone can convey the soul on its magic wings.

===Chinese===

说起过盯着她的那些眼睛，说起过逼她干点糊涂事、好叫她这一回无可挽回地毁掉的那些眼睛。埃尔莎和安娜！何等的镇定、何等的自制的榜样！罗斯玛丽几乎要说服自己：发生了什么事，叫她们俩都放了心——事实上，她们知道危险已经过去了。
只是埃尔莎总避开她的目光；这颗平日那样恬静、那样安稳的好心，也显得比往常多了一分不安，她温柔的声音不时会变得尖利。
末了，被这许多喧闹弄得当真又累又昏，罗斯玛丽跳起来，笑着宣布她必须逃出这吵嚷的园子，去园林里透口气。要走到通往游廊的玻璃门，罗斯玛丽必须从钢琴边经过。离得很近。她走近时，彼得抬起头，她走过时对他说：“他们真快活，是不是？”
“埃尔莎脑子里有一个十足疯狂的计划，”彼得答道，随手敲出几个响亮的和弦，好叫除了罗斯玛丽谁也听不见他的话，“看在上帝份上，你若还劝得动她，劝她丢开它。”
随后他快活地喊：“这些可怕的美国调子我受够了。谁要听恰尔达什？”
可他没有弹恰尔达什。有一两分钟，他的手指漫无目的地在琴键上游移，眼睛却跟着罗斯玛丽，望她步出玻璃门，走进洒满阳光的游廊。当罗斯玛丽感到太阳、清冽甘美的空气、花香和远处松林的气息像一件暖斗篷裹住自己时，一缕轻柔的乐声随风吹进她的耳朵：那支精美的匈牙利情歌《世上只有一个美丽的姑娘》。琴在彼得轻巧的触键下仿佛唱了起来；罗斯玛丽停住，静静地、静静地站着，任音乐渗进自己，向它靡曼的调子屈服，听凭她的思绪、她的愿望、她的渴慕，向那唯有音乐才能用魔翼把灵魂送达的无穷，扶摇而上。

## XX / 二十
===Original===

Rosemary had wandered beyond the confines of the park, and roamed about in the woods, having lost all sense of time. When presently she came back to the reality of things she looked at her watch and saw that it was close on twelve o’clock. Luncheon at the château was at half-past. It meant stepping out briskly so as to be in time.
As soon as she reached the flower-garden, it struck her as strange that the château suddenly appeared to be so quiet. No sound reached her as she came near to the veranda steps, either of shrill, excited voices, or of laughter or song.
She found the family assembled on the veranda﻿—Maurus, Elza, Philip and Anna. Only Peter was not there. A first glance at them all revealed to Rosemary what had occurred. Elza had told them what the gipsy had said. Maurus sat in his chair like a man in a trance, his dark face flushed, his hair towzled, his large, dark eyes staring out before him, with a look in them that was not entirely sane.
Philip, on the other hand, was pacing up and down the veranda floor, whilst Anna stood quite still, leaning against a column, looking for all the world like a little martyr tied to the stake, her small, thin hands clasped together, a faint flush on her cheeks. These two children looked excited rather than horror-filled. Anna’s face suggested that of an idealist﻿—not altogether resigned, but nevertheless eager to suffer for the cause. But Philip looked like a fighter, seeking for a chance to hit back, a combatant not yet brought to his knees.
Elza’s round, blue eyes just wandered from one to the other of these faces all dear to her.
They were dry eyes, anxious eyes, but there was nothing in them today of that tragic despair which had been so heartbreaking to behold the evening before.
Rosemary’s first thought had been: “They know. Elza has told them!” The second was “Elza has a plan. Peter said it was a mad one. A plan for Philip and Anna’s escape.” She wondered if they would tell her.
“I hope I am not late for lunch,” she said, rather breathlessly, as she had been walking very fast. Then she added casually: “Where is Peter?”
“He is busy packing,” Elza replied.
“Packing?” Rosemary exclaimed, puzzled. “He is not going away already?”
“Yes,” Elza said, “tonight.”
“But he did not say anything yesterday,” Rosemary insisted, “about going away again so soon. Or even this morning.”

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽信步走出了园子的边界，在林子里漫游，把时间全忘了。待她回到眼前的现实，一看表，已经快十二点了。庄园的午饭是十二点半开。这就得加快脚步才赶得上。
一走到花园，她就觉得奇怪：庄园怎么突然这样静。走近游廊台阶，听不见一声尖利的、激动的嗓门，也没有笑声或歌声。
一家人都聚在游廊上——毛罗斯、埃尔莎、菲利普和安娜。只缺彼得。对他们扫一眼，罗斯玛丽就明白出了什么事。埃尔莎把吉普赛人的话告诉了他们。毛罗斯坐在椅子里，像中了邪，黧黑的脸涨红着，头发乱蓬蓬，黑色的大眼睛直瞪着前方，眼里的神色不完全清醒。
菲利普则在游廊地板上走来走去；安娜一动不动地倚着廊柱站着，活像一个被绑上火刑柱的小殉道者，细瘦的小手交握着，颊上一抹淡淡的红晕。这两个孩子看上去不是满怀恐怖，倒是满怀兴奋。安娜的脸是一张理想主义者的脸——不无听天由命，却巴不得为事业受苦。菲利普则像个斗士，寻着还击的机会，一个还没跪下的角斗士。
埃尔莎圆圆的蓝眼睛只是从一张亲爱的脸游移到另一张。
那是干燥的、焦灼的眼睛，可今天里面全没有昨晚那种令人心碎的悲恸欲绝。
罗斯玛丽第一个念头是：“他们知道了。埃尔莎告诉他们了！”第二个是：“埃尔莎有个计划。彼得说那是个疯计划。一个让菲利普和安娜逃走的计划。”她不知道他们会不会告诉她。
“希望我午饭没迟到，”她说，有些气喘——她方才走得很快。随后她随口问：“彼得呢？”
“他忙着收拾行李呢，”埃尔莎答。
“收拾行李？”罗斯玛丽愕然，“他不是这就走吧？”
“是啊，”埃尔莎说，“今晚。”
“可他昨天没说要这么快再走呀，”罗斯玛丽追了一句，“今儿早上也没说。

===Original===

“I don’t think he knew yesterday,” Elza rejoined. “It seems he had a telephone message half an hour ago. He says he must go.”
Anna now appeared to wake out of her trance. Rosemary was standing close to her just then; she took Rosemary’s hand gently in hers and said:
“You see, darling, it is like this: one of Peter’s cricketers has telephoned to him to say that they have such a lot of trouble about their rooms at Hódmezö. Romanians are not exactly popular in Hungary,” she went on with a wan little smile, “and I suppose that hotel-keepers don’t care to put them up. So Peter had to promise to go and put things right for his cricketers.”
“He will come back, of course, after the cricket match,” Elza concluded placidly. “But it is a great nuisance for him, packing and unpacking all the time.”
Rosemary made no further remark. Everything seemed terribly puzzling. That Elza had told the children, had told Maurus, all she knew, was beyond question. That Peter also knew everything, and that he knew and disapproved of some plan which Elza had made, Rosemary supposed, for the escape of Philip and Anna was, to her mind, equally certain. But even if Peter disapproved, how could he go away at this critical time, and leave Elza to plan and contrive alone, hampered by a half-crazy husband, and surrounded by spies? However, no one apparently meant to say anything more just then, and it was quite a relief when the luncheon-bell sounded and the little party on the veranda broke up and everyone trooped downstairs for luncheon.
Peter was already in the dining-room, waiting for the others. Elza in her kind, gentle way asked him about his packing, and whether she could help him to get ready. But Peter declared that he wanted nothing, only the carriage this evening to take him to Cluj.
He grumbled terribly at having to go away. He hated the idea of missing the ball and all the friends who were coming; but when Elza or Maurus tried to persuade him to stay, he was very firm. “I’ve got to go, Aunt Elza. You don’t know what complications might occur if those Romanians got to Hódmezö and were not properly treated. Good God!” he added, with mock horror, “it might land you all in another war! And all through my fault!”
Rosemary had never seen Peter so gay or conversational.

===Chinese===

“我想他昨天还不知道，”埃尔莎应道，“半小时前他接了个电话。他说他非去不可。”
安娜这时仿佛从恍惚中醒来。罗斯玛丽正站在她近旁；她轻轻把罗斯玛丽的手握在手里，说：
“你瞧，亲爱的，是这样的：彼得队里一个打板球的给他挂了电话，说他们在霍德梅泽的住处出了大麻烦。罗马尼亚人在匈牙利可不怎么受欢迎，”她带着一丝苍白的浅笑接着说，“我想旅店老板不愿意接待他们。所以彼得只好答应去替他的球员把事情摆平。”
“打完板球赛他当然会回来，”埃尔莎安然收尾，“可老是这样收拾来收拾去，真够他受的。”
罗斯玛丽没有再说什么。一切都蹊跷得可怕。埃尔莎把她知道的一切告诉了孩子们、告诉了毛罗斯，这毋庸置疑。彼得也全知道了，而且他知道并反对埃尔莎订下的某个计划——罗斯玛丽猜想是为菲利普和安娜逃走的计划——在她看来同样确凿。可就算彼得反对，他怎么能在这个节骨眼上走开，撇下埃尔莎独自去筹划、去设法，拖着一个半疯的丈夫，四周都是密探？不过，眼下显然谁也不打算多说什么，幸好午饭铃响了，游廊上的这一小群人散了，大家成群下楼去吃饭。
彼得已经在饭厅里等大家了。埃尔莎用她那和善、温柔的样子问他行李收得怎样，要不要帮忙。可彼得说他什么都不要，只要今晚的车送他去克卢日。
他嘀咕着舍不得走。一想到要错过舞会、错过要来的那些朋友，他就腻烦；可埃尔莎或毛罗斯一劝他留下，他就非常坚决。“我非去不可，埃尔莎姨。您不知道，要是那些罗马尼亚人到了霍德梅泽没受到好好款待，会出多大的乱子。天哪！”他装出恐怖的样子补一句，“兴许给你们大家再招来一场战争！全怪我！”
罗斯玛丽从没见过彼得这样欢快、这样健谈。

===Original===

He appeared entirely unconscious of the undercurrent of tragedy that flowed through Elza’s pathetic attempts at conversation, and Maurus’s equally tragic silences. He talked incessantly, chiefly about the cricket match and chiefly to Philip, who made desperate efforts to appear interested. Rosemary did her best, too, but she was anxious and puzzled, and frankly she did not believe in the story of the telephone message.
She tried now and then to catch Elza’s eye, but in this she never once succeeded. Elza was avoiding her glance. She meant to say nothing about her plan﻿—this mad plan of which Peter disapproved so thoroughly that he preferred to be out of the way. Did these dear, kind people mistrust her then, because of what the gipsy had said? Or was this reticence merely the natural outcome of a sense of supreme danger that mistrusted everything and everybody?
Rosemary felt the mystery deepening around her. She could not understand Peter.
Sometime after luncheon she found Elza and Anna sitting together in the small brick-built summerhouse at the farther end of the lake. Rosemary had wandered as far as there with a book, anxious as she was to be out of the way. It was hot, and the air was very still, and the scent of tuberoses and heliotrope was almost too heady. In the perennial border a number of hummingbird moths were busy about a bed of sweet sultan; the soft whirring sound of their wings could be heard quite distinctly in the extreme stillness of this late summer’s afternoon. From time to time distant sounds of village life came in quick, short waves to Rosemary’s ear, as well as the sharp click of tools wielded by the gardeners at work somewhere in the park. Close beside the summerhouse one man was busy hand-weeding the path. As Rosemary drew nearer, he looked up for an instant, and then he shuffled rapidly away. In the long, stooping figure, the dirty rags and the dark skin, Rosemary thought that she recognised the gipsy of the previous night. It was just like Elza, she thought, to give the poor wretch work on the estate.
When Rosemary saw Elza and Anna sitting together in the summerhouse, her instinct was to pass discreetly on, with just a hasty, cheery word, but Elza called to her.
“Come and sit here a minute, Rosemary darling,” she said. “Anna and I want to tell you everything.”
Everything! Rosemary without a word stepped into the little pavilion.

===Chinese===

埃尔莎那些凄婉的搭话里、毛罗斯同样凄惨的沉默里流淌着的悲剧潜流，他仿佛全然不觉。他说个不停，主要谈板球赛，主要对菲利普说；菲利普则拼命装出有兴趣的样子。罗斯玛丽也竭力应景，可她心里又急又疑，而且说白了，她根本不信那个电话的故事。
她不时想捕捉埃尔莎的目光，却一次也没成功。埃尔莎在躲她的眼。她打算对自己的计划只字不提——这个彼得起反感、宁可一走了之的疯计划。难道因为这些亲爱厚道的人信不过她——因为那吉普赛人的话？还是这份缄默，不过是那种对一切人一切事都不敢信的极境危惧的自然结果？
罗斯玛丽觉得身边的谜越结越深。她弄不懂彼得。
午饭后过了一会儿，她看见埃尔莎和安娜一起坐在湖那头砖砌的小凉亭里。罗斯玛丽拿着一本书逛到那边——她正巴不得躲开。天气热，空气纹丝不动，晚香玉和天芥菜的香气浓得几乎熏人。宿根花境里，一群蜂鸟蛾在一畦香芙蓉间忙碌；在残夏午后的极度寂静里，它们翅膀轻柔的嗡嗡声听得清清楚楚。村里生活的远声一阵一阵短促地传到罗斯玛丽耳中，还有园丁们在园中某处干活时工具清脆的磕碰声。凉亭近旁，有一个人正蹲在小径上用手拔草。罗斯玛丽走近时，他抬眼一瞬，随即拖着脚步匆匆躲开了。那瘦长的佝偻身影、肮脏的破衣、黧黑的皮肤，罗斯玛丽觉得认出了正是昨夜那个吉普赛人。她心想，这也正是埃尔莎的为人：给这可怜虫在庄园里派份活干。
看见埃尔莎和安娜同坐在凉亭里，罗斯玛丽的本能是知趣地走过去，只丢下一句匆匆的客气话，可埃尔莎叫住了她。
“过来坐一会儿，亲爱的罗斯玛丽，”她说，“安娜和我要把一切都告诉你。”
一切！罗斯玛丽一言不发，跨进了小亭子。

===Original===

Anna pulled a wicker chair forward between herself and Elza, and Rosemary sat down, a little anxious, a little fearful, wondering what these dear, enthusiastic hotheads had devised, and how she herself would act when she knew. Elza at once took hold of her hand and fondled it.
“You asked me last night, darling,” she began, “not to give up hope, didn’t you?”
Rosemary nodded acquiescence.
“And I promised that I would not give up faith,” Elza went on quietly. “Well, I have kept my faith all through last night, which was very trying. With the dawn, hope came to me, and after that I once more felt in charity with all the world.”
Rosemary gave Elza’s podgy white hand a tender squeeze. “Dear!” she whispered.
“We have a plan, darling,” Elza said triumphantly. “A splendid plan! Tomorrow night Philip and Anna will be in Hungary, safely out of the way.”
Rosemary had known all along what was coming. She looked at Anna, who gave an excited little nod.
“Tell Rosemary, Aunt Elza,” she said. “All from the beginning. There’s no one in the world you can trust as you can Rosemary.”
“Listen then, darling,” Elza said, speaking quite quietly at first, then allowing excitement to get hold of her voice, making it tremble while she spoke, and husky with eagerness, while her command of the English tongue became less and less pronounced.
“It has all been made possible by this cricket business, for which I thank God and Peter Blakeney. As I told you this morning, Peter’s cricket people are all coming here tomorrow for the ball. They have to be at Hódmezö the following day for the cricket. So they will bring their luggage, and make a start from here after the ball﻿—I suppose about midnight﻿—in three motorcars which the Governor, General Naniescu, has himself placed at their disposition. Hódmezö is, as you know, in Hungary, just the other side of the frontier. It will be about four or five hour’s drive from here, as there is a shortcut﻿—quite a good road﻿—which avoids Cluj. In two of those motorcars the cricket people themselves will go; they are mostly young Romanian officers and men of the better class. General Naniescu has, of course, given them all free passes for the occasion. Fortunately he has also given them passes for four servants to accompany them. These four men will go in the third motor, and they will also go in the motor all the way to Hódmezö.

===Chinese===

安娜把一把藤椅拉到自己和埃尔莎中间，罗斯玛丽坐下，有点不安，有点害怕，猜着这对亲爱的、满腔热忱的急性子想出了什么办法，又猜着自己知道之后会怎么做。埃尔莎立刻握住她的手抚弄着。
“昨天夜里你求我，亲爱的，”她开口道，“别放弃希望，是不是？”
罗斯玛丽点头应承。
“我答应过，我不放弃信念，”埃尔莎静静地说下去，“嗯，我守住了我的信念，守过了昨天整整一夜——那一夜真难熬。天一亮，希望来了，从那以后，我再一次觉得同全世界都和好了。”
罗斯玛丽温柔地捏了捏埃尔莎那白胖的手。“亲爱的！”她低声说。
“我们有个计划，亲爱的，”埃尔莎得意地说，“一个顶好的计划！明天夜里，菲利普和安娜就在匈牙利了，稳稳地脱了险。”
要来的事罗斯玛丽早就知道了。她看看安娜，安娜兴奋地轻轻点头。
“告诉罗斯玛丽吧，埃尔莎姨，”她说，“从头说起。世上再没有人能像信罗斯玛丽这样信得过谁了。”
“那就听着，亲爱的，”埃尔莎说。起初说得相当平静，随后任兴奋攥住她的嗓音，说话时那声音直发颤，急得发哑，她那英语也越来越不成样子。
“这全亏了板球这桩事——为此我感谢上帝，感谢彼得·布莱克尼。今儿早上我告诉过你，彼得的板球队明天全要来这儿参加舞会。后天他们得赶到霍德梅泽打板球。所以他们会带上行李，舞会一散就从这儿动身——我想是半夜前后——坐三辆汽车，汽车是总督南尼斯库将军亲自拨给他们用的。霍德梅泽在匈牙利，你们知道，就在边界那一边。从这儿开车过去大约四五个钟头，因为有一条近路——路面很好——可以绕开克卢日。三辆车里，两辆坐板球队的人；他们多半是罗马尼亚的年轻军官和上等人家子弟。南尼斯库将军当然给他们全发了这次用的免费通行证。幸好他还给他们发了四个随行仆役的通行证。这四个人坐第三辆车，而且这辆车要一路开到霍德梅泽。

===Original===

Now two of these servants, whom the local commissary of police has himself chosen and to whom passes have been given, are the two sons of János the miller, who is devoted to us all. His two sons have certainly served in the Romanian army because they were obliged, but they have remained Hungarian at heart and would do anything for me and for Philip.”
Elza paused. Her eager, round eyes searched Rosemary’s face. Rosemary, of course, had already guessed the rest, her own excitement while she listened was as tense as Elza’s. She gripped the white podgy little hand of her friend, and looked from her to Anna﻿—a mute question in every glance.
“You can guess, of course?” Anna said.
Rosemary nodded: “I can guess,” she said, “but do go on.”
“I sent for János early this morning,” Elza went on. “All I had to tell him was that Philip and Anna were in great danger, and must be got out of the country at any cost. He understood! We Hungarians in this occupied territory all understand one another. We understand danger. We live with danger constantly at our door. And János was so clever, so helpful. I only had to outline my plan, he thought out all the details. The mill is about a kilometre from here, the last house in the village; as soon as the first two motors have gone with the cricket people and the Romanian officers, Philip and Anna will at once run round to the mill, and János will give them clothes belonging to his sons. The clothes they will put on. In the meanwhile the third motorcar will have collected the two other men in the village who are going as servants to Hódmezö﻿—one is the brother of the Jew over at the inn, and the other the son of the Romanian storekeeper. Then it will call at the mill. János will ask the two men to come in. He and his two sons will give them some strong spirit to drink. The brother of the Jew and the son of the storekeeper are both of them great drunkards. When they have become what you English call, I think, *blotto*, János will take them back into the motor. There they will sit; and will probably at once go to sleep. But Philip and Anna will also get into the motor. They will be dressed in peasant’s clothes, and they will have the free passes which Naniescu has given to János’ sons. They will get to Hódmezö about five o’clock in the morning. And once they are in Hungary they are safe. Rosemary darling! they are safe!”

===Chinese===

“这四个仆役里，有两个是本地警察总监亲自挑的、发了通行证的，是磨坊主亚诺什的两个儿子——亚诺什对我们一家忠心得不得了。他两个儿子确实被逼着在罗马尼亚军队里当过差，可心始终是匈牙利的，为我、为菲利普什么都肯干。”
埃尔莎顿住。她那急切的圆眼睛探着罗斯玛丽的脸。其余的罗斯玛丽当然早就猜到了；她听着时的紧张，不亚于埃尔莎。她攥住朋友白胖的小手，目光从她移到安娜身上——每一眼都是无声的询问。
“你猜到了，是不是？”安娜说。
罗斯玛丽点头：“猜是猜到了，”她说，“可你还是往下说吧。”
“今儿一早我打发人叫来亚诺什，”埃尔莎接着说，“我只需对他说：菲利普和安娜大难临头，无论如何得送出国去。他就懂了！我们这些沦陷区的匈牙利人，彼此心照不宣。我们懂什么叫危险。危险天天蹲在我们门口。亚诺什多能干、多得力啊。我只要把计划描个大概，细节全是他想出来的。磨坊离这儿一公里，是村里最后一家；头两辆车载着板球队的人和罗马尼亚军官一走，菲利普和安娜立刻绕到磨坊去，亚诺什会把两个儿子的衣裳给他们。他们把衣裳换上。与此同时，第三辆车已在村里接上另外两个要去霍德梅泽当仆役的人——一个是客栈那犹太人的兄弟，一个是罗马尼亚杂货铺主的儿子。然后车到磨坊停一停。亚诺什请那两个人进屋，他同两个儿子拿烈酒给他们喝。那犹太人的兄弟和杂货铺主的儿子，两个都是天大的酒鬼。等他们喝到你们英国人叫作——我想是——‘烂醉’的地步，亚诺什就把他们扶回车上。他们就那么坐着；多半立刻睡着。可菲利普和安娜也上车。他们穿着农民的衣裳，拿着南尼斯库发给亚诺什儿子的免费通行证。早晨五点左右他们就到霍德梅泽了。一进匈牙利，他们就安全了。罗斯玛丽，亲爱的！他们安全了！”

===Original===

Rosemary had remained silent. The whole thing certainly at first glance appeared so easy, so simple that she found herself wondering why she or Jasper﻿—or Peter﻿—had never thought of such a plan. She also wondered why Peter should have spoken of it as a mad plan, and begged her if she had any influence with Elza to dissuade her from it. What had been in his mind when he said that? Of what was he afraid? Spies, of course. But spies, like the poor, were always there, and, after all, Philip and Anna would only be risking what already was forfeit﻿—their lives.
Rosemary sat there in silence, her fingers closed over Elza’s soft, warm hand. She gazed straight before her, thinking. Thinking; her mind already following Philip and Anna’s flight through this hostile, cruel country, to the land which would mean freedom and life for them. She saw them in her mind’s eye, like a vision floating before her across the lake, which in this daydream had become a wide, dusty road with a motorcar speeding along toward life and toward freedom.
It seemed a solution. It must be a solution. Thank God Jasper would be there to help with counsel and with suggestions. Elza was talking again now. In her quaint English, which became more and more involved, she continued to talk of her plan, as a child will talk of some event that made it happy. She harped on the details, on János’ devotion, the two sons who would make their way to the frontier in their father’s bullock cart, and then cross over to Hungary on foot, through the woods and over a mountain pass where there would be no fear of meeting Romanian sentinels. At Hódmezö they would find Peter and the cricket people. They would get back their passes, and return quite gaily with the others, having saved the lives of Philip and Anna. Such devotion! Wasn’t it splendid?
Rosemary only nodded from time to time, and from time to time she squeezed Elza’s hand. It was so hot and so airless here in the little pavilion with those clusters of climbing heliotrope all over the roof and half-blocking up the entrance. The bees and hummingbird moths were making such a buzzing and a whirring; it was just like the hum of motorcar wheels on the dusty road. And through it all came the swishing sound of a garden broom upon the gravel path, between the summerhouse and the stone coping around the ornamental lake.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽一直沉默着。这整个计划乍一看确实那样容易、那样简单，她不禁纳闷：她自己、贾斯珀——或者彼得——怎么就从来没想过。她也纳闷，彼得怎么把它说成一个疯计划，还求她——倘若她对埃尔莎还有几分影响力——劝埃尔莎打消它。他说这话时心里想的是什么？他怕什么？当然是密探。可密探同穷人一样，从来都在那儿；再说，菲利普和安娜冒的危险，不过是拿本已充公的东西去冒险——他们的命。
罗斯玛丽坐在那里不出声，手指扣着埃尔莎柔软温热的手。她直望前方，想着。想着；她的心思已经跟着菲利普和安娜的逃亡，穿过这片怀敌意的、残酷的土地，奔向那片对他们意味着自由和生命的国土。她在心里看见他们，像一幅幻景浮在湖面上：湖在白日梦里变成了一条宽阔的、尘土飞扬的路，一辆汽车在路上飞驰，奔向生命，奔向自由。
这似乎是一条出路。它必定是一条出路。感谢上帝，贾斯珀到时候会在，出主意、想办法。埃尔莎这时又说起话来。用她那古怪的、越来越缠夹的英语，她絮絮地谈她的计划，像个孩子絮絮谈一件叫它高兴的事。她把细节颠来倒去地讲：亚诺什的忠心，那两个儿子怎样乘父亲的牛车到边界，再步行穿过树林、翻一道山口进匈牙利，那条道上不怕撞见罗马尼亚哨兵。到了霍德梅泽，他们会找到彼得和板球队，取回通行证，同大伙儿一起欢欢喜喜回来——救下了菲利普和安娜的命。这样的忠义！多了不起，是不是？
罗斯玛丽只是不时点头，不时捏一捏埃尔莎的手。小亭子里又热又闷，满顶的攀缘天芥菜把入口也堵了一半。蜜蜂和蜂鸟蛾嗡嗡嘤嘤，活像尘土路上汽车轮子的嗡嗡声。这一切之中，又夹着园扫帚在碎石小径上刷刷的扫动声——就在凉亭和装饰湖石沿之间。

===Original===

Rosemary caught herself watching the broom swinging backwards and forwards across the path, and across; she saw the two hands﻿—very dark lean hands they were﻿—that wielded the broom, and finally the gipsy’s tall, thin figure bent almost double to his task. It seemed just right that the man should be there at this hour, sweeping the path for Elza to walk on presently, for Philip also and for Anna. It was right because it was the gipsy who had told Elza what she, Rosemary, had not had the courage to say. There was very little mystery about the gipsy now; he was just a ragged, dirty labourer, bending to his task. Did the strange intuition﻿—or was it divination﻿—that had brought him all the way from his native village to speak with Elza whisper to him that his warning had already borne fruit, and that the gracious lady whom he had come to warn had found in faith and hope the way out of dark destiny?
“Oh, that’s all right, darling! We spoke English all the time!”
Elza said this with a light laugh. Rosemary woke from her daydream. She must have been speaking in her dream﻿—about the gipsy who haunted her thoughts.
“Did I say anything?” she asked.
“Yes, darling,” Anna replied, “you have been very silent for the last minute or two, and then suddenly you said: ‘The gipsy, the gipsy,’ twice, like that. It sounded so funny.”
“I thought,” Elza put in, “that perhaps you were afraid that the dirty old gipsy had heard what we said. But gipsies in Hungary don’t speak English, you know. For one thing they never go to school.”
Elza appeared quite lighthearted now.
“I knew,” she said, “that you would approve of my plan.”
She said this, but Rosemary herself was quite unconscious that she had spoken. She had dreamed and dreamed, and seen a motorcar speeding along the dusty road. But through it all, she had approved, approved of the plan. It was so feasible, and so simple. She only wondered why Peter disapproved.
“What does Peter Blakeney say to all that?” she asked presently.
“Peter?”
Elza asked wide-eyed.
“Yes. You told him about your plan, didn’t you?”
“No! No!” Elza asserted firmly. “We have told no one but you. Peter is going away. Why should we tell Peter?”
“I thought﻿—” Rosemary murmured.
“It will be time enough to tell him,” Anna put in gaily, “when Philip and I turn up at the hotel at Hódmezö. Won’t he be surprised when he sees us?”
How strange it all was!

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽发觉自己盯着那把扫帚在小径上来回、来回地摆；看见那两只手——很黑很瘦的手——挥着扫帚；最后看见吉普赛人那瘦高的、几乎对折在活计上的身影。这个人在这个时辰在这里，似乎再合适不过：为埃尔莎、也为菲利普和安娜待会儿走过来扫净小径。合适，因为这警告本是那吉普赛人带给埃尔莎的——罗斯玛丽自己没有勇气说的话。如今这吉普赛人身上没什么神秘可言了；他不过是个衣衫褴褛的肮脏苦力，弯着腰干活。那种奇特的直觉——或者叫先知？——那种催他从家乡村子一路走来向埃尔莎进言的直觉，可曾低声告诉他：他的警告已经结了果，他前来相警的仁慈夫人，已在信念和希望里找到了走出黑暗命运的生路？
“哦，不要紧的，亲爱的！我们一直说的是英语！”
埃尔莎轻轻笑着说。罗斯玛丽从白日梦里醒来。她一定是梦里说了话——说到那个盘踞在她心头的吉普赛人。
“我说什么了吗？”她问。
“说了，亲爱的，”安娜答，“这一两分钟你一直不说话，后来忽然说：‘吉普赛人，吉普赛人，’一连两遍，就那样。听着可逗了。”
“我还当，”埃尔莎插嘴，“你是怕那个肮脏的老吉普赛人听见了我们的话。匈牙利的吉普赛人可不会英语，你知道。一来他们从来不上学。”
埃尔莎此刻显得轻松愉快。
“我就知道，”她说，“你会赞成我的计划。”
她这样说；可罗斯玛丽自己并没意识到自己说过话。她梦了又梦，看见一辆汽车在尘土路上飞驰。而这一切之中，她是赞成的，赞成这个计划。它这样可行，这样简单。她只是不明白彼得为什么反对。
“这些，彼得·布莱克尼怎么说？”过了一会儿她问。
“彼得？”
埃尔莎睁大了眼睛问。
“是啊。你把你的计划告诉他了，是不是？”
“不！没有！”埃尔莎斩钉截铁，“除了你我们谁也没告诉。彼得要走。干吗告诉彼得？”
“我还以为——”罗斯玛丽喃喃道。
“到时候再告诉他也不迟，”安娜快活地插嘴，“等菲利普和我在霍德梅泽的旅馆里露面的时候。他见了我们，还不得吓一跳？”
这一切多么奇怪！

===Original===

Peter knew, since he spoke of a mad plan in Elza’s head, and begged Rosemary to dissuade her from it. Peter knew, though no one had told him. Another mystery added to all those which had of late filled Rosemary with such a torturing sense of foreboding. Another mystery that seemed to surround Peter’s changed personality, that seemed a part of this new personality of his, flippant and indifferent, so unlike the Peter she had known.
Now she longed passionately for Jasper﻿—dear, kind Jasper, around whom there hung no mystery﻿—the strong hand that would guide her through this maze of intrigue which bewildered as much as it terrified her. Fortunately her promise to Jasper had been kept. With this new mystery about Peter that she vaguely dreaded, she would have been racked with anxiety if she had confided in him. And yet, how disloyal was this thought, this fear! Fear of Peter! Mistrust of Peter! A very little while ago she would have staked her soul that Peter was true, loyal, the soul of honour, an English gentleman, an English sportsman! A Blakeney! A Scarlet Pimpernel of today! What was there in the atmosphere of this unfortunate country groaning under a foreign, hated yoke to taint his simple soul with the foul breath of intrigue?

===Chinese===

彼得知道，因为他说起过埃尔莎脑子里的疯计划，还求罗斯玛丽劝阻她。彼得知道，尽管没有人告诉他。又一层谜，加在近来那些使罗斯玛丽充满折磨人的不祥预感的谜上。又一层谜，仿佛裹着彼得变了的人格，仿佛他那副新人格——轻浮、淡漠，同她认识的彼得判若两人——的一部分。
此刻她热烈地渴望贾斯珀——亲爱的、厚道的贾斯珀，他身上不带谜——渴望那只强有力的手领她走出这座既叫她惶惑又叫她恐惧的迷阵。幸亏她对贾斯珀的诺言守住了。对着这个她隐隐畏惧的、关于彼得的新谜，她若把心事吐给他，早该焦虑得五内俱焚了。然而，这念头、这恐惧多么不忠！怕彼得！疑彼得！就在方才不久，她还会拿灵魂打赌：彼得真诚、忠贞、荣誉的化身，一位英国绅士，一位英国运动家！一位布莱克尼！一位当代的红花侠！这个在异族痛恨的枷锁下呻吟的不幸国度，空气里到底有什么，要来玷污他单纯的灵魂，染上密谋的浊气？

## XXI / 二十一
===Original===

Walking across the lawn toward the château half an hour later, Rosemary found herself once more laughing at her suspicions of Peter. Peter!! Heavens above! what turn were her suspicions taking?
Did she really believe for one moment that Peter was intriguing with these crafty Romanians for the undoing or the persecution of his own kith and kin? The very thought was preposterous. The suggestion untenable. Whatever Jasper might think, whatever he might fear, she, Rosemary, was nothing but a traitor if she allowed herself for one moment to harbour such thoughts of Peter.
He was changed, certainly he was changed. But between that and Jasper’s suspicions﻿—! It was Jasper who had first put thoughts into Rosemary’s head by extracting that strange promise from her. Not to talk to Peter. Not to discuss the situation with Peter. Otherwise she would never for one moment﻿—
Of course, of course, the thought was preposterous. Peter and intrigue! Peter and crafty Machiavellism! Peter and a double game he was ashamed to avow! Why, reason should have rejected the first hint of such a possibility, even if loyalty did not.
“Hallo, Rosemary!”
Peter’s voice brought Rosemary back to reality. She had wandered up the veranda steps, hardly conscious of where she was. Thank Heaven, after her musings she was able to look Peter loyally in the face. He had his hands buried as usual in the pockets of his trousers, and the inevitable cigarette between his lips. Rosemary felt hot and tired; the sun had been baking the lawn while she walked across it, and she had no parasol. With a contented little sigh she sank into the basket chair that Peter pulled forward for her.
“I suppose,” he said abruptly, “that they have been telling you about the nonsense that’s going on in their dear, silly heads.”
And with a nod he indicated the summerhouse where, against the creeper-clad entrance, Elza’s white dress gleamed in the sunshine. Rosemary made no reply. Peter’s words had somehow acted like a douche of cold water upon her sense of rest and well-being. It was true then! He did know. Though Elza and Anna had told him nothing, he knew. How? Rosemary would have given worlds for the right to ask him, but suddenly her promise to Jasper loomed before her with paramount importance, and put a seal upon her lips.
“Won’t you tell me?” Peter insisted.
Of course there was a simple explanation for the whole thing.

===Chinese===

半个钟头后，罗斯玛丽穿过草坪往城堡走，发觉自己又在为对彼得的怀疑发笑。彼得！！天哪！她的怀疑都转到什么路上去了？
她难道真有一刻相信，彼得会同这些奸诈的罗马尼亚人勾结，去坑害、迫害他自己的骨肉至亲？这个念头本身就近乎荒唐。这种说法站不住脚。无论贾斯珀怎么想、怕什么，她罗斯玛丽，只要容许自己对彼得生出这种念头一瞬，她就是个叛徒。
他变了，他确实变了。可是这同贾斯珀的猜疑之间——！是贾斯珀，从她那里硬讨走那个奇怪的承诺，才头一个把念头塞进罗斯玛丽的脑袋。不许同彼得谈。不许同彼得商量局面。要不然她一刻也不会——
当然，当然，这念头荒唐透了。彼得，玩阴谋！彼得，奸诈的马基雅维利手段！彼得，玩一场他羞于承认的双面把戏！喏，就算忠心不排斥它，理智也早该把这种可能性的第一丝苗头拒之门外。
“喂，罗斯玛丽！”
彼得的声音把罗斯玛丽拉回现实。她恍恍惚惚走上游廊台阶，几乎不知道自己身在何处。谢天谢地，这番冥想之后，她还能忠厚地正视彼得的脸。他照例双手插在裤袋里，唇间照例叼着烟。罗斯玛丽觉得又热又累；她走过草坪时，太阳一直烤着草地，她又没带阳伞。她满足地轻叹一声，陷进彼得替她拉过来的帆布椅里。
“我猜，”他突兀地说，“他们把那桩装在他们亲爱的、糊涂的脑袋里的傻事讲给你听了。”
说着他朝凉亭点了点头，埃尔莎的白裙子正映在爬满藤蔓的门口的阳光里。罗斯玛丽没有回答。彼得的话不知怎么像一盆冷水，浇在她安宁适意的感觉上。这么说来是真的！他确实知道。虽然埃尔莎和安娜什么也没对他说，他都知道。怎么知道的？罗斯玛丽甘愿拿整个世界换取问他一句的权利，可她对贾斯珀的承诺忽然横在眼前，压倒一切，给她的嘴上了封条。
“你不肯告诉我？”彼得追问。
当然，这一切自有一个简单的解释。

===Original===

Those dear people, Elza, Maurus, even Anna, were not models of discretion. Their voices were loud and penetrating, and, when they were excited about any project or event, they would discuss it here, there and everywhere at the top of their voices, and with a total disregard of possible eavesdroppers. Peter’s knowledge of Elza’s plans may have come about quite innocently. Rosemary was quite sure it had come about innocently. But somehow she longed for that perfect security and trust in Peter which she used to feel even when he was most capricious and his lovemaking most tempestuous. Why hadn’t he told Elza that he knew? Why, instead of discussing the plan over with Elza or one of the others, did he feign ignorance with them, and suddenly elect to go away on an obviously futile excuse?
Oh, how Rosemary hated all this mystery! And how she feared it! And how, above all, she hated that promise which she had made to Jasper, and which prevented her at this moment from having a straight talk with Peter.
“So you won’t tell me?” he reiterated, and his voice sounded curiously harsh, quite different to his usual very pleasant, musical tones. Peter had the voice of a musician. It was deep in tone and beautifully modulated. Peter’s voice had been one of the things about him that had captivated Rosemary’s fancy in the past. Now, he spoke through his teeth, with that hateful cigarette in the long holder held between the corners of his lips. Rosemary tried to be flippant.
“Dear me!” she exclaimed, with a little broken laugh, “are you trying to play the role of the heavy father, Peter, or of the silent strong man? And now you are frowning just like the hero in one of Ethel M. Dell’s books. When are you going to seize me by the wrist and whack me with a slipper?”
It was very easy to make Peter laugh. He was laughing now, and the scowl fled for the moment from his face.
“Don’t play the fool, Rosemary,” he said in his slangy, boyish way. “Tell me what Aunt Elza has been saying to you out here?”
“But, you silly boy,” she riposted, “there’s nothing to tell.”
Back came the scowl on Peter’s face, darker than before.
“So,” he said curtly, “I suppose that you and Aunt Elza and Anna have been discussing frocks for the past hour and a half.”
“No, dear,” she replied coolly, “only the arrangements for tomorrow’s ball.”
Whereupon Peter said “Damn!”

===Chinese===

这些亲爱的好人——埃尔莎、毛罗什，连安娜也算上——都算不得谨慎的模范。他们嗓门大，传得远，一为哪桩计划或事情兴奋起来，便会这里那里到处扯着嗓子讨论，全然不管有没有人偷听。彼得对埃尔莎计划的了解，也许来得全然无辜。罗斯玛丽确信它来得无辜。可她不知怎么，渴望那种对彼得十足的安全感和信任——从前哪怕他最反复无常、他的求爱最狂风暴雨时，她也曾有过的那种信任。他为什么不告诉埃尔莎他知道？为什么不同埃尔莎或别的什么人把那计划摊开来谈，反而对他们装糊涂，又忽然借着一条分明无谓的借口跑开？
哦，罗斯玛丽多么憎恨这一切神秘！她又是多么害怕它！而尤其是，她多么憎恨她对贾斯珀许下的那个承诺，是它此刻不让她同彼得开诚布公地谈一谈。
“那么你不肯告诉我？”他重复道，嗓音古怪地生硬，全不像他平日那十分悦耳、音乐似的调子。彼得有一副音乐家的嗓子，音色深沉，抑扬美妙。彼得的嗓音，从前正是迷住罗斯玛丽心意的那些东西之一。此刻他咬着牙说话，唇角还叼着长烟嘴里那支可恶的烟。罗斯玛丽试着耍贫嘴。
“哎呀！”她发出一小声破破碎碎的笑，叫道，“你这是要扮严父的角色呢，彼得，还是沉默的硬汉？瞧你现在皱眉的样子，活像埃塞尔·M·戴尔小说里的男主角。几时你才打算抿住我的手腕、脱下拖鞋揍我？”
逗彼得笑是顶容易的事。他此刻就笑了，怒容暂时从他脸上退了。
“别耍宝了，罗斯玛丽，”他用他那俚语连篇的少年腔说。“告诉我，埃尔莎姑妈在这儿对你说了什么？”
“可是你这傻小子，”她回敬，“没有什么可告诉的。”
怒容又回到彼得脸上，比先前更黑。
“这么说，”他生硬地说，“过去一个半钟头，你、埃尔莎姑妈和安娜一直在谈论裙子。”
“不是，亲爱的，”她冷冷地答，“只谈了明天舞会的安排。”
于是彼得说了声“该死！”

===Original===

and swung round on his heel, as if he meant to leave her there without another word. But for this move of his Rosemary was unprepared. She did not want Peter to go. Not just yet. She was perfectly loyal to him in her thoughts, and she was irrevocably determined not to break her promise to Jasper, but she was not going to let Peter go off today without some sort of explanation. She might not see him again after this﻿—for weeks, for months, for years! So she called him back.
“Peter!” she cried.
He swung back and returned to her side. His deep, changeful eyes, which at times were the colour of the ocean on the Cornish coast, and at others recalled the dark tints of his Hungarian ancestors, looked strangely resentful still. But as his glance rested on Rosemary, wandered from her delicate face in the pearly shadow of her garden hat, along the contour of her graceful shoes, the resentful look fled. And Rosemary, glancing up, caught a momentary flash of that soul-holding gaze which had taken her captive that lovely night in June by the river, when she had lain crushed and bruised in his arms, the gaze which that other night in the Albert Hall box had filled her soul with abiding regret.
“What do you want me to tell you, Peter?” she asked in that stupid way that comes to the lips when the soul is stirred and the mind commands self-control.
“Nothing,” he replied roughly, “that you don’t want to.”
“Peter,” she retorted, “why are you so strange with me? One would think I had done something to offend you. You scarcely will speak to me; when you do you are so rough and so abrupt, as if﻿—as if﻿—Oh, I don’t know,” she went on rapidly, and her voice shook a little as she tried to avoid that memory conjuring glance of his. “It seems as if something had come between us, almost as if we were enemies.”
Peter laughed at this, but his laugh sounded rather forced and harsh.
“Enemies!” he exclaimed. “Good God, no!”
“But something has happened, Peter,” she insisted. “I cannot tell you how I find you changed.”
“Well,” he said curtly, “something did happen, you know, when you married Jasper.”
“I don’t mean that, Peter. I saw you in London after I was engaged, and you had not changed then. It is here﻿—in this place﻿—that you seem so different.”
“You must admit the place gets on one’s nerves,” he said with a shrug.
“You must make allowances, Peter,” she rejoined gently.

===Chinese===

他猛地转身，像是要一言不发地把她撂在那里。可他这一招罗斯玛丽没有防备。她不要彼得走。至少这会儿不要。她在思想上对他是完全忠实的，她也拿定主意决不背弃对贾斯珀的承诺，可她不打算今天就这样放彼得走掉，不给个说法。过了这遭，她也许再见不到他了——几星期，几个月，几年！于是她把他唤了回来。
“彼得！”她喊。
他旋身回来，走到她身边。他那双善变的深眼睛，有时像康沃尔海岸上海洋的颜色，有时又叫他匈牙利先祖的深色调，此刻仍带着奇特的怨懟。可当他的目光落在罗斯玛丽身上，从她园帽珍珠色阴影下那张精致的脸，一路游到她那双雅致的鞋的轮廓，怨懟的神色退了。罗斯玛丽抬眼，正接住那一瞬摄人魂魄的凝视——六月里河上那个可爱的夜晚，正是这凝视把她俘虏，当时她瘫软地、带着伤痛躺在他怀里；而在阿尔伯特音乐厅包厢里的那一夜，又是这凝视给她的灵魂填满了长驻的悔恨。
“你要我告诉你什么呢，彼得？”她问，用的是灵魂激动、理智却命令自制时浮上唇头的那种蠢话。
“没什么，”他粗暴地答，“你不想说的就不说。”
“彼得，”她回敬，“你对我怎么这样生分？人家会以为我做了什么开罪你的事。你几乎不同我说话；说起来又这样粗暴、这样突兀，仿佛——仿佛——哦，我说不清，”她急急地说下去，想躲开他那勾魂摄魄的记忆之光，嗓音微微发颤。“仿佛有什么东西横在我们中间，几乎仿佛我们成了冤家。”
彼得听了笑了，可他的笑声听来相当勉强、刺耳。
“冤家！”他叫道。“天哪，不是！”
“可总归是出了什么事，彼得，”她坚持。“我说不出我发现你变了多少。”
“唔，”他生硬地说，“你知道，你嫁给贾斯珀的时候，总归是出了点事。”
“我不是说那个，彼得。订婚之后我在伦敦见过你，那时你没有变。是在这儿——在这个地方——你显得这样不同。”
“你得承认，这地方磨人的神经。”他耸耸肩说。
“你得体谅，彼得。”她温和地接道。

===Original===

“They are in such trouble.”
“Are they?” he retorted.
“Why, you know they are!” And her voice rang with a note of indignant reproach. “How can you ask?”
“I ask because I don’t know. You say that they﻿—I suppose you mean Aunt Elza and Maurus and the kids﻿—are in trouble. How should I know what you mean? Since I’ve been here they have done nothing but shout, dance and make plans for more dancing and shouting, and when I ask you anything you only tell me lies.”
“Peter!”
“I beg your pardon, dear,” he said with sudden gentleness. “I didn’t mean to be caddish. But you know,” he went on, harshly once more, “you did tell me that Jasper had gone to Budapest on business.”
“Well?” she queried.
“Well! Knowing you to be truthful by nature, I am wondering why you should have told me such an unnecessary lie.” Then, as Rosemary was silent, he insisted: “Won’t you tell me, Rosemary?”
“You are talking nonsense, Peter,” she replied obstinately. “There is nothing to tell.”
“Which means that Jasper has told you﻿—or insinuated﻿—that I am not to be trusted.”
She protested: “Certainly not!”
“Then,” he concluded, “the mistrust comes out of your own heart.”
“That again is nonsense, Peter. There is no question of trust or mistrust, and I have no idea what you mean. It is you who try to deceive me by feigning ignorance of what is going on in this house. If Aunt Elza has not spoken openly with you, it certainly is not for me to enlighten you. There,” she added, as she caught a look of eager questioning in his eyes, “I have already said more than I have any right to say. Elza and Anna are coming across the lawn. If you want to know anything more, you had better ask them.”
And abruptly she rose and left him and went into the house. She felt hurt and angry and not a little ashamed. She felt hurt with Peter, angry with Jasper, and ashamed of herself. Peter was quite right. She had told him lies﻿—unnecessary lies. And Jasper had forced her to tell them and to be disloyal to Peter. The present situation was a false one. Utterly false. It was Peter who should take over the direction of Elza’s plan. With his help the chances of Philip’s and Anna’s escape would be increased tenfold.

===Chinese===

“他们的难处太大了。”
“是吗？”他反诘。
“怎么，你知道的呀！”她的声音里响起了愤應责备的调子。“你怎么问得出口？”
“我问，因为我不知道。你说他们——我猜你是指埃尔莎姑妈、毛罗什和那两个孩子——难处大。我怎么知道你指什么？我到这里之后，他们除了喊叫、跳舞、计划再跳舞再喊叫什么也没干，而我问你什么，你只对我撒谎。”
“彼得！”
“请你原谅，亲爱的，”他忽然温和下来说。“我不是要做无赖。可是你知道，”他又生硬地说下去，“你确实告诉过我，贾斯珀到布达佩斯办事去了。”
“怎么样呢？”她问。
“怎么样！你天生诚实，我就在纳闷，你何必对我撒这样一个不必要的谎。”见罗斯玛丽不作声，他追问：“你不肯告诉我吗，罗斯玛丽？”
“你在胡说，彼得，”她固执地答。“没有什么可告诉的。”
“那就是说，贾斯珀告诉过你——或者暗示过——我信不得。”
她抗议：“当然没有！”
“那么，”他作结道，“这猜疑是打你自己心里出来的。”
“那也是胡说，彼得。这里根本谈不上信不信任，我也根本不明白你的意思。倒是你在装糊涂、想骗我，假装不知道这座宅子里在进行什么。埃尔莎姑妈既然没有同你打开天窗说亮话，当然轮不到我来点破你。瞧，”她瞥见他眼里那急切探询的神色，又加了一句，“我已经说得比我有权说的多了。埃尔莎和安娜正穿过草坪过来。你还想知道什么，最好去问她们。”
说罢她霍地起身，撂下他进了宅子。她觉得受了伤，生了气，还有几分羞愧。她为彼得伤心，对贾斯珀生气，为自己羞愧。彼得说得对。她对他撒了谎——不必要的谎。而贾斯珀逼她撒谎，逼她对彼得不义。眼下的局面是虚伪的。彻头彻尾虚伪。该由彼得来接手主持埃尔莎的计划。有他帮忙，菲利普和安娜脱险的机会会增加十倍。

===Original===

It seemed an awful thing﻿—it was an awful thing﻿—that he should be shut out of Elza’s councils, that he should go away on a futile and trivial errand while those his own kith and kin were in such terrible danger, and running into dangers that were worse still.
