He was in the act of lighting a cigarette when he spoke, and he finished lighting it, blew out the match, and threw it into the nearest ashtray before he glanced at Naniescu. Then he smiled, because Naniescu’s face expressed arrogance first, then bewilderment, and finally indecision.
“Is it a challenge?” he reiterated sardonically. “I don’t mind, you know, one way or the other. There are at least three governments﻿—neighbours of yours, by the way﻿—who will pay me ten thousand pounds apiece for certain services which they require, and which I can render them. But you have behaved like a knave and a fool, my friend, and it will amuse me to punish you. So listen to me! Unless you give me a cheque for the ten thousand pounds which you promised me, and which I can cash at your fusty old bank over the way this very afternoon, I guarantee you that Lady Tarkington’s articles will not be published in any English newspaper.”
He smoked on in silence for a little while longer, blowing rings of smoke through his pursed lips, and in the intervals laughing softly, mockingly to himself, or throwing an occasional glance of intelligence in the direction of Kervoisin, who apparently immersed in a book had taken no part in the conversation. Naniescu’s bewilderment had become ludicrous, and at one moment when he took his perfumed handkerchief out of his pocket and mopped his streaming forehead, the face of his spy-in-chief became distorted with that look of ferocious cruelty which was so characteristic of him.
“I haven’t a great deal of time to spare,” Number Ten remarked drily, after a few minutes’ silence; “if you accept my challenge I start for London tonight.”

===Chinese===

“听你这么说我高兴，”十号简截地回敬，“因为这样一来，你现在付我一万就容易了。”
可这主意逗得将军差点笑得从椅子上滚下来。
“Ils sont impayables ces Anglais!（这些英国人真是没法说）”他笑得眼泪直流、气都喘不上来，好不容易才找到话表达他觉得多么可笑。“看在老天的份上，我凭什么要付你一万英镑？”
“因为你不付，那几篇报纸文章就永远别想见报。”
“啊，呸！”南尼斯库带着讥讽的咧嘴喊，“谁能拦住？”
“我，当然。”
“你，当然？我倒想请教，怎么拦？”
“那是我的事。”
“你办不到，我的朋友，”南尼斯库得意地应道，“你办不到。我倒要看看你怎么办得到。”
“这算是叫阵？”
十号说这话时非常平静。他正点着烟，说完把烟点好，吹灭火柴，丢进近旁的烟灰缸，才瞥了南尼斯库一眼。然后他笑了，因为南尼斯库的脸先是傲慢，继而惶惑，最后变成了犹疑。
“算是叫阵吗？”他讥诮地重问一遍。“怎么着都行，告诉你，我不在乎。至少有三家政府——顺便说，都是贵国的邻邦——肯为我需要的差事每家付我一万英镑，而我办得到。可你行事又无赖又蠢，我的朋友，惩罚惩罚你是我的乐子。所以听好！你若不给我一张一万英镑的支票——你答应过我的，今天下午我就能在对过你那家发霉的老银行里兑现——我向你担保，塔金顿勋爵夫人的文章，任何一家英国报纸都登不出来。”
他噘着唇吐着烟圈，又默默抽了一小会儿烟，间歇里自己轻轻地、嘲弄地笑，或朝凯尔瓦赞那边丢去一个心照不宣的眼色——后者仿佛浸在一本书里，没有参与谈话。南尼斯库的惶惑已变得滑稽；有一刻他掏出洒了香水的手帕擦淋淋的额头，他的密探头目的脸上扭出了那副凶残的表情——那正是他的招牌。
“我没有多少闲工夫，”沉默几分钟后，十号干巴巴地说，“你若应战，我今晚就动身去伦敦。”

===Original===

“You’ll never get there in time,” Naniescu rejoined, with an attempt at swagger.
Number Ten smiled. “Don’t you think so?” he asked simply.
“The frontier is closed﻿—”
“Would you rather risk it than pay me the ten thousand pounds?”
Naniescu appealed to his friend.
“De Kervoisin﻿—” he said, almost pitiably.
But M. de Kervoisin, with a shrug, indicated that this was no concern of his.
“M. de Kervoisin,” Number Ten said, still smiling, “knows my methods. During the war I had other and more dangerous frontiers to cross than this one, my friend﻿—and I never failed.”
In Naniescu’s puny mind, obviously a war was waging between greed and avarice. He was seeing his beautiful daydream vanishing into the intangible ether﻿—whence come all dreams﻿—and he was not prepared to take any risks. Those articles which a reliable courier was even now taking to London with all speed were the most precious things he, Naniescu, had ever possessed. They meant honour, security, money﻿—far more money than Number Ten was demanding with such outrageous impudence. And Naniescu was afraid of Number Ten﻿—afraid of his daring, his courage, his unscrupulous determination to carry through what he had set out to do.
Ten thousand pounds! It was a great deal, but it would come out of secret service funds, not out of Naniescu’s own pocket. There was only that slight tickling of one’s amour propre to subdue. The desire to get the better of Number Ten, to win this battle of wits against so crafty an opponent. But what was amour propre when weighed in the balance with the realisation of Naniescu’s wonderful daydreams?
Nevertheless he made one more effort at a bargain.
“If I pay you that ten thousand,” he said, with a savage oath, “what guarantee have I that the articles *will* be published?”
“None,” was Number Ten’s cool reply; “but if you don’t pay me the ten thousand, I guarantee that they will *not* be published.”
At which M. de Kervoisin put down his book and indulged in a good laugh.
“Take care, my friend,” he said to Number Ten, “our friend here is beginning to lose his temper, and you may find yourself under lock and key before he has done with you.”
“I wonder!” Number Ten retorted drily “It would mean raising hell in the English press, wouldn’t it? if a British subject﻿—what?”
He did not pursue the subject. Even Naniescu himself had put such a possibility out of his reckoning.

===Chinese===

“你根本来不及，”南尼斯库回敬，想装出一点派头。
十号笑了。“你不信？”他只简单地问。
“边境封了——”
“你是宁可冒这个险，也不肯付我一万？”
南尼斯库向朋友求援。
“德·凯尔瓦赞——”他说，几乎可怜巴巴的。
可德·凯尔瓦赞先生耸耸肩，表示与他无关。
“德·凯尔瓦赞先生，”十号仍然笑着说，“知道我的手段。战争那会儿，我越过的是比这道更难、更危险的边界，我的朋友——而且我从来没有失手。”
在南尼斯库那颗小小的心里，显然正打着一场贪婪对贪婪的仗。他眼看着他美妙的白日梦消散进无形的天宇——一切梦都从那里来——而他不想冒任何风险。那几篇文章，此刻正由一个可靠信使全速送往伦敦，是他南尼斯库生平握过的最珍贵的东西。它们意味着荣誉、安稳、金钱——比十号如此无法无天地索要的多得多的钱。而且南尼斯库怕十号——怕他的大胆，他的勇气，他那不择手段也要把开端的事办到底的狠劲。
一万英镑！数目不小，可它出自特务经费，不出南尼斯库自己的腰包。要压下去的，只是自尊心那一点微微的刺痒：胜过十号一头的欲望，同这么狡猾的对手斗智取胜的欲望。可是，同实现南尼斯库那些美妙白日梦相比，自尊心又算得了什么？
不过他还最后讨了一次价。
“我要是付了你那一万，”他恶狠狠咒了一声说，“我有什么把握知道那些文章一定见报？”
“没有，”十号冷冷地答，“可你若不付我一万，我担保它们一定见不了报。”
听了这话，德·凯尔瓦赞先生放下书，畅快地笑了一场。
“当心，我的朋友，”他对十号说，“我们这位朋友要动火了，弄不好不等他打发完你，你就进了班房。”
“那倒要试试！”十号干巴巴地回敬，“那可是要在英国报界闹翻天的事，是不是？一个英国臣民——怎么样？”
他没有把话说下去。连南尼斯库自己也早已把这种可能性排除在算盘之外了。

===Original===

“All that our friend could do,” Number Ten went on, speaking over his shoulder to M. de Kervoisin, “would be to have me murdered, but he would find even that rather difficult. Ten thousand pounds of secret service money is considerably safer﻿—and cheaper in the end.”
Then at last Naniescu gave in. “Oh, have it your own way, curse you!” he exclaimed.
“The money now,” Number Ten said coolly, raising a warning finger. “You may as well send one of your clerks over to the bank for it. I prefer that to taking your cheque.”
Then he turned to Kervoisin, and picked up the book which the latter had thrown down on the table “Ah!” he remarked, with a total change of tone, “Marcel Proust’s latest. You are an epicure in literature, my friend.”
He fingered the book, seemingly as indifferent to what Naniescu was doing and saying, as if the whole matter of a ten thousand pound cheque did not concern him in the least.
The general had gone across to a desk which stood in the farther corner of the room. He had written out a cheque, rung the bell, and was now giving orders to a clerk to fetch the money from the Anglo-Romanian bank over the way.
On the whole he was not displeased with the transaction. The articles signed by Uno and published in the *Times* would redound to his credit, would bring him all that he had striven for all his life; and, after all, they would cost him nothing﻿—nothing at all.
Number Ten and de Kervoisin were discussing Marcel Proust; he, Naniescu, was savouring his daydreams once again; and presently when the clerk returned with a bundle of crisp English banknotes in his hand, Naniescu handed the money over to his spy-in-chief without a qualm, and certainly without regret.
“This being Monday,” Number Ten said, after he had stowed the money away in his pocketbook, “and your courier having started last night, you will probably see the first of the articles in Thursday’s *Times*. By the way,” he went on casually, “what are you doing about young Imrey and the girl?”
“What do you mean by that? What should I be doing with them?”
“Well, when these articles appear﻿—”
“I send them packing, *c’est entendu*. I never go back on my word,” Naniescu said, with a grandiose gesture.
“It would not pay you to do that in this case, my friend. Lady Tarkington has your written promise and she would raise hell if you played her false. But I wasn’t thinking of that.

===Chinese===

“咱们这位朋友充其量只能让人谋杀我，”十号扭头对德·凯尔瓦赞先生接着说，“可就是这一条他也嫌难。一万英镑特务经费，到底安全得多——到头来也便宜得多。”
南尼斯库终于让步了。“唉，随你的便，见你的鬼！”他喊道。
“现在付钱，”十号冷冷地说，竖起一根警告的手指，“你不妨派一名职员到银行去取。我宁可这样，也不要你的支票。”
然后他转向凯尔瓦赞，拈起后者丢在桌上的那本书。“啊！”他说，语气全变了，“马塞尔·普鲁斯特（Marcel Proust）最新的小说。您真是文学的鉴赏家，我的朋友。”
他翻着那本书，对南尼斯库的一举一动、一言一语浑不在意，仿佛那一万英镑支票的事同他毫不相干。
将军已走到房间远角的写字台前，开好支票，按了铃，正吩咐一名职员到对过英罗银行取钱。
总的来说，他对这桩交易并不不满。“乌诺”署名、登在《泰晤士报》上的那几篇文章将为他增光，将带给他他毕生奋斗的一切；说到底，它们不用他破费分文——分文不费。
十号和德·凯尔瓦赞正讨论马塞尔·普鲁斯特；他南尼斯库，又在咂摸他的白日梦。过了一会儿，职员手里攥着一沓崭新的英镑钞票回来了，南尼斯库毫不含糊、也决不后悔地把钱交给了他的密探头目。
“今天是星期一，”十号把钱收进皮夹子后说，“你的信使昨夜已经动身，你多半星期四就能在《泰晤士报》上看到头一篇文章。顺便问一句，”他随口接着说，“小伊姆雷和那姑娘的事，你打算怎么办？”
“你这话什么意思？我该拿他们怎么办？”
“嗯，等这几篇文章一见报——”
“我打发他们滚蛋，c'est entendu（说定了）。我说过的话从不反悔，”南尼斯库说，打了个轩昂的手势。
“这回你可不能那么办，我的朋友。塔金顿勋爵夫人手里有你的书面许诺，你若赖账她会闹翻天。不过我不是想这个。

===Original===

I only wished to warn you to keep an eye on those two young firebrands.”
“Oh,” Naniescu retorted, with a shrug, “once I have them out of the country they can do what they like. They no longer hurt me. Especially after the publication of those beautiful articles.”
“That is so, but you are sending Count and Countess Imrey out of the country aren’t you?”
“What makes you say that?”
“Well, you paid Blakeney for the articles with the title-deeds of Kis-Imre, didn’t you?”
“How did you know that?”
“I didn’t,” Number Ten replied drily. “I guessed, and you gave yourself away.”
“Well, and if I did﻿—what is it to you?”
“Nothing, my friend. Nothing. I come back to my original warning. Keep a close eye on young Imrey and Anna Heves, and above all keep a close eye on Blakeney.”
“That young blackguard?”
“Yes, that young blackguard! He may be playing a double game, you know. I suppose he is still in Cluj?”
“I thought of that,” Naniescu broke in curtly, “so I have had Imrey and the Heves girl transferred to Sót.”
“Sót? Isn’t that rather near the frontier?”
“Thirty kilometres.”
“But why Sót?”
“We have commandeered a château there, which we use as a prison for political offenders. We chose it because it stands alone in an out-of-the-way part of the country, and it saves the nuisance of public manifestations and disturbances when a prisoner who happens to have been popular is condemned. We try them by a military tribunal which holds its sittings at Sót, and if an execution is imperative﻿—well, it is done without any fuss.”
“I see. Well,” Number Ten went on, as he rose to take his leave, “I need not detain you any longer. Let me assure you,” he concluded, with his habitual sardonic smile, “that I shall not now think of interfering with any of the measures which you have adopted to stop Lady Tarkington from running after her manuscript.”
“I don’t believe that you could have interfered in any case,” Naniescu retorted gruffly.
“It is not too late, my friend. I would rather like to pit my wits against yours. So if you have repented of the bargain﻿—” And Number Ten half drew his bulging pocketbook out of his pocket.
“Oh, go to the devil!” Naniescu exclaimed, half in rage and half in laughter.
“And I hope soon to meet you in his company,” Number Ten replied, and he finally took his leave from the two men.
As soon as the door had closed on him, Naniescu turned and looked at his friend.

===Chinese===

我只是想警告你：盯紧那两个小火性子。”
“哦，”南尼斯库耸耸肩回敬，“他们一出境，爱干什么干什么。再也伤不着我了。尤其是那几篇漂亮文章见报之后。”
“那是。可你不是要把伊姆雷伯爵和伯爵夫人送出国吗？”
“你为什么这么说？”
“嗯，你付给布莱克尼文章的价钱，是小伊姆雷的地产契据，对不对？”
“你怎么知道的？”
“我不知道，”十号干巴巴地答，“我猜的，你自己露的马脚。”
“好，就算是这样——与你何干？”
“不相干，我的朋友。不相干。我不过回到我原先的警告：盯紧小伊姆雷和安娜·赫维斯，尤其要盯紧布莱克尼。”
“那个小恶棍？”
“对，那个小恶棍！他兴许在玩两面把戏呢，你知道。我想他还在克卢日？”
“我也想到了，”南尼斯库简截地插进来，“所以已经把伊姆雷和赫维斯家那姑娘转去索特了。”
“索特？不是离边界很近吗？”
“三十公里。”
“可为什么去索特？”
“我们在那里征用了一座庄园，用作政治犯的监狱。选那座庄园，是因为它孤零零坐落在偏僻地带，省得哪个犯人碰巧有人缘、被判刑时还要应付公开的示威和骚乱。军事法庭在索特开庭审他们，若非处决不可——嗯，办起来也不声不响。”
“明白了。那么，”十号起身告辞时接着说，“我就不多留你了。请相信，”他用他惯常的讥诮的微笑收尾，“我如今不会再去妨碍你为阻拦塔金顿勋爵夫人追赶她的手稿而采取的任何措施了。”
“反正你本来也妨碍不了，”南尼斯库粗声回敬。
“现在也还不晚，我的朋友。我还挺乐意同你斗一斗智。你若反悔了这桩买卖——”说着十号把鼓囊囊的皮夹子从口袋里抽出一半。
“哦，见鬼去吧！”南尼斯库喊道，一半是恼，一半是笑。
“我也盼着不久同他在一块儿时见到你，”十号答完，终于向两人告辞。
他一出门，南尼斯库就转身看他的朋友。

===Original===

But de Kervoisin had picked up his book, and gave him no encouragement to discuss the intriguing personality of Number Ten.
His face, too, was quite inscrutable. Marcel Proust was engaging his full attention. For a moment it seemed as if Naniescu would fall back on his stock phrase, or else on a string of cosmopolitan oaths; he even drew his breath ready for either; then it seemed as if words failed him.
The intriguing personality was above comment.

===Chinese===

可德·凯尔瓦赞已重新拈起书，毫不鼓励他讨论十号这个谜一样的人物。
他的脸也深不可测。马塞尔·普鲁斯特占住了他全部心神。有一刻，南尼斯库像是又要祭出他的口头禅，或是来一串世界主义的咒骂；他甚至提好了气，两样都备着；随后却像是没了词。
这个谜一样的人物，无从置喙。

## XXXVIII / 三十八
===Original===

Rosemary had never before welcomed her husband so eagerly as she did that afternoon. As soon as she heard the whirring of his motor she ran to the gates to meet him.
“What news?” she cried when he had brought the car to a standstill.
As usual, his dark eyes flashed with joy when he saw her. He jumped down and raised both her hands to his lips.
“Very vague, I am afraid,” he replied. “And some of it a mere conjecture.”
“Tell me.”
“To begin with, young Imrey and Anna Heves have been transferred to Sót.”
“Where is that?”
“Between Cluj and the frontier. It seems that there is a château there that is being used as a prison for political offenders.”
“Who told you that?”
“Naniescu. I saw him for a moment. He was very busily engaged with the Minister for Home Affairs who was over from Bucharest, so he could only give me a few minutes.”
“Had he seen Elza?”
“No. But she had applied for permission to see Philip and Anna, and he gave the permission. He supposed that she had gone on to Sót by train.”
“Even so,” Rosemary mused, “she would be back by now, or else she would have wired.”
Jasper appeared to hesitate for a moment or two, and then he said: “I don’t think that she has been allowed to do either.”
“Why not?”
“It is mere surmise, my dear,” Jasper went on quietly, “but one thing Naniescu did tell me and that was that he had on behalf of his government definitely made over the Kis-Imre estates to Peter Blakeney.”
They were walking round the house towards the veranda when he said this. Rosemary made no response; indeed, it might be thought that she had not heard, for the next question which she put to Jasper appeared irrelevant.
“Does the midnight express stop at Sót?”
“It does,” Jasper replied.
“Then, I can see Elza there. I am sure that is where she is. You inquired at Ujlak?”
“Yes, Elza went there first and then to Cluj.”
They had reached the veranda now, and Rosemary went up the steps and then into the house.
“You still wish to come with me tonight?” she asked her husband before she went upstairs.
“Why, of course.”
“You are not too tired after all this running about?”
“I?” he exclaimed with a laugh. “Tired? When it is a question of being near you!”
He tried to capture her wrist, but she evaded him and ran quickly through the hall and up the stairs.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽从来没有像那天下午那样急切地迎候过丈夫。一听见他汽车的嗡嗡声，她就跑到大门口去接他。
“有什么消息？”车子停稳后她喊道。
同往常一样，他一见她，黑眼睛里便闪出喜色。他跳下车，把她的双手举到唇边。
“恐怕很含糊，”他答。“有几分还是纯然的猜测。”
“讲给我听。”
“头一件，小伊姆雷和安娜·赫韦什被转移到索特去了。”
“那在哪儿？”
“克卢日和国境之间。那边好像有一座城堡，正用来关政治犯。”
“谁告诉你的？”
“纳尼埃斯库。我见了他一会儿。他正忙着陪从布加勒斯特来的内务部长，只能给我几分钟。”
“他见过埃尔莎吗？”
“没有。可她申请过探望菲利普和安娜，他批了。他猜她已经坐火车去索特了。”
“即便这样，”罗斯玛丽沉吟道，“她此刻也该回来了，不然也该来电报了。”
贾斯珀迟疑了一两秒，随后说：“我看，这两样都不容许她做。”
“为什么？”
“纯属推测，亲爱的，”贾斯珀平静地说下去，“可有一件事纳尼埃斯库确实告诉了我：他已代表他的政府，把基什伊姆雷的产业正式移交给彼得·布莱克尼了。”
说这话时，他们正绕过宅子往游廊走。罗斯玛丽没有答话；说实在的，人家会以为她没听见，因为她接下去问贾斯珀的那句话显得文不对题。
“午夜快车在索特停吗？”
“停。”贾斯珀答。
“那么我可以在那儿见到埃尔莎。我确信她就在那儿。乌伊拉克你问过了？”
“问过。埃尔莎先去了那儿，然后去克卢日。”
这时他们已到游廊，罗斯玛丽上了台阶，走进宅子。
“你还是要今晚同我一道走？”上楼前她问丈夫。
“那当然。”
“跑了这一整天，你不累吗？”
“我？”他笑着叫道。“累？只要是挨着你，还谈什么累！”
他想捉她的手腕，她闪开了，快步穿过大厅跑上楼去。

===Original===

Before going into her room she called down to him:
“If we use your motor we need not start before eleven o’clock, and there is still a chance of Elza being home before then.”
It was just before dinner that the culminating tragedy occurred. Rosemary was in her room, when she heard loud commotion coming from the hall﻿—harsh, peremptory voices, a word or two from Anton, and then Jasper’s voice raised as if in protest. She opened her door, and to her horror saw a squad of soldiers in the hall, and between them an officer, and a man in civilian clothes who had an official-looking paper in his hand, and was apparently explaining something to Jasper.
“I regret, my lord, but these are my orders,” the man was saying, “and I cannot enter into any discussion with you.”
Jasper tried to protest again. “But surely﻿—” he began. The man, however, cut him short.
“If you like,” he said, “I can allow you to see Count Imrey first, but this order I must deliver into his own hands.”
Rosemary in the meanwhile had run downstairs.
“What is it, Jasper?” she asked quickly.
“An order of eviction,” Jasper replied curtly, “against that wretched Maurus.”
“Whatever does that mean?”
“That he must quit this place within twenty-four hours.”
“Impossible!” she exclaimed hotly.
She turned to the officer and the civilian who had brought this monstrous order.
“The whole thing is a mistake,” she said coolly; “some error in the name. Count Imrey is a loyal subject of the King. There has never been a hint of disloyalty levelled against him.”
The officer in charge gave a curt laugh and shrugged his shoulders, and the civilian said with a sneer:
“They all say that, milady. They are all wonderfully loyal after they have been found out.”
“But General Naniescu himself is a friend of the family. And Lord Tarkington and I can vouch﻿—”
“Pardon, milady,” the civilian broke in coldly. “This affair does not concern you or Lord Tarkington, and the order of eviction is signed on behalf of the present owner of Kis-Imre by His Excellency the Governor himself.”
“On behalf﻿—”
It was Rosemary who spoke, but the sound of her voice might have come out of a grave. She had never been so near to swooning in her life.

===Chinese===

进房之前，她朝楼下喊他一句：
“要是坐你的汽车，我们十一点前动身就行，那时之前埃尔莎还有赶回来的机会。”
就在晚饭前，那场压顶的悲剧发生了。罗斯玛丽正在房里，忽然听见大厅里响起大声的骚动——刺耳的、不由分说的嗓音，安东的一两句话，随后是贾斯珀提高了的、像是抗议的声音。她打开房门，骇然看见大厅里来了一队士兵，士兵中间是一名军官，还有一个便衣，手里拿着一份官样的文书，正在向贾斯珀解释什么。
“我很遗憾，勋爵大人，可这是我的命令，”那人正说着，“我不能同您讨论。”
贾斯珀想再抗议。“可是——”他刚开口，那人就把他打断了。
“您若愿意，”他说，“我可以让您先见伊姆雷伯爵，但这道命令我必须面交他本人。”
这当儿罗斯玛丽已跑下楼来。
“什么事，贾斯珀？”她急急地问。
“一道逐出令，”贾斯珀简短地答，“针对可怜的毛罗什的。”
“那是什么意思？”
“意思是，他必须在二十四小时内离开这个地方。”
“不可能！”她激烈地叫道。
她转向带来这道骇人命令的军官和便衣。
“这整个是个误会，”她冷冷地说；“是名字弄错了。伊姆雷伯爵是国王忠顺的臣民。从来没有一丝不忠的嫌疑加在他身上。”
领队的军官干笑一声，耸耸肩；便衣讥诮地说：
“人人都这么说，夫人。等查出来了，个个都忠顺得不得了。”
“可纳尼埃斯库将军本人就是这个家的朋友。塔金顿勋爵和我都可以作保——”
“对不起，夫人，”便衣冷冷地插进来。“这件事与您和塔金顿勋爵无关，逐出令是总督阁下亲笔、代表基什伊姆雷现今的业主签署的。”
“代表——”
说话的是罗斯玛丽，可那声音像是打坟墓里出来的。她生平从没有这样接近晕倒。

===Original===

The walls around her, the woodwork, the stairs, all took on distorted shapes, and moved, round and round and up and down, until everything was a blur through which the faces of the Romanian officer and the civilian stared at her and grinned. “On behalf of the present owner of Kis-Imre!” But that was Peter! Peter! And the world did not totter, the earth did not quake, and engulf all these monstrous crimes, this cruelty and this shame!
Luckily none of the Romanians appeared to have noticed this sudden weakness in her; the civilian was consulting with the officer whether he should allow milord Tarkington to break the awful news to Maurus. Neither raised any objection, and Jasper pronounced himself ready to go. Rosemary turned appealingly to him:
“You will be very patient, Jasper,” she begged, “and very, very gentle?”
“Leave it to me, dear,” he responded; “I’ll do my best.”
When he was gone, Rosemary mechanically asked the officer and his companion to come into the smoking-room and sit down. She offered cigarettes. They made her ceremonious bows, and were as polite and conventional as circumstances demanded. She tried to talk; she even asked questions; but they were diplomatically ignorant of everything except of their duty. They explained that this consisted in seeing Count Imrey personally, and giving the eviction order into his own hands.
“It will kill him,” Rosemary said, with conviction, “or else send him out of his mind.”
Both the men shrugged. They had seen so much of this sort of thing, one of them said, people always threatened to die or to go mad, but nothing of the sort had ever happened.
“Are you quite sure of that?” Rosemary retorted.
Somehow the episode had brought back into the forefront of her consciousness her responsibility with regard to her newspaper articles. Not that conscience had been dormant, but Peter’s infamy had been such an overwhelming shock that every other emotion had slipped away into the background. But now it all came back to her. Those articles of hers if they were published would bring a justification of all this﻿—of these orders of eviction, the sort of thing that men died of, or went mad over out of grief, while officials shrugged their shoulders, having seen it all so often.
A few minutes after Jasper returned and Maurus was with him.

===Chinese===

四周的墙、木装修、楼梯，都变了扭曲的形状，转着、上下晃着，直到一切都成了一片模糊，罗马尼亚军官和那便衣的脸隔着模糊盯着她、咧着嘴笑。“代表基什伊姆雷现今的业主！”可那就是彼得！彼得！而世界竟不倾倒，大地竟不震裂，把这一切骇人的罪行、这样的残忍、这样的耻辱整个吞下去！
幸而罗马尼亚人里似乎谁也没有注意到她这阵突然的虚弱；便衣正同军官商量，该不该让塔金顿勋爵大人去向毛罗什透露这个可怕的消息。两人都没有异议，贾斯珀表示可以去。罗斯玛丽恳求般转向他：
“你要非常耐心，贾斯珀，”她央告道，“要非常、非常温和？”
“交给我吧，亲爱的，”他应道；“我尽力。”
他走后，罗斯玛丽机械地请军官和他的同伴进吸烟室坐下。她敬烟。两人朝她规规矩矩鞠躬，礼数周全得同场合要求的分毫不差。她试着攀谈；甚至问了几句；可他们对职责以外的一切都外交式地一问三不知。他们解释说，他们的职责就是面见伊姆雷伯爵，把逐出令交到他本人手里。
“这会要了他的命，”罗斯玛丽言之凿凿地说，“不然也会把他逼疯。”
两个人都耸耸肩。这类事他们见得多了，其中一个说，人人动不动就威胁说要死要疯，可这种事从来没有发生过。
“这一点你们敢担保？”罗斯玛丽回敬。
不知怎么，这段插曲把她对自己那批文章的责任，重新推到了意识的最前面。倒不是良知睡过觉，而是彼得的卑劣给她的震撼太大，别的情绪全被挤到了背后。可现在这一切都回来了。她那些文章倘若发表，就等于给这一切背书——给这些逐出令，给这种男人会为之丧命、女人会为之悲伤发疯、官员们却耸耸肩见惯了的事背书。
几分钟后贾斯珀回来了，毛罗什同他在一起。

===Original===

At sight of Maurus, Rosemary had risen from her chair as if drawn up by mechanical force, and she remained standing, staring at the man whom she had last seen as a fragile weakling, babbling incoherent words. Maurus had dressed himself with unusual care. It almost seemed as if he had been expecting visitors. Rosemary had never seen him with hair so sleekly brushed, or chin so smooth. The officer and the civilian had risen to greet him, and he went up to them with perfect calm, inquiring politely what they desired to say to him. Rosemary turned a questioning glance on Jasper. He, too, appeared puzzled, and followed Maurus’ every movement as if he dreaded that something would happen presently, and all the man’s self-possession disintegrate in a tempest of fury.
But nothing of the sort happened. Maurus took the order from the civilian, and read it through carefully. Not a muscle of his face twitched, and his hands were perfectly steady. For the moment Rosemary wondered whether this outward calm was not some form of madness.
“I can’t understand it,” she whispered to Jasper, while the three men were engaged together.
“I am just as puzzled as you are,” Jasper replied.
“So long as he is not just putting a terrible strain on himself﻿—in which case the reaction will be frightful.”
Maurus was now taking leave of the officials.
“I quite understand the position,” he said quietly. “If I had bought a house, I, too, would wish to take possession of it as soon as possible. Perhaps,” he added, with a smile, “I should not have been quite in such a hurry, but we all know that with the English time is money, eh, messieurs? And now all I need do is to thank you for your courtesy. I will comply with the order, chiefly because I have no choice.”
It was almost unbelievable. Rosemary thought that her eyes and ears must be playing her a trick. The two Romanians took their leave with their habitual elaborate politeness and Maurus himself saw them to his front door, where the squad of soldiers still stood at attention. When they had all gone, he came back into the smoking-room, and he was actually laughing when he entered.
“Did you ever see such swine?” he said lightly, and then apologised to Rosemary for his language.
“You are taking it so bravely, Maurus, dear,” Rosemary murmured bewildered. “But what about Elza?”

===Chinese===

一看见毛罗什，罗斯玛丽就像被机械的力量拉起似的从椅子里站起，直挺挺站着，盯着这个她上次见到时还是个虚弱病人、语无伦次地喃喃着的人。毛罗什把自己打扮得异乎寻常地齐整。几乎像是一直在等客人来。罗斯玛丽从没见过他头发梳得这样光顺、下巴修得这样干净。军官和便衣起身相迎，他十分平静地走上前去，客气地问两位有什么话要对他说。罗斯玛丽带着疑问望了贾斯珀一眼。他看来也一样困惑，紧盯着毛罗什的一举一动，仿佛生怕下一刻就要出事，这个人全部的镇定会在一阵狂怒里散掉。
可这类事什么也没有发生。毛罗什从便衣手里接过命令，从头到尾仔细读完。脸上没有一块肌肉抽动，双手稳稳当当。有一瞬罗斯玛丽疑心，这外表的平静莫不是疯癫的一种。
“我真不懂，”三个男人凑在一起时，她低声对贾斯珀说。
“我同你一样纳闷。”贾斯珀答。
“只要他不是在硬扛着一股可怕的劲——那样的话，回过头来的发作会吓死人。”
毛罗什这会儿正在向官员们告辞。
“我完全明白处境，”他平静地说。“要是我买下一座宅子，我也想尽早搬进去。也许，”他微笑着加了一句，“我不会这么急，可是谁都知道，对英国人来说时间就是金钱，是不是，二位？现在我只需要感谢二位的客气。我会遵令照办，主要也是因为我别无选择。”
这几乎叫人不敢相信。罗斯玛丽想，一定是自己的眼睛耳朵在捉弄她。两个罗马尼亚人带着他们惯常的那套繁縜礼貌告辞，毛罗什亲自送他们到正门——那队士兵还立正站在那里。人都走净了，他回到吸烟室，进来时居然还笑着。
“你们见过这样的猪猡么？”他轻飘飘地说，随后又为自己出言粗鲁向罗斯玛丽道歉。
“你挨得这样勇敢，亲爱的毛罗什，”罗斯玛丽困惑地喃喃道。“可是埃尔莎呢？”

===Original===

“Oh, she prepared me for it; she knew all about it yesterday, and she sent me word what to bring along in the way of clothes for her. And, of course, there will be her jewellery, and one or two little things to see to. However, I have got twenty-four hours before me, and there will be Anton and Rosa to help me.”
“But, Maurus, dear﻿—”
“You are astonished, dear Lady Tarkington,” Maurus broke in, with rather a sad smile, “to see me take it all so calmly. I was born in this house, and I always thought that I would die in it; but lately these walls have seen so much sorrow and so many villainies that I would just as soon turn my back on them.”
“But what does Elza feel about it?”
“The same as I do. She writes quite calmly.”
“When did you hear from her?”
“Early this afternoon﻿—so you see I was prepared.”
“But where is she?” Rosemary asked insistently.
“She was at Sót when she wrote to me. She had seen Philip and Anna. And she was on the point of leaving for Hódmezö. This was late last night. She is in Hungary by now﻿—and in safety. Please God I shall be with her soon.”
He still spoke quite quietly, in short, crisp sentences, with nothing of the rambling and babbling about his speech that had been so pathetic to witness yesterday. But though Rosemary ought to have felt reassured and comforted about him, she could not rid herself of a persistent feeling of dread: the same sort of feeling that invades the nerves at the manifestation of a supernatural phenomenon. There was nothing supernatural about Maurus certainly, but his attitude was so abnormal, so unlike himself, that Rosemary caught herself watching with ever-increasing anxiety for the moment when his real, violent nature would reassert itself.
A moment or two later the dinner-bell rang, and Maurus was full of apologies.
“My stupid affairs have prevented your getting on with your packing, dear Lady Tarkington. Can you forgive me?”
Rosemary could only assure him that all her packing was done. “And anyway,” she added, “as Jasper has a car we need not start before eleven o’clock.”
“Ah, then,” Maurus said, and offered her his arm to lead her into the dining-room, “we need not hurry over dinner; and I shall have the pleasure of two or three more hours of your company.”
Jasper all the while had been strangely silent.

===Chinese===

“哦，她早给我打过预防针；她昨天就全知道了，还捎话告诉我要替她带些什么衣裳。当然，还有她的首饰，还有一两件小东西要收拾。好在，我面前还有二十四个钟头，还有安东和罗莎帮我。”
“可是，亲爱的毛罗什——”
“您很惊讶，亲爱的塔金顿勋夫人，”毛罗什插进来，带着相当凄凉的笑，“看我把这一切看得这样平静。我生在这座宅子里，也一直想死在这里；可是近来这些墙看了这么多伤心事、这么多罪恶，我巴不得背过身去不看它。”
“可埃尔莎是什么感受？”
“同我一样。她写信来就平平静静的。”
“你什么时候接到她信的？”
“今天下午早些时候——所以您看，我是有备而来。”
“可她在哪儿？”罗斯玛丽追问。
“写信时她在索特。她见过菲利普和安娜了。正要动身去霍德梅泽。那是昨夜的事。此刻她已经在匈牙利了——平安了。求上帝保佑我不久也能同她在一起。”
他说话还是那样安静，短短的、干脆的句子，全无昨天那种叫人不忍听的絮叨和吃语。可尽管罗斯玛丽本该为他宽心、得到安慰，她却摆脱不掉一种执拗的惧意：就像超自然现象显现时侵上神经来的那种感觉。毛罗什身上当然毫无超自然之处，可他的态度太反常、太不像他自己了，罗斯玛丽发觉自己越来越焦灼地守着，等他真实的、暴烈的天性重新冒头的那一刻。
过了一两分钟，晚饭铃响了，毛罗什满口抱歉。
“我这些糊涂事，耽误了您收拾行李，亲爱的塔金顿勋夫人。您能原谅我吗？”
罗斯玛丽只能向他保证，行李早已收拾停当。“再说，”她补了一句，“贾斯珀有汽车，我们十一点前动身都来得及。”
“啊，那么，”毛罗什说着，把胳膊递给她，引她进饭厅，“晚饭不必赶；我还能有三两个钟头享受您的陪伴呢。”
贾斯珀一直出奇地沉默。

===Original===

Rosemary could see that he was just as much puzzled as she was, and that he was studying Maurus very keenly while the latter was talking. During dinner and while the servants were about, the conversation drifted to indifferent subject. This was the first time that Maurus had had a meal in the dining-room since he was taken ill four days ago, and he was like a child enjoying his food and delighted with everything. It was only when coffee had been brought in and the servants had gone away that he reverted to the important subject of his departure.
“My chief cause of regret, dear Lady Tarkington,” he said, “is that I cannot welcome you here when you return from your journey. But perhaps we could meet at Budapest, not? Elza speaks about that in her letter to me. She is very anxious to see you.”
“I shall break my journey at Hódmezö,” Rosemary said, “and probably wait there twenty-four hours till you come.”
She had it in her mind that she could wire from there to the *Times* office, and in any case she had to see Elza.
“There are two good hotels in Hódmezö,” Maurus rejoined. “Elza is staying at the Bristol. A very grand name for a simple provincial hotel, but it is very comfortable, I believe. Peter Blakeney’s cricket people stayed there last week, you know.”
He even could mention Peter’s name calmly; and a quaint old English saying came to Rosemary’s mind, ever her professional activities brought her in contact with extraordinary people. “Nought so queer as folk!” She almost said it aloud; for never in all her life had she witnessed anything so strange as this metamorphosis of a violent-tempered, morbid epileptic into a calm, sensible man of the world, who takes things as he finds them, and Fate’s heaviest blows without wearing his heart on his sleeve.
“I shall not forget the Bristol at Hódmezö,” she said after a little while, “and I will certainly remain with Elza until you come. Perhaps I can help her to endure the suspense.”
“Perhaps.”
“How did the letter get to you? Through the post?”
“No; she sent a peasant over from Sót, a lad who lives in Kis-Imre and was returning home. You know him, dear Lady Tarkington﻿—him and his brother﻿—the two sons of János the miller.”
“Those two brave lads who﻿—”
Rosemary paused abruptly.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽看得出，他同她一样纳闷，毛罗什说话时，他正细细地打量他。席间有仆人在场，谈话漂到了不相干的话题上。这是毛罗什四天前病倒以来头一回在饭厅用饭，他像个孩子，吃得香甜，样样都新鲜。直到咖啡端上来、仆人退下去，他才重新提起他动身这件要紧事。
“我最大的遗憾，亲爱的塔金顿勋夫人，”他说，“是您旅行回来时，我不能在这里迎您了。不过我们也许可以在布达佩斯见，不是吗？埃尔莎信里提到了这个。她急着想见您。”
“我将在霍德梅泽中途下车，”罗斯玛丽说，“多半在那儿等您二十四小时。”
她心里盘算着，从那儿可以给《泰晤士报》发电报，何况她反正也要见埃尔莎。
“霍德梅泽有两家好旅馆，”毛罗什应道。“埃尔莎住的是布里斯托尔。名字堂皇，其实就是家简朴的内地旅馆，不过我相信很舒服。彼得·布莱克尼的板球班子上星期就住在那儿，您知道。”
他居然能平静地提起彼得的名字；一句古怪的英国老话浮上罗斯玛丽心头——干她这一行，总同形形色色的奇人打交道。“人这东西，天底下最怪！”她差点说出声来；因为她平生从未见过这样奇怪的事：一个暴性子的、病态的癫痡人，竟化作一个平静、达观的世间人，随遇而安，命运最重的打击打下来也不动声色。
“霍德梅泽的布里斯托尔，我记住了，”过了一会儿她说，“我一定陪埃尔莎住到您来。也许我能帮她捱过悬望。”
“也许。”
“信怎么送到您手上的？走邮局？”
“不；她从索特托了个庄稼人捎来，一个住基什伊姆雷的小伙子，正回家来。您认识他的，亲爱的塔金顿勋夫人——他和他弟弟——磨坊主亚诺什的两个儿子。”
“那两个勇敢的小伙子，他们——”
罗斯玛丽猛地顿住了。

===Original===

The last thing she wanted to do was to bring back to Maurus’ memory that fateful night of the children’s abortive escape; but Maurus himself broke in quietly:
“Yes, the two fellows who helped us all they could that night when Philip and Anna tried to get out of the country. The attempt was unsuccessful, as you know. Philip and Anna were captured. They are in Sót now. But the two sons of János﻿—I forget their names﻿—got over the frontier safely. They joined the cricketers at Hódmezö, and are safely back at the mill now.”
“Thank God,” Rosemary exclaimed fervently, “they did not suffer for their devotion.”
“No, I am glad of that,” Maurus concluded, with obvious indifference. “But the authorities don’t trouble about the peasants. It is the landed aristocracy and the professional classes who have to suffer, if they belong to the conquered race.”
It was past ten o’clock before the small party broke up. During the latter part of the time it had been Rosemary’s turn to become silent. Maurus started the subject of politics, and Jasper carried on a desultory argument with him on that inexhaustible question. In almost weird contrast to his previous calmness, Maurus’ violent temper broke out once or twice during the course of the discussion, and it needed all Jasper’s tact and Rosemary’s soothing influence to steer clear of all that tended to aggravate him. It was the real man peeping through the armour of all the previous unnatural self-control, the gipsy blood reasserting itself﻿—self-willed, obstinate, impatient of control, bitter against humiliation. Rosemary almost welcomed the change when it came. It was more like the Maurus she knew﻿—a man eccentric and violent, walking close to but not overstepping the borderland that separates the sane from the insane. It was only when Philip, or Elza, or Kis-Imre were mentioned that he seemed to step over that borderland, encased in an armour of impish indifference.
The soldier-chauffeur brought the car round at eleven o’clock. Rosemary took affectionate leave of Maurus.
“We meet very soon,” she said. “In Hungary.”
“Yes,” he replied. “In Hungary. I shall be so thankful to be there.”
He also shook hands very cordially with Jasper.
“I am afraid this has not been a very agreeable stay for you,” he said.
“Better luck next time,” Jasper responded, as he settled himself down in the car beside his wife.

===Chinese===

她最不愿做的，就是把孩子们出逃失败的那个要命的夜晚重新带回毛罗什的记忆里；可毛罗什自己平静地接了下去：
“是的，菲利普和安娜设法出国的那天夜里，那两个小伙子尽力帮了我们的那两个。企图失败了，您知道。菲利普和安娜被抓了。他们如今在索特。可亚诺什的两个儿子——我记不得名字了——安全过了境。他们到霍德梅泽投了打板球的那些人，如今安安稳稳回到磨坊了。”
“感谢上帝，”罗斯玛丽热切地叫道，“他们的忠义没有换来苦头。”
“没有，我很高兴，”毛罗什作结，带着明显的淡漠。“当局才不找庄稼人的麻烦。要受罪的，是地主贵族和有职业的阶级——倘若他们属于被征服的种族。”
这场小小的聚会散时已过十点。后半晌轮到罗斯玛丽沉默了。毛罗什谈起政治，贾斯珀同他就这个谈不完的题目断断续续地争起来。同他先前的平静几乎古怪地相反，讨论中毛罗什的暴性子发作了一两回，全靠贾斯珀的分寸和罗斯玛丽的抚慰，才绕开了一切可能激怒他的话头。这才是透过先前那副不自然的自制铠甲露出来的真人，是重新抬头的吉普赛血——任性、执拗、受不得管束、对屈辱满怀怨毒。这变化来的时候，罗斯玛丽几乎表示欢迎。这更像她认得的那位毛罗什——一个古怪而暴烈的人，贴近却并未越过那条隔开清醒与疯癫的界线。只有提到菲利普、埃尔莎或基什伊姆雷时，他才像是一脚踏过了那条界线，裹在一副顽童似的漠然的甲胄里。
十一点，士兵司机把车开到了门前。罗斯玛丽同毛罗什深情道别。
“我们很快再见，”她说。“在匈牙利。”
“是的，”他答。“在匈牙利。到了那儿，我就谢天谢地了。”
他也同贾斯珀热络地握手。
“只怕这一趟您住得不大愉快，”他说。
“下回运气会好些，”贾斯珀应道，在妻子身旁的车座里坐定。

===Original===

The car swung out of the gates. Rosemary, looking back, had a last vision of Maurus standing under the electric lamp in the porch, his hand waving a last farewell.

===Chinese===

汽车驶出大门。罗斯玛丽回头望去，最后望见毛罗什站在门廊电灯底下，挥着手作最后的道别。

## XXXIX / 三十九
===Original===

Rosemary must have fallen asleep in the corner of the carriage, for she woke with a start. The train had come to a halt, as it had done at two or three stations since Cluj was left behind. So it was not the sudden jerk, or the sound of the exhaust from the engine, that had caused Rosemary suddenly to sit up straight, wide awake and with that vague feeling of apprehension which comes on waking when sleep has been unconsciousness rather than rest. Jasper sat in the other corner with his eyes closed, but Rosemary did not think that he was asleep. They had a sleeping compartment, but hadn’t had the beds made up; it was perhaps less restful for the night journey, but distinctly cleaner. The carriage was in semidarkness, only a feeble ray of blue light filtered through the shade that tempered the gaslight up above.
Rosemary pulled up the blind. They were at a small station dimly lighted by one oil-lamp above the exit door. A clump of acacia trees in full leaf effectually hid the name of the station from view. A couple of soldiers stood at the door through which a number of peasants, men with bundles and women with baskets, one or two Jews in long gabardines and a prosperous-looking farmer in town clothes and top-boots were filing out. Someone blew a tin-trumpet, a couple more soldiers stalked up the line in the direction of the engine. There was a good deal of shouting.
Rosemary drew the blind down again, and tried to settle herself comfortably in her corner once more. But sleep would not come. She looked at her watch. It was past two. This seemed an unconscionably long halt, even for a train in this part of the world. Rosemary peeped again behind the blind. The station appeared quite deserted now except for the two soldiers on guard at the door. Everything seemed very still﻿—of that peculiar stillness which always seems so deep when a train comes to a halt during the night away from a busy station, and all the more deep by contrast with the previous ceaseless rumbling of the wheels. From the direction of the engine there came the sound of two men talking. Otherwise nothing.
Rosemary reckoned that they should be over the frontier soon, but, of course, if they were going to have these interminable halts﻿—
Half an hour went by. Even the distant hum of conversation had ceased, and the silence was absolute.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽准是在车厢角落里睡着了，因为她惊醒过来时吓了一跳。火车停了，自从离开克卢日，它已在两三个站停过。所以惊醒罗斯玛丽的，既不是那突来的一顿，也不是机车排气的声音——那使她猛然坐直、彻底醒来、心里带着醒来时那种说不清的忧惧的，另有所故；那种睡眠是失去知觉，不是休息。贾斯珀坐在另一角，闭着眼，可罗斯玛丽不认为他睡了。他们包的是卧车包厢，却没有铺床；夜车这样也许不好歇，却干净得多。车厢里半明半暗，只有一缕微弱的蓝光从上头罩住煤气灯的灯罩里滤下来。
罗斯玛丽拉起卷帘。他们停在一个小站，只有出口门上一盏油灯昏昏地照着。一丛叶叶满枝的刺槐把站名挡得严严实实。两个士兵站在门边，一伙庄稼汉正鱼贯而出——扛包裹的男人，挎篮子的女人，一两个穿长罩袍的犹太人，还有一个穿进城衣裳、蹬长统靴的殷实庄稼汉。有人吹响了锡喇叭，又有两个士兵朝机车方向沿线路走去。喊声乱成一片。
罗斯玛丽放下卷帘，想再在角落里安顿下来。可睡意不来了。她看了看表。已过两点。停车停得这么没个头，即便在这一带的火车，也未免太不像话。罗斯玛丽又从帘后偷看了一眼。车站此刻相当空，只剩把门的两个士兵。一切都非常静——火车夜里停在远离繁忙大站的时分，总有那种特别的静，格外深；同先前车轮不停的隆隆相比，显得更深。机车那边传来两个人说话的声音。此外什么也没有。
罗斯玛丽算计着他们该很快过界了；当然，要是老这么没完没了地停——
半个钟头过去了。连远处的人语嗡嗡也停了，寂静彻底了。

===Original===

Feeling unaccountably agitated rather than nervous, Rosemary called to Jasper. At once he opened his eyes.
“What is it, my dear?” he asked vaguely. “Where are we?” And he added, with a shake of his long, lean body: “These carriages are deuced uncomfortable.”
“We are at a small station, Jasper,” Rosemary said. “And we’ve been here over half an hour. Have you been asleep?”
“I remember this confounded train pulling up. I must have dropped off to sleep after that. I wonder where we are.”
“We can’t be very far from the frontier. I thought at first they would turn us out for the customs, or passports or something. But nothing has happened, and we don’t seem to be getting on. I do hope there has not been a breakdown on the line.”
“My dear!” Jasper exclaimed, rather impatiently, “why in the world should you think that there is a breakdown on the line? There’s a signal against us, I suppose. That’s all.”
But Rosemary was not satisfied. “Do you mind,” she said, “seeing if you can get hold of anybody. I can’t help feeling nervous and﻿—”
At once Jasper was on his feet, courteous, attentive as always. “Of course I’ll go and see, my darling,” he said. “But it’s not like you to be nervous.”
He drew back the shade so as to get a little light into the carriage, straightened his clothes, then went out into the corridor. Everything was so still that Rosemary could hear his footstep treading the well-worn strip of carpet, then the opening of the carriage door, which sent a welcome draught of air through the stuffy compartment. Rosemary pulled up the blind, and leaned out the window. It was pitch-dark, though the sky was starry. The small oil-lamp still flickered over the exit door, and the two soldiers were still there. Rosemary saw Jasper’s vague silhouette in the gloom. He stood for a moment looking up and down the line; then he walked away in the direction of the engine. A few minutes went by, and presently Rosemary saw Jasper coming back, accompanied by the guard.
“What is it, Jasper?” she called. “Where are we, and what has happened?”
The two men had come to a halt immediately beneath her window. The guard doffed his cap at sight of her, and scratched his head in obvious perplexity.
“We are at Sót, my darling, but I have bad news for you, I am afraid,” Jasper said. “There has been a very serious landslide lower down the line. I suppose it is due to the heavy storms.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽心里说不清地发慌——不是害怕，是激动——喊了贾斯珀。他立刻睁开眼。
“怎么了，亲爱的？”他茫然地问，“我们到哪儿了？”又抖了抖那瘦长的身子，加一句：“这些车厢难受死了。”
“我们停在一个小站上，贾斯珀，”罗斯玛丽说，“已经停了半个多钟头。你睡着了？”
“我记得这该死的火车停下，后来准是睡着了。也不知我们到哪儿了。”
“离边界不会太远。我起初还当他们要叫我们下车办海关、查护照什么的。可什么动静也没有，车也不见往前走。千万别是线路上出了故障。”
“亲爱的！”贾斯珀颇不耐烦地喊，“你怎么无缘无故想什么线路故障？多半是信号把我们拦了。就这么点事。”
可罗斯玛丽不放心。“你能不能，”她说，“去看看能不能找到人。我忍不住发慌，而且——”
贾斯珀立刻起身，一如既往地殷勤周到。“我这就去看，亲爱的，”他说，“可你发慌可不像你。”
他拉开灯罩，好让车厢里有点亮，理了理衣裳，走出去到走廊。四周静得罗斯玛丽听得见他踩过磨旧的地毯条的脚步声、开车厢门的声音——一阵可喜的凉风灌进这闷气的包厢。罗斯玛丽拉起卷帘，探身窗外。天漆黑，星空倒亮。那盏小油灯还在出口门上忽闪，两个士兵还在。罗斯玛丽看见昏暗里贾斯珀模糊的身影。他站了一会儿，朝线路两头望了望；随后朝机车方向走去。过了几分钟，罗斯玛丽看见贾斯珀回来了，身后跟着列车员。
“怎么了，贾斯珀？”她喊，“我们到哪儿了，出什么事了？”
两人就在她窗下停住。列车员一见她便脱帽，又困惑地挠头。
“我们到索特了，亲爱的。不过恐怕有坏消息要告诉你，”贾斯珀说，“前方线路塌方了，很严重。多半是那几场暴雨闹的。

===Original===

Anyway, the line is blocked for a distance of nearly half a kilometre, and of course there will be considerable delay. I don’t understand all the man says, but it seems to have been a terrible catastrophe.”
But out of all this only two words had penetrated Rosemary’s brain﻿—“considerable delay.” What did that mean? She asked the guard, but he only shook his head. He didn’t know. He didn’t know anything except that there had been a landslide, and that no train could get through till the line was clear. He supposed that a gang would come down in the morning, but he couldn’t say. Rosemary wanted to know whether there would be any other way of continuing the journey and picking up a train the other side of the frontier. The guard again shook his head. He really couldn’t say; he was a stranger to these parts, but perhaps in the morning﻿—He suggested respectfully that the gracious lady should allow him to make up a couple of beds in two of the sleeping compartments. There was no one else on the train, so﻿—
“No one else on the train?” Rosemary broke in curtly. “What does he mean, Jasper? There must be other passengers on the train. Where have they gone to?”
Jasper put the question to the guard.
“The last of the passengers got out at this station, gracious lady. When it was known that the line was blocked this side of the frontier, no one took a ticket further than Sót.”
“How do you mean? When was it known that the line was blocked?”
“Before we left Cluj, gracious lady, and so﻿—”
“But they sold us tickets to Budapest, and said nothing about a breakdown,” Rosemary exclaimed. And then she turned to her husband: “Jasper, tell me, is this man a fool or a liar, or am I half-witted? You took our tickets to Budapest. Did the man at the ticket-office say anything to you about a block on the line?”
“No,” Jasper replied, “he did not.”
“But our luggage?”
“We have no registered luggage﻿—only what we have with us in the carriage.”
“Of course, how stupid of me! But when the man clipped your ticket?”
“He didn’t say anything.”
Rosemary, impatient, her nerves on edge, turned again to the guard. “You saw the gracious gentleman’s tickets,” she said, “when we got into the train. Why didn’t you warn us?”
“I thought perhaps the gracious lady and gentleman would only go as far as Sót and sleep there. I thought everyone knew about the landslide, and that every passenger had been warned.”

===Chinese===

总之，线路堵了将近半公里，当然要耽搁很久。这人的话我没全听懂，可看样子是场大灾。”
可这一切里，只有两个字钻进罗斯玛丽的脑子——“耽搁很久”。那是什么意思？她问列车员，他只摇头。不知道。他什么都不知道，只知道塌了方，线路不通之前哪趟车也过不去。他想早晨会来一拨人，可也说不准。罗斯玛丽想知道有没有别的法子接着走，到边界那头再搭火车。列车员又摇头。实在说不上来；他也不是本地人，或许早晨——他恭恭敬敬地建议，仁慈的夫人容他去两个卧车包厢铺两张床。车上再没有别人了，所以——
“车上再没有别人？”罗斯玛丽简截地打断，“他什么意思，贾斯珀？车上一定还有旁的乘客。他们哪儿去了？”
贾斯珀把问题转给列车员。
“最后一批乘客就在本站下的车，仁慈的夫人。边界这边线路堵了的消息传开之后，谁也不买过索特的票了。”
“怎么说？什么时候传开的？”
“我们离开克卢日之前，仁慈的夫人，所以——”
“可他们把票卖给我们到布达佩斯，只字没提故障，”罗斯玛丽喊道。随即她转向丈夫：“贾斯珀，告诉我，这人是个傻瓜还是骗子？还是我傻了？我们的票是你买的。售票处那人跟你提过线路不通吗？”
“没有，”贾斯珀答，“没提。”
“那我们的行李呢？”
“我们没有托运行李——只有随身带在车厢里的。”
“当然，我真傻！可那人剪你票的时候呢？”
“他什么也没说。”
罗斯玛丽不耐烦，神经绷着，又转向列车员。“我们上车的时候，你见过仁慈的先生的票，”她说，“为什么不提醒我们？”
“我以为仁慈的夫人先生们只到索特，就在这儿过夜。我以为塌方的事人人都知道，乘客个个都得了信儿。”

===Original===

“Can we get a car here that will take us to Hódmezö?”
“Not at this hour, gracious lady.”
“Or a vehicle of any sort?”
The guard shook his head. Rosemary could have screamed with impatience until Jasper’s quiet voice broke in: “I think, my dear, that by far the best thing to do will be to let the man make up a couple of beds for us, and to try and possess ourselves in patience until the morning. There is nothing to be done﻿—really, darling, nothing. And, after all, it may only mean a delay of eight to ten hours.”
Then, as Rosemary remained silent, making no further objection, he slipped some money into the guard’s hand, and told him to get the beds ready. After that he reentered the carriage, and rather diffidently sat down beside his wife.
“I feel terribly guilty, dear one,” he said humbly, “but you know I don’t speak Romanian very well, and when these sort of people jabber away, I don’t always understand what they say. And I was rather anxious about you at Cluj. You seemed so agitated, so unlike yourself.”
“Can you wonder? Twenty-four hours’ delay may mean that Naniescu’s courier will get to London and make arrangements before I have time to wire. I must see Elza first, and in the meanwhile﻿—”
“My darling,” Jasper put in, with a quick, wearied sigh, “it is not like you to be so illogical. Do you really suppose that events move at such a rate in a newspaper office? There is bound to be delay﻿—and there’s ample time for your telegram to reach the *Times* before the editor has even thought of inserting your articles. Even if we are held up here for twenty-four hours, you can see Elza and send your wire from Hódmezö before Peter Blakeney, or whoever Naniescu’s courier happens to be, can possibly have made any arrangements with the *Times*.”
“Of course, dear, of course,” Rosemary said, more calmly. “I am stupid tonight. This whole business has got on my nerves, I suppose. I don’t seem to know what I am doing.”

===Chinese===

“这儿能找辆车迭我们去霍德梅泽吗？”
“这个时辰不行，仁慈的夫人。”
“随便什么车呢？”
列车员摇头。罗斯玛丽急得几乎要喊出来，贾斯珀平静的嗓音插了进来：“亲爱的，我看最好的办法，莫过于让这人给我们铺两张床，耐下心等到天亮。没别的办法——实在没别的，亲爱的。说到底，也许不过耽搁八到十个钟头。”
见罗斯玛丽沉默、不再反对，他把几张钞票塞进列车员手里，叫他去铺床。随后他回到车厢里，有点讪讪地在妻子身边坐下。
“我心里过意不去得很，亲爱的，”他谦卑地说，“可你知道我罗马尼亚话说得不好，这号人叽里呱啦起来，我不是句句都懂。在克卢日我又为你悬心。你那时那么激动，全不像你。”
“还用说吗？耽搁二十四个钟头，也许南尼斯库的信使就赶在我来得及拍电报之前到了伦敦，把一切都安排好了。我必须先见埃尔莎，而这一来——”
“亲爱的，”贾斯珀插进来，发出一声急促而疲倦的叹，“你这么不讲逻辑，可不像你。你真以为报馆里的事办得这么快？必有周折——你的电报尽来得及在主编还没想到刊你文章之前到达《泰晤士报》。就算我们在这儿耗二十四个钟头，你也可以见到埃尔莎、从霍德梅泽把电报发出去，赶在彼得·布莱克尼——或者随便哪个南尼斯库的信使——来得及同《泰晤士报》做任何安排之前。”
“当然，亲爱的，当然，”罗斯玛丽说，平静了些，“今晚我真蠢。这整个事情闹的，我想。我简直不知道自己在干什么。”

## XL / 四十
===Original===

On the narrow made-up bed, with the coarse linen and the heavy blanket, and the smell of sulphur and dust about her, Rosemary found it quite impossible to get any rest. At first there had been a good deal of clumsy shunting, the engine probably had been detached, the tin-trumpet sounded at intervals, and there was a good deal of shouting; but all these noises ceased presently, and the night seemed peculiarly still. Still, but not restful. Rosemary could not sleep. Fortunately the communicating doors between her compartment and the one which Jasper occupied were closed, so she felt free to fidget, to get up or to lie down as the mood seized her, to turn on the light to read or to meditate, without fear of disturbing him.
She could not help feeling desperately nervous. Jasper, of course, was quite right: there was plenty of time in which to see Elza, and then to send a telegram to London if necessary, so there was nothing in a few hours’ delay to worry about. Nevertheless she, who had always prided herself on independence and level-headedness, felt a strange kind of foreboding﻿—something vague and indefinite that nevertheless was terrifying. She tried to compose herself and could not. She forced herself into quietude, deliberately kept her eyes closed and her body still. It was torture, but she did it because she wanted to feel that she still controlled her nerves, and that she was not giving way to this stupid sense of fear.
And there was no denying it; the fear that beset her was on account of her coming interview with Elza. Maurus’ attitude had been very strange, even abnormal, and it was consequent on a letter from Elza. And Rosemary, though she had not owned it to herself before, felt a growing conviction that Elza’s lofty patriotism had given way at last to mother-love. Confronted with Philip and Anna, who no doubt had youth’s passionate desire to live, with Anna’s mother who was all for conciliating the tyrants, and with Maurus whose reason was threatening to give way, Elza had laid down her arms, had capitulated and decided that her son’s life must be saved at any cost. Perhaps she knew that Rosemary’s articles had fallen into Naniescu’s hands, perhaps she and Peter had actually been in collusion over the theft, perhaps﻿—perhaps﻿—There was no end to conjecture, and no limit to Rosemary’s dread of what the next four-and-twenty hours would bring.

===Chinese===

在那张狭窄的硬铺上，粗麻布床单、沉重的毯子，四周一股硫磺和尘土的气味，罗斯玛丽觉得根本无法歇息。起初还有好一阵笨拙的调车，机车多半是摘走了，锡喇叭不时响着，还有许多喊叫；可这些声响不久都停了，夜显得格外静。静，却不宁。罗斯玛丽睡不着。好在她这间包房同贾斯珀那间之间的连通门关着，她尽可以随意辗转，想坐就坐、想躺就躺，想开灯看书或冥想都行，不怕搅扰他。
她没法不感到心慌意乱得厉害。贾斯珀当然说得对：见埃尔莎有的是时间，必要时再往伦敦发封电报也来得及，所以耽搁几个钟头没有什么可愁的。然而她——一向以自立和头脑冷静自矜的她——却感到一种奇怪的预感：一样模糊而说不清、却叫人害怕的东西。她想定下心来，定不下。她强迫自己安静，故意闭着眼、一动不动地躺着。这是受刑，可她照做，因为她要觉得自己仍管得住神经，没有向这股愚蠢的恐惧感屈服。
无可否认：缠着她的这股恐惧，是为了即将同埃尔莎的会面。毛罗什的态度很古怪，甚至反常，而那是埃尔莎一封信的结果。罗斯玛丽虽然从前不曾对自己承认，此刻却越来越确信：埃尔莎那高贵的爱国心，终于向母爱让了步。面对着无疑怀着青春的求生热望的菲利普和安娜，面对着一心要同暴君们和解的安娜的母亲，面对着理智眼看要垮的毛罗什，埃尔莎放下了武器，投降了，决定不惜任何代价救下儿子的命。也许她已知道罗斯玛丽的文章落到了纳尼埃斯库手里；也许她同彼得在偷稿这件事上真有勾结；也许——也许——猜想没有个完，罗斯玛丽对这未来二十四小时会带来什么的恐惧也没有个边。

===Original===

Only now did she realise what it had meant to her to place the final decision into Elza’s hands. With it she had given her professional honour, her very conscience into another woman’s keeping. She had probably only done it because she was so sure of Elza, of Elza’s patriotism and her sense of justice and honour. Poor Elza! Who could blame her for being weak, for being a mother rather than a patriot? She should never have been placed before such a cruel alternative. Self-reproach, the stirrings of conscience helped to aggravate Rosemary’s racking anxiety. She got up in the early dawn, made what sketchy toilet the limited accommodation allowed, and went out into the open. The little station appeared quite deserted; only the two soldiers were still there on duty at the exit door. Rosemary marvelled if they were the same two who had been there during the night. They looked perfectly stolid, unwashed and slouchy in their faded, coarse-looking uniforms and dusty boots and képis.
Rosemary looked up and down the line. The train, consisting of half a dozen coaches, looked derelict without its engine, and there was no guard in sight. She had no eyes for the beautiful scenery around﻿—the narrow valley bordered by densely wooded heights; the mountainside covered with oak and beech that were just beginning to clothe themselves in gold and at the approach of autumn; the turbulent little mountain-stream; the small station nestling amidst gnarled acacia trees; and on the right the quaint Transylvanian village with the hemp-thatched roofs and bunches of golden maize drying in the sun, with its primitive stuccoed church and whitewashed presbytery. Rosemary saw nothing of this; her eyes searched the landscape for the château﻿—now a prison for political offenders﻿—where Philip and Anna were detained﻿—those children whose safety would be paid for perhaps by countless miseries, by worse tyranny and more cruel oppression. But there was no large building in sight, and presently Rosemary caught sight of Jasper, some way up the line, walking toward her in company with a man in very negligee toilet, who probably was the stationmaster.
At sight of Rosemary, Jasper hastened to meet her, while the man kept at a respectful distance.
“What news?” Rosemary cried eagerly.
Jasper appeared dejected. “Not very good I am afraid,” he said.

===Chinese===

直到此刻她才明白，把最后的决定交到埃尔莎手里，对她意味着什么。随它一起交出去的，还有她的职业荣誉、她自己的良知，都托给另一个女人保管。她之所以那样做，多半正因为对埃尔莎那样有把握——对埃尔莎的爱国心、她的正义感和荣誉感。可怜的埃尔莎！谁能怪她软弱、怪她做了母亲而没有做爱国者呢？本就不该把她摆到这样残忍的两难面前。自责和良心的悸动，火上浇油地加重了罗斯玛丽的煎熬。拂晓时分她起来，就着有限的设备草草梳洗，走到露天去。小站显得空无一人；只有那两个士兵还守在出站口。罗斯玛丽纳闷，他们还是夜里那两个么。他们看上去全然木然，没洗的脸，萎靡不振，褪色的粗制服、蒙尘的靴子和军帽。
罗斯玛丽沿着铁路两头张望。列车有六七节车厢，没了机车，像被遗弃了，连个列车员也看不见。四下美丽的景色她一概视而不见——两侧密林夹峙的窄谷；满山的橡树山毛榉正待披金、迎接秋天；湍急的山溪；偎在虬曲洋槐间的小站；右边那座古色古香的特兰西瓦尼亚村庄，大麻草的屋顶，一束束金黄的玉米在太阳下晾晒，还有那座灰泥抹的原始教堂和粉白的神父宅。这些罗斯玛丽一样也没看见；她的眼睛在景色里搜寻那座城堡——如今的政治犯监狱——菲利普和安娜被关押的地方——那两个孩子，他们的安全也许要用数不清的苦难、更坏的暴政和更残酷的压迫来偿付。可望不见什么大建筑；不一会儿，罗斯玛丽望见贾斯珀在铁路那头一段路外，正同一个衣着十分邋遢的男人朝她走来，那多半是站长。
一见罗斯玛丽，贾斯珀快步迎上来，那男人恭恭敬敬地隔着一段距离。
“什么消息？”罗斯玛丽急切地喊。
贾斯珀显得丧气。“恐怕不大好。”他说。

===Original===

“The stationmaster here tells me that he has been advised that the line will take the whole of the day to clear﻿—probably more.”
“Very well, then,” Rosemary said resolutely, “we must get a car.”
“Impossible, my dear; you can’t get across if the road is blocked.”
“All the roads in Transylvania are not blocked, I imagine,” Rosemary retorted drily. Then she called to the stationmaster: “I want,” she said, “to get to Hódmezö today. Can I get a car anywhere in Sót?”
“But the roads are impassable, gracious lady,” the man exclaimed; “the landslide﻿—”
“Never mind about the landslide. There are other roads in Transylvania besides this one. I can go by a roundabout way, but I can get there somehow if I have a car. Or,” she added impatiently, seeing that the man was looking very dubious, “a conveyance of any sort, I don’t care what it is.”
“Alas! gracious lady, that is just the trouble. The soldiers were here yesterday, and they commandeered all the horses and bullocks in Sót for military purposes. It is so hard,” the man went on, muttering half to himself; “no sooner does a man scrape together a little money and buy an old horse, then the soldiers come down and take it away from him.”
The man was full of apologies and explanations, but somehow Rosemary had the impression that he lied. He rambled on for a while in the same strain; Rosemary did not hear him. Her brain was at work trying to find a way to combat this net of intrigue that was hemming her in. She was quite sure that the man was lying﻿—that Naniescu had ordered these ignorant yokels to tell the lies that suited him. She, Rosemary, Lady Tarkington, a British subject, could not be held up at the frontier, of course, but there could be a landslide, a block on the line, no conveyance available, horses commandeered by the military, two, three, perhaps four days’ delay while Naniescu’s courier was speeding to London with Rosemary’s manuscript and her letter to the editor of the *Times* asking for early publication.
She turned with some impatience to Jasper.
“What shall I do?”
Gravely he shook his head.
“Accept the inevitable,” he replied gently. “I understand that there is quite a clean little hotel in the place, and twenty-four hours’ delay is not very serious, is it?”
“It would not be,” she admitted, “if it were not prolonged.”
“It can’t be prolonged indefinitely.”

===Chinese===

“这儿的站长告诉我，他接到通知，线路要一整天才能抢通——多半还不止。”
“那好，”罗斯玛丽断然说，“我们得找一辆汽车。”
“不可能，亲爱的；路要是堵了，你也过不去。”
“特兰西瓦尼亚的路总不见得条条都堵了吧。”罗斯玛丽干巴巴地回敬。随后她唤过站长：“我要今天赶到霍德梅泽。索特哪儿能弄到一辆车吗？”
“可是路不通啊，仁慈的夫人，”那人叫道；“塌方——”
“塌方不用管。特兰西瓦尼亚又不是只有这一条路。我可以绕道走，可只要有一辆车，我总能想法子到。或者，”见那人一脸狐疑，她不耐烦地补了一句，“随便什么代步的家什都行，我不管它是什么。”
“唉！仁慈的夫人，难就难在这里。昨天大兵来过，把索特的马和牛都征去军用。真是难啊，”那人半自言自语地咕哝下去；“一个人好不容易攢下点钱买了匹老马，大兵一来就牵走了。”
那人满口道歉和解释，可罗斯玛丽不知怎么总觉得他在撒谎。他还是那一套，絮絮地说个不停；罗斯玛丽没有听。她的脑子正在开动，想找出一条路来，冲破这张把她围住的罗网。她十分肯定这人在撒谎——是纳尼埃斯库指使这些无知的乡巴佬说合他心意的谎。她，罗斯玛丽，塔金顿勋夫人，一个英国臣民，当然不能被扣在边境上；可是可以塌方，可以断路，可以没有车，可以马匹被征，可以耽误两天、三天、也许四天——趁着这个当口，纳尼埃斯库的信使正揣着罗斯玛丽的手稿和她给《泰晤士报》主编请早日刊发的信，兼程赶往伦敦。
她带着几分不耐烦转向贾斯珀。
“我该怎么办？”
他沉重地摇头。
“接受不可避免的事吧，”他温和地答。“我了解到本地有一家挺干净的小旅馆，耽误二十四小时不算太要紧，是不是？”
“要是不再拖下去，”她承认，“就不算。”
“总不能无限期拖下去吧。”

===Original===

“No,” she retorted, “for I can always walk to the frontier.”
“Over mountain passes?” he queried, with a smile.
But she only gave a scornful shrug. “Accept the inevitable?” How little he knew her. The more she saw difficulties ahead, the more she felt ready for a fight. Time was still in her favour. Hódmezö was not far with its telegraph service, and Naniescu’s power did not extend beyond the frontier.
Always supposing that Elza did wish her to wire.
Rosemary thought tings over for a moment or two; then she said to Jasper: “Very well! I’ll possess myself in patience for twenty-four hours. Will you see about rooms at the hotel? And I suppose this man will see about our luggage being taken across?”
“Of course I’ll see to everything, dear,” Jasper said meekly. “But you would like some breakfast, wouldn’t you?”

===Chinese===

“是不能，”她回敬，“因为我总可以走到边境去。”
“翻山口？”他含笑问。
她只轻蔑地耸了耸肩。“接受不可避免的事？”他多么不了解她。眼前的困难越多，她越觉得有一仗好打。时间仍在她一边。霍德梅泽不远，那儿有电报局，纳尼埃斯库的权力也过不了国境。
——前提始终是，埃尔莎当真要她发电报。
罗斯玛丽把事情掂量了一两秒；随后她对贾斯珀说：“很好！我按捺着性子等二十四小时。你去张罗旅馆的房间好不好？行李搬运的事，我想这个人会照应的？”
“我当然会把一切办妥，亲爱的，”贾斯珀温顺地说。“不过你想用点早餐吧？”

## XLI / 四十一
===Original===

Meekly and obediently Jasper went off to see after the luggage, and Rosemary wandered away as far as the village. Her first thought was to ascertain definitely whether indeed there was no chance of hiring some sort of conveyance to take her as far as Hódmezö. The first man she spoke to was the keeper of the inevitable grocery store. He had heard a rumour that there was a block on the railway line somewhere near the frontier, and this annoyed him very much because he was expecting a consignment of maize from Hungary, and he supposed that he would not now get it for two or three days. He had no horse. Hadn’t had one since the beginning of the war, when his nag was commandeered. Now even an old crock was so dear it did not pay to buy.
Rosemary asked him if he knew of anyone in the village from whom she could hire a horse and cart to take her as far as the frontier, but the man shook his head. The Jew at the hotel had two horses, and the priest had one, but the military were down from the barracks yesterday and took those away. There were manoeuvres in progress somewhere, it seems. The soldiers said they would bring the horses back in two or three days, but it was very hard and inconvenient for everybody when that sort of thing was done.
Rosemary asked, what about oxen? But draft-oxen and some buffaloes belonging to the mayor had also been commandeered. It was very hard. Did not the gracious lady think so?
Finally the storekeeper made a suggestion that with the help of a little baksheesh the gracious lady might succeed in getting the officer at the château to let her have what she wanted. The château was only a couple of kilometres from the village. It lay close to the road; the gracious lady couldn’t fail to spy the great iron gates. It had belonged at one time to Count Fekete, but the family had been gone some time, and the château was now a cavalry barrack, and some prisoners of war were still kept there.
The storekeeper offered his son as an escort to the gracious lady, so that she should not miss her way. But Rosemary declined the offer; she purchased a few stale biscuits from the man, intending to ask for a glass of milk from some cottage on the way; then she set out at a brisk pace down the road.

===Chinese===

贾斯珀温顺听话地照着行李去了，罗斯玛丽则一路走到村子那头。她头一个念头，是切实弄清到底还有没有可能雇到什么运载工具送她去霍德梅泽。她开口问的头一个人，是那家必不可少就有的杂货铺老板。他听说了传闻，说边界附近的铁路哪里堵了；这使他非常恼火，因为他正等着一批匈牙利来的玉米，估摸着这下子三两天到不了手了。他没有马。打战争一开打就没有了，他那匹小马被征走了。如今连一匹老弱牲口都贵得不值得买。
罗斯玛丽问他，村里有谁的马和车可以雇，送她到边界，那人摇头。旅店的犹太人有两匹马，神父有一匹，可昨天兵营里下来人，都牵走了。看样子什么地方正在演习。士兵们说两三天就把马送回来，可这种事一干起来，人人都不方便，苦得很。
罗斯玛丽问，牛呢？可市长家的挽牛和几头水牛也被征走了。真是苦啊。仁慈的夫人不这样想吗？
末了，铺主出了个主意：使点小费，仁慈的夫人说不定能说动庄园里的军官，要什么给什么。庄园离村子只有两公里，就在大路边上；仁慈的夫人准能一眼望见那两扇大铁门。它从前是费克特伯爵家的，可那一家人走了有些年头了，庄园如今是骑兵营房，还关着些战俘。
铺主要派儿子给仁慈的夫人领路，免得走岔。罗斯玛丽谢绝了；向那人买了几块走味的饼干，打算路上到哪家村舍讨杯牛奶；便迈开轻快的步子顺路去了。

===Original===

It ran along the mountainside, and some fifty feet below the turbulent little stream tossed and tumbled over stones and boulders, its incessant murmuring making a soothing accompaniment to Rosemary’s thoughts. At the last cottage in the village, where Rosemary had obtained a glass of fresh milk from a comely peasant woman, the latter had directed her to a mountain path which ran below the road, parallel with it, and close to the edge of the stream. Here it was perfectly lovely; the moist, sweet air, the occasional call of birds, the beech and oak and dense undergrowth, the carpet of moss, the occasional clearing where the grass was a luscious green, and the mauve campanula grew to a stately height. At times the path rose sharply, twenty feet or more above the stream; at others it ran level with the water’s edge; and at one place the stream widened into a little bay, where the water was as clear as a fairy pool and of a translucent blue.
Rosemary lingered for a little while beside the pool, thinking how delicious it would be to bathe in it. When she went on again she came to a sharp bend in the path, and as soon as she had rounded this she saw some twenty yards farther on a man dressed in the uniform of a Romanian officer, sitting upon a tree stump close by the water’s edge. The man sat with his elbows resting on his knees, and his head was buried in his hands. He looked like a man in trouble. Rosemary walked on, a dry twig crackled under her tread, and the man suddenly looked up.
It was Peter.
The moment he caught sight of Rosemary he jumped up, and then made a movement as if he meant to run away. But Rosemary, with sudden impulse, called to him at once:
“Don’t go, Peter.”
It seemed as if the magic of her voice rooted him to the spot. He stood quite still, but with his back to her; and then he took off his képi with one hand, and passed the other once or twice across his forehead.
Rosemary felt strangely disturbed and puzzled. Why was Peter here? How did he come to be here? And in this uniform?
“Aren’t you going to speak to me, Peter?” she asked, because Peter being here seemed so amazing that for the moment she thought that she was seeing a vision; “or even look at me?” she added.
“I did not suppose you particularly wished me to speak to you,” he said, without turning round to face her.
“Why should you say that?” she asked simply.

===Chinese===

路沿山腰走，五十来英尺底下，一条湍急的小溪在石头和巨砾上翻腾摔打，它不停的呜咽，为罗斯玛丽的思绪作了一路抚慰的伴奏。在村里最后一家农舍——罗斯玛丽在那儿向一位秀气的农妇讨了一杯新鲜牛奶——农妇指给她一条山路，在公路下头，同它平行，紧贴溪边。这里美极了：湿润香甜的空气，偶尔的鸟鸣，山毛榉、橡树和茂密的下木，铺地的青苔，间或一片林间空地，草绿得油润，淡紫的风铃草长到齐腰高。小路时而陡起，升到溪上二十多英尺；时而又同水面齐平；有一处溪面展宽成一个小小的水湾，水清得像仙池，透着碧蓝。
罗斯玛丽在水湾边流连了片刻，想着在里头洗个澡该多美。再往前走，小路一个急拐，刚转过弯，她就看见二十来码外一个穿罗马尼亚军官制服的男人，坐在紧靠水边的一个树桩上。那人两肘支膝，头埋在两手间，一副心事重重的样子。罗斯玛丽往前走，一根枯枝在脚下咔嚓一响，那人猛地抬头。
是彼得。
一看见罗斯玛丽，他跳了起来，随即作势像要走开。可罗斯玛丽一时冲动，立刻喊他：
“别走，彼得。”
她嗓音的魔力仿佛把他钉在了原地。他一动不动站着，却背对着她；随后一只手摘下军帽，另一只手在额头上抹了一两下。
罗斯玛丽心里说不清地乱、说不清地困惑。彼得怎么在这儿？他怎么来的？还穿着这身制服？
“你不打算同我说话吗，彼得？”她问——彼得在这儿这件事太不可思议，一时间她竟疑心自己见了幻影；“连看都不看我一眼？”她补一句。
“我想不出您有什么特别想同我说话的意思，”他说，没有转身面对她。
“为什么这样说？”她单纯地问。

===Original===

“Because I imagine that you look upon me as such an unmitigated blackguard that the very sight of me must be hateful to you.”
She said nothing for a moment or two. Perhaps she was still wondering if he was real, and if so, how he came to be here﻿—just today and at this hour. Then she went deliberately up to him, put her hand on his arm, and forced him to look at her.
“It is true, then?” she asked, and her eyes, those pixie eyes of hers, luminous and searching, were fastened on his as if seeking to penetrate to the very soul within him. But a look of dull and dogged obstinacy was all that she got in response.
“It is all true, Peter?” she insisted, trying with all her might to steady her voice, so that he should not hear the catch in her throat.
He shrugged his shoulders, indifferent and still obstinate.
“I don’t know what you mean,” he retorted, almost roughly.
“I mean,” she said slowly, “that these last few days have not just been a hideous nightmare, as I still hoped until﻿—until two minutes ago. That things have really happened﻿—that you﻿—that you﻿—”
She paused, physically unable to continue. It was all too vile, too hideous to put into words. Peter gave a harsh laugh.
“Oh, don’t spare me,” he said, with a flippant laugh. “You mean that you did not believe until two minutes ago that I was really a spy in the pay of the Romanian Government, and that you did not believe that I had intrigued to have Philip and Anna arrested, stolen your articles for the *Times*, and bought Kis-Imre over Aunt Elza’s head, and turned her and Maurus out of their home. Well, you believe it now, don’t you? So that’s that. And as I am on my way to meet a friend, you’ll excuse me, won’t you, if I run away? Is there anything else I can do for you?”
“Yes,” she said. “You can look me straight in the eyes and tell me what has brought you down to﻿—to this. Is it money?”
Peter shrugged. “The want of it, I suppose,” he replied.
“I have no right to ask, I know. Only﻿—only﻿—we were friends once, Peter,” she went on, with a note of pleading in her tone. “You used to tell me all your plans﻿—your ambitions. You used to say that you did not want to﻿—to bind me to a promise until you had made a name for yourself. If you had told me that you were short of money, and that you were actually thinking of taking up this﻿—this sort of work, I could have helped you. I know I could have helped you.

===Chinese===

“因为我想，您把我看成了一个十足的恶棍，见我一眼都嫌恶心。”
她有一两分钟没说话。也许她还在疑心他是不是真的；若是真的，又怎么偏偏在这儿——偏偏今天、这个时辰。随后她从容地走近他，把手搭在他手臂上，迫使他看她。
“那么，是真的了？”她问；她那双精灵的眼睛，明亮而探求，牢牢盯住他的眼睛，仿佛要一直望进他的灵魂。可回应她的，只有一种死死执拗的神情。
“全是真的吗，彼得？”她坚持问，竭尽全力稳住嗓音，别让他听出她喉咙里的哽咽。
他耸耸肩，淡漠而依旧执拗。
“我不知道您指什么，”他回敬，几乎粗暴。
“我是说，”她慢慢地说，“这几天不只是场可怕的噩梦——直到两分钟以前我还这样指望。事情真的发生了——你——你——”
她顿住了，身子续不下去。这一切太卑劣、太丑恶，说不出口。彼得发出一声刺耳的笑。
“哦，别饶着我，”他轻浮地说，“您是想说：两分钟前您还不信我真是罗马尼亚政府拿钱豢养的密探；不信是我设计让菲利普和安娜被捕、偷了您给《泰晤士报》的文章、越过埃尔莎姨的头买下小伊姆雷、把她和毛罗斯赶出家门。嗯，现在您信了，是不是？就这样吧。我正要去会一个朋友，您原谅我拔腿就跑吧？还有什么旁的事我能为您效劳？”
“有，”她说，“你可以直视我的眼睛，告诉我，是什么把你弄到——弄到这步田地。是为钱吗？”
彼得耸耸肩。“大概是缺钱，”他答。
“我知道我没有权利问。只是——只是——我们从前是朋友，彼得，”她接着说，话音里带出了恳求。“你从前什么都告诉我——你的计划，你的抱负。你从前说，你不想——不想在我身上绑一个承诺，等你闯出了名堂再说。你要是告诉我你缺钱，告诉我你当真在考虑干这种——这种事，我本可以帮你的。我知道我本可以帮你。

===Original===

I know I should have found the right words to dissuade you. Oh, Peter!” she went on almost wildly, unable to hold her tears longer in check, or to control the tremor in her voice, “it is all so horrible! Can’t you see? Can’t you see? We were such friends! You used to tell me everything. You were taking up your father’s work. Some of your scientific experiments were already attracting attention. And you were a sportsman, too! And your V.C. And now this﻿—this! Oh, it is too horrible﻿—too horrible!”
Her words were carrying her away. The murmur of the water grew louder and louder in her ears, and in the trees the soughing of the wind among the leaves grew almost deafening. She felt herself swaying, and for a few seconds she closed her eyes. But when she put out her hand she felt it resting on Peter’s arm. There was the feel of the rough cloth of his tunic. So she opened her eyes and raised them slowly until they met his. Her glance had wandered on the ugly uniform, the livery of his unspeakable shame. Her eyes expressed the contempt which she felt, the loathing which was almost physical. But Peter’s glance now was not only dogged, but defiant. In it she read the determination to follow in the path of life which he had chosen for himself, and a challenge to her power to drag him away from it. This was no longer the Peter of Kis-Imre, the irresponsible young English athlete, whose thoughts would never soar above the interest in a cricket-match. It was more the Peter of olden times﻿—the tempestuous lover, the wayward creature of caprice, the temperamental enthusiast capable of heroic deeds, and always chafing under the restraint imposed by twentieth-century conventions; the Peter whose soul had once been equally great in virtue as it was now steeped in crime; the gallant soldier, the worthy descendant of the Scarlet Pimpernel. It was the Peter of olden times, but his love for her was dead. Dead. If one spark of it had remained alive, if something of her image had remained in his heart, he could never have given himself over to this vile, vile thing. But while she had been battling bravely to banish from her mind all memories of their early love, he had torn her out of his heart, and turned to this ignominious calling to help him to forget.
Rosemary felt giddy and ill; even the sweet woodland air seemed to have turned to poisonous fumes of intrigue and venality.

===Chinese===

我知道我准能找到合适的话劝住你。哦，彼得！”她几乎狂乱地说下去，再也压不住眼泪，也压不住嗓音的颤抖，“这一切太可怕了！你难道看不见？看不见吗？我们曾是那样的朋友！你什么都告诉我。你当时正接手你父亲的事业。你几项科学实验已经引人注目了。你还是个运动家！还有你的维多利亚十字勋章。可如今这个——这个！哦，太可怕了——太可怕了！”
她的话把她自己卷走了。水声在她耳中越来越响，树叶间风的萧萧几乎震耳。她觉得自己在摇晃，闭了几秒钟眼。可她一伸手，那只手正搭在彼得臂上。粗呢军服布料的感觉就在那里。于是她睁开眼，缓缓抬眼，直到同他的目光相遇。她的目光曾在那身丑陋的制服上游移——那件他难以言说的耻辱的号衣。她的眼里流露着她所感到的鄙夷，那近乎生理的憎恶。可此刻彼得的目光不光执拗，更是挑衅。她在里面读到了沿他自己选定的生活之路走下去的决心，读到了对她能否把他拉离那条路的挑战。这已不是小伊姆雷的那个彼得——那个心思从不会高过一场板球赛的兴趣、不负责任的英国青年运动员了。这更像是旧日的彼得——暴风骤雨的恋人，任性的、凭兴之所至活着的生灵，动不动就热血沸腾、干得出英雄事迹的人，永远为二十世纪习俗的束缚而烦躁的人；那个灵魂曾经同美德一样宏大、如今却深陷罪恶的彼得；勇敢的军人，红花侠当之无愧的后裔。是旧日的彼得——只是他对她的爱死了。死了。那爱若剩下一星火，她若还留在他心里一点影子，他绝不会把自己交给这卑劣、卑劣的勾当。可当她一路英勇地想把早年爱情的记忆从心里赶出去的时候，他却把她从心里拔走了，转向这丢人的行当，好帮着他忘记。
罗斯玛丽只觉头晕、难受；连这香甜的林间空气，仿佛也变成了密谋与贪贿的毒雾。

===Original===

She roughly pushed away from Peter’s arm that supported her, but she was still swaying; her hat fell from her head, and her glorious hair lay in a tumbled mass of ruddy gold around her face.
“Better sit down on this old stump,” Peter remarked drily. “You’ll have to lean on me till you get to it.”
But Rosemary did not really know what happened just then﻿—she had such a gnawing pain in her heart. She certainly tottered forward a step or two until she reached the tree-stump, and she sank down on it, helped thereto, no doubt, by Peter’s arm. The next thing of which she was conscious was a flood of tears that would not be checked. It welled up to her eyes, and eased that heavy pain in her heart. Great sobs shook her bowed shoulders, and she buried her face in her hands, for she was ashamed of her tears. Ashamed that she cared so much.
And the next thing that struck her consciousness was that Peter sank down on his knees before her, that he raised her skirt to his lips, and that he murmured: “Goodbye, sweetheart. My Rosemary for remembrance. God bless and keep you. Try to forget.” Then he jumped to his feet and was gone. Gone! She called him back with a cry of despair: “Peter!” But he was nowhere to be seen. He must have scrambled up the incline that led to the road. She certainly heard high above her the crackling of dry twigs, but nothing more. Peter had passed out of her life, more completely, more effectually, indeed, than on the day when she became Jasper Tarkington’s wife. Peter﻿—her Peter, the friend of her girlhood, the master from whom she had learned her first lesson of love, was dead. The thing that remained was a vague speck, a creation of this venal postwar world. It was as well that he should go out of her life.

===Chinese===

她粗鲁地挣开彼得撑着她的手臂，人还在摇晃；帽子从头上跌落，那一头华美的红金头发乱乱地堆在脸旁。
“还是坐到这个老树桩上吧，”彼得干巴巴地说，“到那儿之前您得靠着我。”
罗斯玛丽其实不知道随后发生了什么——她心口那样剜痛。她确实踉跄着朝前挪了一两步，挨到树桩，跌坐下去，多半是彼得的手臂送了她一把。她再一有知觉，是一股再也收不住的泪水。泪水涌上眼眶，缓解了心口那沉重的痛。大口的抽噎摇着她低垂的肩，她把脸埋进双手——她为自己的眼泪羞愧。羞愧自己还这样在乎。
再一桩撞进她知觉的事，是彼得跪倒在她面前，把她裙裾举到唇边，喃喃道：“别了，心肝。我的迷迭香，纪念的迷迭香。上帝保佑你、看顾你。试着忘了吧。”随后他跳起来，走了。走了！她绝望地喊他回来：“彼得！”可他已无影无踪。他必定是从通向公路的坡爬上去了。她确实听见高处传来枯枝的碎响，可再没有别的。彼得从她的生命里退出了——比她成为贾斯珀·塔金顿妻子的那一天退出得更彻底、更干净。彼得——她的彼得，她少女时代的朋友，教她上了爱情第一课的师父——死了。剩下的那个东西只是一个模糊的斑点，一个这战后贪贿世界的造物。他退出她的生命，也好。

## XLII / 四十二
===Original===

A minute or two later Rosemary was startled out of her daydream by the sound of Jasper’s voice calling to her from somewhere in the near distance. She had barely time to obliterate the traces of tears from her eyes and cheeks before he appeared round the bend of the path. The next moment he was by her side. Apparently he had been running, for he seemed breathless and not quite so trim and neat in his appearance as he usually was.
“I heard a scream,” were the first words he said, as soon as he came in sight of her. “It terrified me when I recognised your voice. Thank God you are safe!”
He was obviously exhausted and, for him, strangely agitated. He threw himself down on the carpet of moss at her feet; then he seized her hand and covered it with kisses. “Thank God!” he kept on murmuring. “Thank God you are safe!”
Then suddenly he looked up at her with an inquiring frown. “But what made you scream?” he asked.
Rosemary by now had regained control over her nerves. She succeeded in disengaging her hand, and in smiling quite coolly down upon him.
“It was very stupid of me,” she said, with a light laugh. “I saw a pair of eyes looking at me through the undergrowth. It startled me. I thought that it was a wild cat﻿—I had heard that there were some in these parts﻿—but it was only a homely one.”
She tried to rise, but Jasper had recaptured her hand. He was engaged in kissing her fingertips one by one, lingering over each kiss as if to savour its sweetness in full. Now he looked up at her with a glance of hungering passion. Rosemary felt herself flushing. She was conscious of an intense feeling of pity for this man who had lavished on her all the love of which he was capable, and hungered for that which she was not able to give. He looked careworn, she thought, and weary.
“You were not anxious about me, Jasper, were you?” she asked kindly.
He smiled. “I am always anxious,” he said, “when I don’t see you.”
“But how did you find me?”
“Quite easily; I went to the hotel, you know. Not at all a bad little place, by the way; rather primitive, but with electric light and plenty of hot water. I engaged the rooms, and had a mouthful of breakfast. Then I sallied forth in quest of you. A man in the village told me you had been asking the way to the château, and I knew you would never stand the dusty road.

===Chinese===

一两分钟后，贾斯珀的声音从近处唤她，把罗斯玛丽从白日梦里惊出来。她刚来得及抹去眼睛和脸颊上的泪痕，他就从小路的拐弯处出现了。转眼他已到她身边。他显然一路跑来，气息不平，仪容也不像平日那样齐整。
“我听见一声尖叫，”他一望见她就说。“听出是你的声音，把我吓坏了。谢天谢地，你平安！”
他显然精疲力竭，而且以他而论，激动得反常。他扑倒在她脚边的苔藓地上；随后攥住她的手，吻个不停。“谢天谢地！”他反复喃喃。“谢天谢地，你平安！”
忽然他抬头，皱着探问的眉。“可你为什么尖叫？”他问。
罗斯玛丽此刻已重新管住了神经。她把手抽出来，相当冷静地朝他微笑。
“我太没出息了，”她轻笑着。“我看见一双眼睛在灌木丛里望着我。吓了我一跳。我还当是野猫——听说这一带有——结果不过是只家猫。”
她想站起来，可贾斯珀又捉住了她的手。他正一根一根地吻她的指尖，每一个吻都流连着，仿佛要把它的甜味尝个够。这时他抬眼望她，目光里是饥渴的激情。罗斯玛丽觉得自己脸红了。她心里涌起一股强烈的怜悯，怜悯这个男人——他把他的爱能给的都给了她，渴求的却是她给不出的东西。他显得憔悴，她想，还疲惫。
“你方才不是在为我担心吧，贾斯珀，是不是？”她和气地问。
他微笑。“看不见你的时候，我总在担心。”他说。
“可你怎么找到我的？”
“容易得很；你知道，我到旅馆去了。顺便说一句，那小地方还不坏；简陋了些，可有电灯，热水管够。我订了房间，匆匆用了几口早饭，就出来寻你。村里一个人告诉我，你问过去城堡的路，我就知道你断不肯吃那条土路的灰。”

===Original===

So when I found that there was a woodland path that went through the same way as the road, I naturally concluded that you would choose it in preference. You see,” Jasper concluded, with a smile, “that there was no magic in my quest.”
Then he looked up at her again, and there was a gleam of suspicion in his dark, questioning eyes. “You must have walked very slowly,” he said. “I started quite half an hour, probably more, after you did.”
“I did walk very slowly. This path is enchanting, and this is not the first time I have sat down to think and to gaze at this delicious little stream. But,” Rosemary went on briskly, “I think I had better be getting on.”
But Jasper put out his arms and encircled her knees. “Don’t go for a minute, little one. It is so peaceful here, and somehow I have had so little of you these last days. I don’t know, but it seems as if we had taken to misunderstanding one another lately.” Then, as she made an involuntary movement of impatience, he continued gently: “Do I annoy you by making love to you?”
Rosemary tried to smile. “Of course not, dear. What a question!”
“Then tell me if there is anything in the world I can do to make you happier. You have not looked happy lately. I have been tortured with remorse, for I feel somehow that it has been my fault.”
“You are sweet and kind, Jasper, as always. But you must be a little patient. I have gone through a great deal these last few days.”
“I know, I know, little one. Don’t let us talk any more about it.”
He was wonderfully kind﻿—kinder and gentler than he had been since the first days of their married life. It almost seemed as if he had set himself the task of making her forget all that he had involuntarily revealed to her of his violent, unbridled temperament, and of that lawless passion that lay at the root of his love for her.
He talked of the future, of their return to England, the home that he would make for her, which would be a fitting casket for the priceless jewel which he possessed. Rosemary, who felt inexpressibly lonely, was once more conscious of that feeling of gratitude towards him which she had once hoped might be transmuted, in days to come, into something more ardent than friendship.

===Chinese===

“所以当我发现有一条林间小径同大路走的是同一个方向，我自然断定你宁肯选它。你瞧，”贾斯珀含笑作结，“我这一寻，毫无魔法可言。”
随后他又抬眼望她，那双黝黑质问的眼睛里闪着一丝疑影。“你一定走得很慢，”他说。“我比你晚了足有半个钟头，多半还不止。”
“我确实走得很慢。这条小径迷人极了，而且这不是我头一回坐下来想心事、望这条可喜的小溪。不过，”罗斯玛丽轻快地接着说，“我想我还是该走了。”
可贾斯珀伸出手臂，环住了她的双膝。“再待一分钟，小家伙。这里这样安静，而这些天我偏偏同你待得这样少。我也说不清，好像近来我们动不动就彼此误会似的。”见她不由自主地露出不耐烦的动静，他温柔地接着说：“我向你求爱，惹你厌了么？”
罗斯玛丽想笑。“当然不，亲爱的。这算什么问题！”
“那么告诉我，世上可有什么我能做的，叫你快活些。你近来一直不快活。悔恨把我折磨苦了，因为不知怎么，我觉得那是我的过错。”
“你又甜又厚道，贾斯珀，一如既往。可你得稍稍耐心。这几天我经历了太多。”
“我知道，我知道，小家伙。咱们不再提了。”
他好得不得了——比他们婚后头几天以来更和气、更温柔。他几乎像是给自己立下了任务，要她忘掉他不由自主显露给她的一切：他那暴烈不羁的性子，和藏在他对她的爱根底里的那种无法无天的激情。
他谈起将来，谈他们回英国，他要为她布置的那个家——要配得上他拥有的这颗无价宝石的一只匣子。罗斯玛丽——心里说不出的孤单——又一次感到了对他的感激；这份感激，她曾盼望有朝一日能化成比友谊更炽热的东西。

===Original===

She had suffered so terribly in her love for the one man who, with all his faults, had come very near to her ideals, that she felt a desperate longing to cherish and to cling to the husband whom she had chosen half out of pique, the man on whom she had inflicted so much cruelty by becoming his wife.

===Chinese===

她爱过的那个男人——尽管有种种毛病，却极近于她的理想——叫她吃了那样大的苦，以致她如今怀着一种拼却一切的渴望，想珍爱、想偎依她这个当初一半赌气选下的丈夫，这个因她嫁给他而被她施加了那么多残忍的男人。

## XLIII / 四十三
===Original===

Rosemary was the first to remember that time was slipping by. She looked at her watch. It was past ten o’clock﻿—over an hour since Peter had asked her to try to forget. She rose briskly to her feet, and arm in arm, like two good comrades, she and Jasper made their way together towards the château. When they came in sight of the great gates﻿—a couple of hundred yards still ahead of them﻿—Rosemary was the first to spy a motorcar standing there, and some half-dozen persons in the act of getting into the car. There were two sentries at the gates, and seemingly a few people on the other side.
“It looks like a man and a woman and three soldiers in uniform getting into that car,” Rosemary remarked casually. And immediately, for no apparent reason, Jasper started to walk along more rapidly; a few seconds later he almost broke into a run. At that moment the car started off, and was soon lost to sight in a cloud of dust. Rosemary thought that she heard Jasper utter a savage oath.
“Is anything wrong?” she asked. But he did not answer, only hurried along so quickly that she was not able to keep up with him. He had passed through the gates when she reached them, and when she tried to follow she was stopped by the sentry. She called to Jasper, who apparently did not hear; pointing to him, she explained to the man on duty that she was that gentleman’s wife, and if he was allowed to go in, why not she? They were as mute as if she had spoken in an unknown tongue, but they would not allow her to pass. In the meanwhile Jasper had disappeared inside the château. Rosemary had seen him go in by the main entrance, challenged by the sentry on guard at the door, but after a second or two allowed to pass freely in.
Fortunately she was provided with money, and her experience of this part of the world was that most things could be accomplished with the aid of baksheesh. A young officer was crossing the courtyard; he looked in the direction of the gates, saw an excessively pretty woman standing there, and, true to his race and upbringing, came at once to see if he could enter into conversation with her. Very politely he explained to her that no one was allowed to enter the château, or to visit any of the prisoners, without a special permit from the commanding officer.
Rosemary told him that she desired to speak with the commanding officer. This also, it seems, was impossible.

===Chinese===

罗斯玛丽第一个想起时间在溜走。她看了看表。十点多了——彼得求她试着忘掉，已经过去一个多钟头。她利落地站起身，两人挽着胳膊，像一对好同志，一道朝城堡走去。望见大门时——前面还有两百来码——罗斯玛丽第一个瞥见门前停着一辆汽车，五六个人正在上车。门口有两名哨兵，门那一边好像还有几个人。
“看样子是一个男人、一个女人，还有三个穿军服的士兵在上那辆车，”罗斯玛丽随口说。立刻，不知为什么，贾斯珀加快了脚步；几秒钟后他几乎跑了起来。就在这时汽车开动了，转眼消失在一溜尘土里。罗斯玛丽好像听见贾斯珀骂了一句粗野的咒骂。
“出什么事了吗？”她问。可他不答，只顾急急往前赶，她都跟不上。她走到大门时，他已进门；她想跟进去，被哨兵拦住了。她喊贾斯珀，他显然没听见；她指着他，向值勤的人解释，她是那位先生的妻子，既然放他进，为什么不放她进？他们一言不发，仿佛她说的是天书，就是不放她过去。这当儿贾斯珀已在城堡里不见了。罗斯玛丽看见他从正门进去，门口守卫的哨兵盘问了他，可一两秒后便放他自由进去了。
好在她身上带着钱，而她在这边地方的经验是：靠贿钱没有办不成的事。一个年轻军官正穿过院子；他朝大门这边望，看见一位过分漂亮的太太站在那里，便本着他的种族和教养的本性，立刻走过来，看能不能同她搭上话。他彬彬有礼地向她解释：没有指挥官的特别许可，任何人不得进入城堡，也不得探望任何囚犯。
罗斯玛丽对他说，她想同指挥官说话。这呢，看来也不可能。

===Original===

But a hint from Rosemary as to reward if the matter could be managed simplified matters a great deal.
The young officer conducted her across the courtyard and into the château.
